Every answer appears below its question. Attempt first, then check. Use “Hide all answers” for self-testing and the individual reveal buttons as needed. Search and filters work offline; no external scripts, accounts or network requests are required.

★★★ core first; ★★ important breadth next; ★ extension later. These priorities are educational judgments. Difficulty E/M/H is a separate label. A one-star topic is not excluded from the real examination.

This bank covers all ten broad Group 1 areas, with an expanded undergraduate subtopic selection. It cannot guarantee every conceivable question.

Practice format [R1]: MCQ 50 questions / 50 marks / 50 minutes, minus 0.5 per wrong answer. Written 10 questions / 100 marks / 60 minutes, evaluated after the committee’s unspecified MCQ cutoff. Confirm final instructions with the current notice.

Ten mixed sets: for set k=1…10 take MCQs 5k−4 through 5k from every chapter, and Wk from every chapter. These are selections of existing questions, not additional unseen papers. Use a zero mark for blanks only as an explicit practice convention unless confirmed in official instructions.

01 · Structured and Object-Oriented Programming

Use small traces to rebuild independent programming reasoning. Unless stated otherwise, C fragments use C11, required headers are available, integer results fit, and memory allocation succeeds only when explicitly assumed. C++ examples use C++17 and Java examples state the relevant context. Do not invent a numeric output for undefined behaviour.

Coverage: Expressions and control flow; functions and recursion; arrays, strings and pointers; storage and dynamic memory; structures and files; classes, inheritance, dispatch, exceptions and generics.

Reading references: R3, R4, R5

01-M01 · Expressions and types ★★★ E MCQ

In C, what is the value of 7 / 2 when both operands have type int?

  1. 4
  2. 3.5
  3. A compile-time error
  4. 3

Answer: D — 3

Integer division discards the fractional part, truncating toward zero. Both operands are integers, so no floating-point division occurs. Assigning this result to a double afterward would produce 3.0, not 3.5.

01-M02 · Expressions and types ★★★ E MCQ

In C, what is the value of (double)7 / 2?

  1. 4
  2. 3
  3. 3.5
  4. 2.5

Answer: C — 3.5

The cast converts 7 before the division. The other operand is then converted for floating-point arithmetic, so 7.0 / 2.0 is 3.5. The placement of a cast matters.

01-M03 · Expressions and types ★★★ E MCQ

In C, what is the value of 2 + 3 * 4 % 5?

  1. 4
  2. 14
  3. 0
  4. 2

Answer: A — 4

Multiplication and remainder have higher precedence than addition and associate left to right: 3 * 4 = 12, 12 % 5 = 2, then 2 + 2 = 4.

01-M04 · Short-circuit evaluation ★★★ E MCQ

What value does x have after this C fragment?

C
int x = 0;
if (0 && ++x) { x += 10; }
  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. 11
  4. 10

Answer: A — 0

The left operand of && is false, so the right operand is not evaluated. The increment never happens, and the body is not entered.

01-M05 · Loops and tracing ★★★ E MCQ

What is the final value of s?

C
int s = 0;
for (int i = 1; i <= 5; ++i) {
    if (i == 3) continue;
    s += i;
}
  1. 9
  2. 15
  3. 12
  4. 6

Answer: C — 12

The continue skips only the addition for i = 3. The loop still advances, adding 1 + 2 + 4 + 5 = 12. It does not terminate the loop.

01-M06 · Loops and tracing ★★★ E MCQ

How many times is the inner statement executed?

C
for (int i = 1; i <= 4; ++i)
    for (int j = 1; j <= i; ++j)
        count++;
  1. 4
  2. 8
  3. 10
  4. 16

Answer: C — 10

The inner-loop counts are 1, 2, 3 and 4. Their sum is 10. For a general upper bound n, the count is n(n + 1)/2.

01-M07 · Loops and tracing ★★★ M MCQ

What value is printed?

C
int x = 2;
switch (x) {
  case 1: x += 2; break;
  case 2: x += 3;
  default: x += 4;
}
printf("%d", x);
  1. 9
  2. 6
  3. 5
  4. 7

Answer: A — 9

Control enters case 2 and adds 3. Because there is no break after that case, execution falls through to default and adds 4. The result is 2 + 3 + 4 = 9.

01-M08 · Functions and parameter passing ★★★ E MCQ

What is printed after the function call?

C
void change(int x) { x = 20; }
/* inside main */
int a = 5;
change(a);
printf("%d", a);
  1. 5
  2. 0
  3. 20
  4. An unspecified value

Answer: A — 5

C passes the value of a into the separate local parameter x. Assigning to x does not modify a. A pointer to a would be needed to modify a through a function.

01-M09 · Pointers and aliasing ★★★ E MCQ

What is printed?

C
void change(int *p) { *p += 3; }
/* inside main */
int a = 5;
change(&a);
printf("%d", a);
  1. 8
  2. 5
  3. 3
  4. An address

Answer: A — 8

The pointer parameter receives a copy of the address of a. Dereferencing that address accesses a itself, so adding 3 changes a from 5 to 8.

01-M10 · Recursion ★★★ M MCQ

What does f(5) return?

C
int f(int n) {
    if (n <= 1) return 1;
    return n * f(n - 2);
}
  1. 5
  2. 120
  3. 10
  4. 15

Answer: D — 15

Expand the calls: f(5) = 5f(3), f(3) = 3f(1), and f(1) = 1. Hence 5 * 3 * 1 = 15. The decrement is two, not one.

01-M11 · Recursion ★★★ M MCQ

For this function, what is the recursion depth of f(16), counting the initial call and the base-case call?

C
int f(int n) {
    if (n == 1) return 1;
    return 1 + f(n / 2);
}
  1. 4
  2. 8
  3. 16
  4. 5

Answer: D — 5

The calls have arguments 16, 8, 4, 2 and 1. The definition of depth in the question counts all five active calls at the deepest point.

01-M12 · Arrays and pointers ★★★ E MCQ

What is the value of p[1]?

C
int a[] = {4, 7, 9, 12};
int *p = a + 1;
  1. 12
  2. 9
  3. 4
  4. 7

Answer: B — 9

p points at a[1]. Indexing is relative to that pointer: p[0] is a[1], and p[1] is a[2] = 9. Array subscripting is defined through pointer addition and dereference.

01-M13 · Arrays and pointers ★★★ M MCQ

What is printed?

C
int a[6] = {0};
int *p = &a[1], *q = &a[5];
printf("%td", q - p);
  1. An undefined result
  2. 4
  3. 5
  4. 4 * sizeof(int)

Answer: B — 4

Subtracting pointers within the same array gives the difference in element positions, not bytes. Positions 5 and 1 differ by four elements.

01-M14 · Arrays and pointers ★★★ E MCQ

A C array has 4 elements. Which statement is correct about the pointer a + 4?

  1. It may be formed as one-past-the-end, but must not be dereferenced.
  2. It may always be dereferenced to read zero.
  3. Merely forming it is always undefined.
  4. It is the address of the last element.

Answer: A — It may be formed as one-past-the-end, but must not be dereferenced.

C permits a pointer one position past an array for comparisons and iteration. That position is not an element. Reading or writing *(a + 4) is outside the array.

01-M15 · Array dimensions ★★★ M MCQ

For int a[3][4], which expression designates the element in row 2, column 1 using zero-based indices?

  1. *(*(a + 2) + 1)
  2. *(*(a + 1) + 2)
  3. *(a + 2 + 1)
  4. **a + 3

Answer: A — *(*(a + 2) + 1)

Adding 2 to a selects row 2. Dereferencing gives that row, then adding 1 selects its column 1. The final dereference accesses the element.

01-M16 · Strings and sizes ★★★ E MCQ

What are sizeof(s) and strlen(s), respectively?

C
char s[] = "BUET";
  1. 4 and 5
  2. 4 and 4
  3. 5 and 5
  4. 5 and 4

Answer: D — 5 and 4

The array contains B, U, E, T and the terminating null byte, so its size is five bytes. strlen counts characters before the terminator and returns four.

01-M17 · Strings and mutability ★★★ M MCQ

Which statement about this C fragment is correct?

C
char *p = "abc";
p[0] = 'A';
  1. It portably changes the literal to Abc.
  2. It always produces a compile-time error in C11.
  3. It allocates a new writable string automatically.
  4. The attempted modification has undefined behaviour.

Answer: D — The attempted modification has undefined behaviour.

A string literal must not be modified. Assigning its address to char * does not make the literal writable. A writable copy could be created with char p[] = "abc".

01-M18 · Arrays and function parameters ★★ M MCQ

Inside void f(int a[10]), what is a actually treated as?

  1. A pointer parameter of type int *
  2. A pointer to an array of ten ints
  3. A reference parameter in C
  4. A ten-element array passed by value

Answer: A — A pointer parameter of type int *

In a C function parameter declaration, this array notation is adjusted to a pointer. The function does not receive an array-length guarantee; sizeof(a) inside f measures the pointer type.

01-M19 · Storage duration and scope ★★★ M MCQ

What two values are printed by two successive calls to f()?

C
void f(void) {
    static int x = 0;
    printf("%d ", ++x);
}
  1. 1 2
  2. 1 1
  3. 0 0
  4. 0 1

Answer: A — 1 2

A local static object retains its value between calls and is initialized only once. The increment occurs before each print. Its scope is local, but its storage duration spans the execution.

01-M20 · Storage duration and scope ★★ E MCQ

An uninitialized file-scope int g in a C program is initially:

  1. 0
  2. An arbitrary stack value
  3. A compile-time error
  4. 1

Answer: A — 0

Objects with static storage duration, including file-scope variables, are zero-initialized when no explicit initializer is provided. This differs from an uninitialized automatic local int.

01-M21 · Undefined behaviour ★★★ M MCQ

Which classification is correct?

C11
int i = 1;
int x = i++ + i++;
  1. x is guaranteed to be 2
  2. x is guaranteed to be 3
  3. Undefined behaviour
  4. x is guaranteed to be 4

Answer: C — Undefined behaviour

The two modifications of i are unsequenced relative to one another. C11 does not assign this expression a portable numeric result. Parentheses alone would not fix the sequencing problem.

01-M22 · Const correctness ★★ M MCQ

Given const int *p, which operation is forbidden through p?

  1. p++ within the same array
  2. Reading *p
  3. *p = 7
  4. p = another_valid_int_address

Answer: C — *p = 7

The pointed-to int is const-qualified through this access path; assigning to *p is prohibited. The pointer variable itself is not const. Contrast int * const p, where the pointer cannot be reassigned.

01-M23 · Dynamic memory ★★★ M MCQ

Which allocation expression correctly allocates space for n elements for an int *p, assuming n is valid and multiplication does not overflow?

  1. malloc(n + sizeof(int))
  2. malloc(n)
  3. malloc(sizeof p)
  4. malloc(n * sizeof *p)

Answer: D — malloc(n * sizeof *p)

sizeof *p is the size of one pointed-to element, so n times that size supplies the required byte count. sizeof p instead measures the pointer. A real program must still check allocation failure.

01-M24 · Dynamic memory ★★★ M MCQ

After free(p) successfully releases a dynamically allocated block, which statement is correct?

  1. Calling free(p) again is always safe.
  2. p is automatically set to NULL.
  3. Accessing the former block through p is invalid.
  4. The contents remain safe until the next allocation.

Answer: C — Accessing the former block through p is invalid.

The lifetime of the allocated object has ended. A dangling pointer may still retain an address representation, but that does not permit use. Setting p to NULL afterward prevents accidental reuse through that variable.

01-M25 · Dynamic memory ★★ M MCQ

For a nonzero requested size, realloc(p, newsize) returns NULL. What happens to the original allocated block?

  1. It becomes zero-filled.
  2. It is resized to zero.
  3. It remains allocated and unchanged by this failed request.
  4. It is always freed.

Answer: C — It remains allocated and unchanged by this failed request.

On this allocation failure, the original block remains valid. Therefore assigning realloc directly to the only pointer risks losing the address; store the return value in a temporary pointer first.

01-M26 · Structures and unions ★★ M MCQ

Why can sizeof(struct S) exceed the sum of the sizes of its declared members?

  1. Padding may be added to satisfy alignment requirements.
  2. C doubles each member size inside a structure.
  3. Every structure stores all member names at runtime.
  4. Members always contain hidden pointers.

Answer: A — Padding may be added to satisfy alignment requirements.

Implementations may insert internal and trailing padding to meet alignment constraints. Without an ABI or exact layout assumptions, do not infer a universal structure size from member types alone.

01-M27 · Structures and unions ★★ M MCQ

In C, which statement about a union is generally correct?

  1. A union can only contain integer members.
  2. Its members share storage, unlike distinct ordinary structure members.
  3. All union members always have independent storage.
  4. The size equals the sum of all member sizes.

Answer: B — Its members share storage, unlike distinct ordinary structure members.

A union overlays member storage and must be large enough for its largest member, with possible alignment padding. This does not mean arbitrary reads of a different member are portable in every case.

01-M28 · Bit operations ★★★ E MCQ

For unsigned integers, what is the decimal value of 0x2D & 0x0F?

  1. 13
  2. 45
  3. 32
  4. 15

Answer: A — 13

0x2D is binary 0010 1101 and 0x0F is 0000 1111. AND retains the lower nibble 1101, which is decimal 13.

01-M29 · Bit operations ★★ M MCQ

For a nonzero unsigned integer x, x & (x - 1) does what?

  1. Clears the most significant set bit
  2. Clears the least significant set bit
  3. Always divides x by two
  4. Sets all bits to one

Answer: B — Clears the least significant set bit

Subtracting one flips the lowest set bit to zero and the trailing zeros to ones. AND with the original x removes that lowest set bit while preserving higher bits.

01-M30 · File handling ★★ M MCQ

Why should a value returned by getchar() be stored in int rather than char when testing for EOF?

  1. getchar reads an entire integer token.
  2. int can represent both unsigned-char values and the distinct EOF result.
  3. EOF is always the character 0.
  4. A char variable cannot hold letters.

Answer: B — int can represent both unsigned-char values and the distinct EOF result.

getchar returns either a character converted through unsigned char to int or EOF. Narrowing first may lose the distinction needed to detect end of input correctly.

01-M31 · Function pointers ★ M MCQ

Which C declaration makes fp a pointer to a function taking two ints and returning int?

  1. int *fp(int, int);
  2. int (*fp)(int, int);
  3. int (*fp)[int, int];
  4. int fp*[int, int];

Answer: B — int (*fp)(int, int);

Parentheses make *fp the declarator before the parameter list is applied. Without those parentheses, int *fp(int,int) declares a function returning an int pointer.

01-M32 · Macros ★★ M MCQ

What is the value of y after macro expansion?

C
#define SQ(x) x*x
int y = SQ(1+2);
  1. 6
  2. 4
  3. 9
  4. 5

Answer: D — 5

The text expands to 1+2*1+2. Multiplication is evaluated first, giving 1+2+2 = 5. A safer expression macro parenthesizes both the parameter uses and the whole expression, though repeated side effects remain a risk.

01-M33 · C++ references ★★★ E MCQ

What is x after these C++ statements?

C++17
int x = 5;
int &r = x;
r = 8;
  1. 8
  2. 5
  3. A compilation error
  4. 0

Answer: A — 8

r is a reference alias for x. Assigning through r changes x; it does not rebind r to a different object.

01-M34 · Encapsulation ★★★ E MCQ

Which design most directly supports encapsulation for an Account class?

  1. Private balance with operations that enforce invariants
  2. Public writable balance with no validation
  3. Duplicating balance in every caller
  4. Removing all member functions

Answer: A — Private balance with operations that enforce invariants

Encapsulation controls access to representation and centralizes allowed state changes. Private storage alone is not enough if other interfaces still expose unrestricted mutation, but the specified validated operations support the goal.

01-M35 · Inheritance and polymorphism ★★★ M MCQ

In C++, a virtual method is called through a base-class pointer that points to a derived object. Which implementation is selected?

  1. A random implementation
  2. Always the base-class implementation
  3. The final overriding implementation for the dynamic object type
  4. The first method alphabetically

Answer: C — The final overriding implementation for the dynamic object type

Virtual dispatch depends on the dynamic type of the referred-to object. The static pointer type determines which interface is accessible, but does not suppress a valid override.

01-M36 · Overloading and overriding ★★★ M MCQ

Which statement correctly distinguishes overloading from overriding?

  1. Overriding means changing only a local variable name.
  2. Overloading always requires inheritance.
  3. Overloading changes parameter signatures; overriding supplies an inherited virtual operation implementation.
  4. They are identical in all languages.

Answer: C — Overloading changes parameter signatures; overriding supplies an inherited virtual operation implementation.

Overloading provides operations with the same name but different accepted signatures. Overriding is the inheritance-related replacement of an inherited operation, subject to language rules.

01-M37 · Constructors and destructors ★★ M MCQ

For a simple C++ derived object with one base class, which constructor body runs first?

  1. They run simultaneously
  2. The base-class constructor body
  3. Neither runs unless called explicitly from main
  4. The derived-class constructor body

Answer: B — The base-class constructor body

The base subobject is constructed before the derived constructor body executes. Destruction reverses the completed construction order, so the derived destructor body runs before the base destructor.

01-M38 · Constructors and destructors ★★ M MCQ

A base pointer owns a derived C++ object and may delete it polymorphically. Which base-class design is needed for ordinary safe polymorphic deletion?

  1. An overloaded arithmetic operator
  2. A private copy of every derived field
  3. A virtual destructor
  4. A static constructor

Answer: C — A virtual destructor

Deleting through a base pointer requires the appropriate virtual destructor for ordinary polymorphic use. Otherwise the operation can be undefined, rather than merely omitting a harmless print statement.

01-M39 · Abstract classes and interfaces ★★ M MCQ

A C++ class with a remaining pure virtual function is:

  1. A namespace
  2. Guaranteed to contain no data members
  3. Always final
  4. Abstract and cannot be directly instantiated

Answer: D — Abstract and cannot be directly instantiated

The unimplemented pure virtual operation makes the class abstract. It can still define constructors, data and implemented methods, and pointers or references can refer to its interface.

01-M40 · Object slicing ★★ M MCQ

A Derived object is copied into a Base object by value in C++. What is the usual object-slicing effect?

  1. All derived fields are silently added to Base.
  2. The destination becomes dynamically Derived.
  3. The copy always fails at runtime.
  4. Only the base subobject is represented in the destination object.

Answer: D — Only the base subobject is represented in the destination object.

A Base value has Base storage and type. Copying the base portion does not turn it into a Derived value. References or suitable pointers preserve polymorphic access to an existing derived object.

01-M41 · Copy semantics ★★ M MCQ

Two objects independently delete the same heap address after a shallow copy of an owning raw pointer. What is the principal defect?

  1. Automatic deep copying
  2. A guaranteed harmless memory leak only
  3. A deterministic sorting error
  4. Double deletion and invalid shared ownership

Answer: D — Double deletion and invalid shared ownership

A shallow pointer copy duplicates an address, not the allocated object or an ownership policy. If both copies independently free it, the second deletion is invalid. Explicit copy ownership or appropriate smart pointers avoid this pattern.

01-M42 · Composition ★★ E MCQ

A Car object owns an Engine object as a member. Which relationship best describes this?

  1. Lexical scoping
  2. Public inheritance: an is-a relationship
  3. Function overloading
  4. Composition: a has-a relationship

Answer: D — Composition: a has-a relationship

Composition models a whole owning a component. This differs from inheritance, where a derived object is substitutable through a base interface under appropriate behavioural rules.

01-M43 · Java arrays and exceptions ★★ M MCQ

What happens when this Java code executes?

Java
int[] a = new int[3];
a[3] = 7;
  1. It must fail compilation because 3 is a literal.
  2. a expands to length four.
  3. An ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException is thrown.
  4. It writes the fourth element safely.

Answer: C — An ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException is thrown.

The valid indices are 0, 1 and 2. Java performs runtime bounds checking; accessing index 3 throws an exception. The array does not resize automatically.

01-M44 · Java parameter passing ★★ M MCQ

A Java method receives an object reference parameter and changes a field of that object. Why can the caller observe the change?

  1. All Java fields are global.
  2. Java passes every parameter by reference.
  3. A copied reference still refers to the same object.
  4. The caller receives a deep copy automatically.

Answer: C — A copied reference still refers to the same object.

Java passes parameter values by value. For an object reference, the copied value points to the same object, allowing field changes to be observed. Reassigning only the local parameter does not reassign the caller variable.

01-M45 · Exceptions ★★ M MCQ

Which resource-management idea ensures a C++ object releases an owned resource when its lifetime ends?

  1. Always using a global pointer
  2. Replacing every error with return 0
  3. Ignoring all exceptions
  4. RAII through constructors/destructors and ownership types

Answer: D — RAII through constructors/destructors and ownership types

Resource acquisition is tied to object lifetime. Stack unwinding destroys completed automatic objects, enabling deterministic cleanup of owned resources. The exact ownership and destructor behaviour must be correctly implemented.

01-M46 · Templates and generics ★ M MCQ

What is the main purpose of a C++ function template such as template<class T> T max2(T a,T b)?

  1. Express a family of type-parameterized implementations
  2. Force every argument to be an int
  3. Disable compile-time type checking
  4. Run a preprocessor loop over files

Answer: A — Express a family of type-parameterized implementations

The compiler can instantiate the template for suitable types supporting the required operations. Templates provide compile-time generic programming rather than forcing one concrete type for every call.

01-M47 · Algorithmic programming patterns ★★★ M MCQ

What does this function return for a = {2, 5, 2, 7, 2}, n = 5, target = 2?

C
int find_last(const int *a, int n, int target) {
    int ans = -1;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; ++i)
        if (a[i] == target) ans = i;
    return ans;
}
  1. 0
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 2

Answer: C — 4

The answer is overwritten on each match. Matches occur at indices 0, 2 and 4, so the final stored index is 4. A first-match implementation would instead return immediately.

01-M48 · Loop invariants ★★★ M MCQ

During an array maximum scan, what is a suitable invariant after processing indices 0 through i?

  1. best is always a[0]
  2. best equals the sum of unprocessed elements
  3. All array values have been sorted
  4. best equals the maximum of a[0..i]

Answer: D — best equals the maximum of a[0..i]

The invariant describes the processed prefix. It holds after initializing best = a[0], is preserved by comparing the next element, and implies the desired result after the final element.

01-M49 · Memory safety ★★★ M MCQ

Which statement about returning a pointer to a non-static automatic local variable from C is correct?

  1. The local object is automatically moved to the heap.
  2. Returning it extends its lifetime.
  3. The local variable lives until program exit.
  4. The returned pointer must not be used to access that expired local object.

Answer: D — The returned pointer must not be used to access that expired local object.

The local variable lifetime ends when its function returns. Its address can remain in a pointer value, but the underlying object is no longer alive. Return a value or use an explicit longer-lived ownership design.

01-M50 · Input validation ★★ M MCQ

A program reads n and then allocates n * sizeof(int) bytes. Which validation remains important even before checking malloc?

  1. Ensure n is valid and the multiplication cannot overflow the size type.
  2. Require n to be a prime number
  3. Require sizeof(int) to be exactly 4 on every platform
  4. Disable EOF handling

Answer: A — Ensure n is valid and the multiplication cannot overflow the size type.

A negative input converted to an unsigned size or an overflowing multiplication can request an unintended allocation. Validate the logical range and the arithmetic before allocating, then check the allocation result.

01-W01 · Loops, arrays and invariants ★★★ M WRITTEN

Write a C function that returns the largest element of a nonempty int array. State its precondition, prove a loop invariant, and give time and auxiliary-space complexity.

Worked answer

Precondition: a points to at least n valid int elements and n > 0. Initialize from the first element, not zero, so all-negative arrays work.

C
int array_max(const int *a, int n) {
    int best = a[0];
    for (int i = 1; i < n; ++i) {
        if (a[i] > best) best = a[i];
    }
    return best;
}

Before iteration i, best is the maximum of a[0..i-1]. Initialization establishes this for i=1. Comparing a[i] extends the invariant to a[0..i]. At termination the prefix is the whole array. There are n-1 comparisons: time Theta(n), auxiliary space Theta(1). For {-8,-3,-12}, the result is -3.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

01-W02 · Pointers and array tracing ★★★ M WRITTEN

For the code below, trace p, x and the array after each statement. Draw the final pointer-to-array layout.

C
int a[] = {3, 6, 9, 12};
int *p = a + 1;
int x = *p++;
*p += x;

Worked answer

Postfix ++ binds more tightly than unary *. Thus *p++ reads the old pointed-to value and then advances p; it is not (*p)++.

Stagep points toxArray
After initializationa[1]not yet initialized3, 6, 9, 12
After x = *p++a[2]63, 6, 9, 12
After *p += xa[2]63, 6, 15, 12

Only a[2] changes. Pointer advancement is one int element, not one byte.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

01-W03 · Recursion and call tracing ★★★ M WRITTEN

Trace f(4), show the returned value and the maximum active call count, then state time and auxiliary-space complexity for positive n.

C
int f(int n) {
    if (n == 0) return 0;
    return n + f(n - 1);
}

Worked answer

The calls are f(4), f(3), f(2), f(1), f(0). Returns unwind as 0, 1, 3, 6, 10. There are five simultaneously active calls at the base case, including the initial and base calls.

CallReturned value
f(0)0
f(1)1
f(2)3
f(3)6
f(4)10

For positive n the function returns n(n+1)/2. The recurrence T(n)=T(n-1)+Theta(1) gives Theta(n) time. The call stack has n+1 frames, so auxiliary space is Theta(n), assuming no transformation eliminates these frames. An iterative accumulation uses Theta(1) auxiliary space.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

01-W04 · Functions and parameter passing ★★★ M WRITTEN

Write a correct swap function in C using pointers and explain why a swap function with two plain int parameters does not modify the caller variables. Include a concrete before/after trace.

Worked answer

C
void swap_int(int *a, int *b) {
    int tmp = *a;
    *a = *b;
    *b = tmp;
}
/* usage */
int x = 4, y = 9;
swap_int(&x, &y);

Require valid writable pointers. Initially x=4 and y=9. tmp receives 4; writing *a changes x to 9; writing *b changes y to 4. The pointer parameters themselves are passed by value, but their copied addresses still identify caller objects. A plain swap(int a,int b) changes only two local copies. Passing the same address for both parameters leaves the value unchanged and is valid for this implementation.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

01-W05 · Strings and bounds ★★★ M WRITTEN

Write a C function that tests whether a null-terminated string is a palindrome, without modifying it. Include empty-string handling and complexity.

Worked answer

C
#include <stdbool.h>
#include <stddef.h>
#include <string.h>
bool palindrome(const char *s) {
    size_t n = strlen(s);
    for (size_t i = 0; i < n / 2; ++i) {
        if (s[i] != s[n - 1 - i]) return false;
    }
    return true;
}

Require s to point to a valid null-terminated string. Compare mirrored positions until the midpoint. When n=0 the loop is not entered, so no unsigned underflow expression is evaluated and the result is true. "level" returns true; "lever" returns false. Including strlen, worst-case time is Theta(n), with Theta(1) auxiliary space. This tests exact bytes: it does not ignore spaces or letter case unless specified.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

01-W06 · Dynamic memory and ownership ★★★ H WRITTEN

Write a C allocation pattern for n int elements that checks invalid size, multiplication overflow and allocation failure. Explain ownership and cleanup.

Worked answer

C
#include <stdint.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
/* n has already been parsed as a nonnegative size_t. */
if (n == 0 || n > SIZE_MAX / sizeof(int)) {
    /* reject this request */
} else {
    int *p = malloc(n * sizeof *p);
    if (p == NULL) {
        /* report allocation failure */
    } else {
        for (size_t i = 0; i < n; ++i) p[i] = 0;
        /* use p[0..n-1] */
        free(p);
        p = NULL;
    }
}

This policy rejects zero length explicitly. If input begins as a signed integer, reject negatives before conversion. The division bound prevents multiplication overflow. The owning scope releases the allocation exactly once after its last use. Setting p to NULL does not repair any other aliases; they must not access the freed object. calloc is another zero-initialization option, but size and failure handling still matter.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

01-W07 · Structures and linked allocation ★★ M WRITTEN

Define a singly linked-list node in C and write a function that inserts at the front. Return success/failure and preserve the old list if allocation fails. Draw the list after inserting 5 before 8 → 11.

Worked answer

C
#include <stdbool.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
struct Node { int value; struct Node *next; };
bool push_front(struct Node **head, int value) {
    struct Node *node = malloc(sizeof *node);
    if (!node) return false;
    node->value = value;
    node->next = *head;
    *head = node;
    return true;
}

Require head to point to a valid head-pointer variable. Linking happens only after allocation succeeds, so failure leaves the original head untouched. Time is Theta(1), with one newly allocated node. The list owner must later free each node once.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

01-W08 · Object-oriented design ★★★ M WRITTEN

Design a BankAccount class interface that prevents a negative balance. Explain encapsulation and state two invariants. Draw a small UML-style class diagram. Use integer cents and state an overflow policy.

Worked answer

Invariant 1: balanceCents >= 0. Invariant 2: a successful withdrawal changes balance by exactly the requested nonnegative amount. Keep balance private; reject negative deposits/withdrawals and withdrawals exceeding the balance. Check addition overflow before a deposit. Constructor validation must establish the invariants.

C++17 method
bool withdraw(long long cents) {
    if (cents < 0 || cents > balanceCents) return false;
    balanceCents -= cents;
    return true;
}

Encapsulation means callers use validated operations rather than writing representation directly. A private field plus a setter that accepts arbitrary invalid values would still be a poor invariant-preserving interface.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

01-W09 · Polymorphism and dispatch ★★★ M WRITTEN

State the output and explain static versus dynamic type. Why is the virtual destructor appropriate?

C++17
struct Base {
    virtual int value() const { return 1; }
    virtual ~Base() = default;
};
struct Derived : Base {
    int value() const override { return 2; }
};
Derived d;
Base *p = &d;
std::cout << p->value();

Worked answer

The output is 2. The static type of p is Base*, which permits the Base interface. The dynamic type of the object it refers to is Derived; the virtual call selects Derived::value.

A virtual destructor enables appropriate derived-object destruction when a dynamically allocated derived object is deleted through an owning Base pointer. Do not delete p in this specific example: d has automatic storage and was not allocated with new. Without virtual dispatch for value, a nonvirtual call through Base* would use Base::value.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

01-W10 · OOP distinctions ★★ M WRITTEN

Distinguish overloading, overriding, inheritance and composition. Give one small, concrete example of each and explain when composition is preferable.

Worked answer

ConceptExampleMeaning
Overloadingprint(int), print(string)Same name, different parameter signatures
OverridingCircle::area overrides virtual Shape::areaDerived behaviour through a base interface
InheritanceCircle derives from ShapeModels a substitutable is-a relation
CompositionCar contains EngineModels has-a ownership/collaboration

Use inheritance when clients can meaningfully rely on the base contract for all derived instances. Prefer composition when the aim is to reuse a component or vary an implementation without claiming substitutability. A queue containing a dynamic array need not publicly inherit every array operation. Overloading is resolved under compile-time signature rules; virtual overriding participates in runtime dispatch.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

01-W11 · Undefined behaviour and repairs ★★★ H WRITTEN

Identify the defect in each C fragment and provide a safe replacement: (a) int x = i++ + i++; (b) returning &local for an automatic local int; (c) writing s[0] when char *s = "abc".

Worked answer

(a) The modifications of i are unsequenced. Establish the intended order explicitly:

C
int left = i++;
int right = i++;
int x = left + right;

(b) The local object expires when the function returns. Return its int value, or store through a caller-provided valid pointer. Allocating a new object is another design only with a clear ownership/cleanup contract.

C
int make_value(void) { int local = 7; return local; }

(c) The literal is not writable. Create a writable array copy:

C
char s[] = "abc";
s[0] = 'A';

A numeric output inferred from one compiler run does not make undefined behaviour portable. Diagnose the semantic defect before tracing results.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

01-W12 · Java reference values and exceptions ★★ M WRITTEN

Explain the value of b.v after change(b). Then explain what happens when the last line runs.

Java
class Box { int v; }
static void change(Box p) {
    p.v = 7;
    p = new Box();
    p.v = 99;
}
// in a method:
Box b = new Box();
change(b);
int[] a = new int[2];
a[2] = 5;

Worked answer

b.v is 7. Initially p and b refer to the same Box, so p.v=7 changes the object visible through b. Assigning a new Box to the local parameter changes only p; setting that new object to 99 does not modify b.

Java passes reference values by value, not variables by reference. The final array access throws ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException because valid indices are 0 and 1. The array does not grow. Use an index strictly below a.length or choose a resizable collection when growth is intended.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

01-W13 · Bitwise programming ★★ M WRITTEN

Write pseudocode to count the set bits in an unsigned word using x & (x-1). Trace x=44 and give complexity in terms of the number of set bits.

Worked answer

Pseudocode
count = 0
while x != 0:
    x = x AND (x - 1)
    count = count + 1
return count
Before xBinary (6 bits)After clearing lowest 1
4410110040
4010100032
321000000

There are three iterations, so 44 has three set bits. Subtracting one changes the lowest one to zero and trailing zeros to ones; AND removes exactly that lowest one. If k bits are set, time is Theta(k), bounded by the word width, and auxiliary space is Theta(1). Use unsigned arithmetic to avoid signed-overflow assumptions.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

01-W14 · File handling and robust input ★★ M WRITTEN

Write a C function that counts newline bytes from an already-open FILE*. Distinguish end-of-file from a read error and avoid narrowing EOF to char.

Worked answer

C
#include <stdio.h>
int count_newlines(FILE *f, size_t *out) {
    size_t count = 0;
    int ch;
    while ((ch = fgetc(f)) != EOF) {
        if (ch == '\n') ++count;
    }
    if (ferror(f)) return 0;
    *out = count;
    return 1;
}

Require valid f and out and a stream small enough that the counter does not overflow. The return value 1 signals success; 0 signals a read error. fgetc returns int so EOF remains distinguishable from character values. The function counts newline bytes, not necessarily human-perceived lines: a final unterminated line adds no newline. The caller owns the FILE* and closes it when appropriate.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

01-W15 · Matrix indexing and traversal ★★ M WRITTEN

An int matrix a[2][3] occupies row-major storage. Assume 4-byte ints and base address 1000. Find the address of a[1][2], write pseudocode to transpose it into b[3][2], and state complexity.

Worked answer

The zero-based element offset is row * column_count + column = 1*3+2=5. Byte offset is 5*4=20, so the address is 1020.

Pseudocode
for i = 0 to 1:
    for j = 0 to 2:
        b[j][i] = a[i][j]
a row 0a row 1b after transpose for a={{1,2,3},{4,5,6}}
1, 2, 34, 5, 6rows: (1,4), (2,5), (3,6)

For r by c input, time is Theta(rc). The separate result stores rc elements. Excluding the required result, the loops use Theta(1) extra working space. A rectangular transpose is not obtained by simply exchanging dimensions while leaving element order unchanged.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

02 · Discrete & Concrete Mathematics, Probability and Statistics

Prioritize logic, counting, relations, graphs, modular arithmetic and probability. Every probability model states its sampling/independence assumptions. A few one-star matrix, calculus and numerical-method items are labelled enrichment under the broad mathematics heading; they are not separately named Group 1 requirements.

Coverage: Logic and proofs; sets, functions, relations and orders; sums and recurrences; number theory and counting; graph properties; conditional probability, distributions, moments and elementary statistical inference; mathematical enrichment.

Reading references: R6

02-M01 · Propositional logic ★★★ E MCQ

Which formula is logically equivalent to P → Q?

  1. ¬P ∧ Q
  2. P ∧ Q
  3. P ∨ ¬Q
  4. ¬P ∨ Q

Answer: D — ¬P ∨ Q

An implication is false only when P is true and Q is false. The formula ¬P ∨ Q has exactly that false case, so their truth tables agree.

02-M02 · Quantifiers and negation ★★★ E MCQ

What is the negation of ∀x P(x)?

  1. ∃x P(x)
  2. ∃x ¬P(x)
  3. ¬∃x ¬P(x)
  4. ∀x ¬P(x)

Answer: B — ∃x ¬P(x)

To refute the claim that every object has property P, it is enough that some object lacks P. Negation exchanges the universal and existential quantifiers and negates the predicate.

02-M03 · Sets ★★★ E MCQ

If |A|=18, |B|=15 and |A∩B|=6, what is |A∪B|?

  1. 9
  2. 27
  3. 33
  4. 39

Answer: B — 27

Inclusion-exclusion gives |A∪B|=|A|+|B|-|A∩B|. The six common elements were counted twice in 18+15, so subtract them once: 27.

02-M04 · Sets ★★★ E MCQ

How many subsets does a set of 7 distinct elements have?

  1. 49
  2. 128
  3. 127
  4. 14

Answer: B — 128

For each element there are two independent choices: include it or not. Thus there are 2^7=128 subsets, including both the empty set and the full set.

02-M05 · Functions ★★ M MCQ

How many functions are there from a 3-element set to a 4-element set?

  1. 64
  2. 81
  3. 12
  4. 24

Answer: A — 64

Each of the three domain elements may independently map to any of four codomain elements. The count is 4^3=64. Injectivity is not required.

02-M06 · Functions ★★ M MCQ

How many injective functions are there from a 3-element set to a 5-element set?

  1. 10
  2. 60
  3. 15
  4. 125

Answer: B — 60

Choose distinct images in order: 5 choices for the first domain element, 4 for the next and 3 for the last. The product is 60, not the unordered combination count.

02-M07 · Equivalence relations ★★★ E MCQ

Which collection of properties defines an equivalence relation?

  1. Symmetric and antisymmetric only
  2. Reflexive, antisymmetric and transitive
  3. Irreflexive and symmetric only
  4. Reflexive, symmetric and transitive

Answer: D — Reflexive, symmetric and transitive

Equivalence relations group elements into disjoint equivalence classes. Antisymmetry belongs to the usual definition of a partial order, not the general definition of equivalence.

02-M08 · Partial orders ★★ M MCQ

On the positive divisors of 12, order elements by divisibility. Which pair is incomparable?

  1. 4 and 12
  2. 3 and 6
  3. 3 and 4
  4. 2 and 4

Answer: C — 3 and 4

Two elements are comparable when one divides the other. Neither 3 divides 4 nor 4 divides 3, so they are incomparable. The other listed pairs have a divisibility relation.

02-M09 · Proof by induction ★★★ E MCQ

To prove a statement P(n) for every integer n≥1 by ordinary induction, which pair is sufficient?

  1. P(1), and P(k) implies P(k+1) for every k≥1
  2. P(1) and P(2) only
  3. P(n) checked for one hundred values
  4. P(k+1) implies P(k) with no base case

Answer: A — P(1), and P(k) implies P(k+1) for every k≥1

A base case starts the chain and the implication propagates truth one integer at a time. A finite set of numerical checks is not a proof for all positive integers.

02-M10 · Sums and concrete mathematics ★★★ M MCQ

What is 1+3+5+...+19?

  1. 90
  2. 100
  3. 110
  4. 190

Answer: B — 100

There are ten odd numbers. The sum of the first n odd positive integers is n^2, so the sum is 10^2=100. The arithmetic-series formula gives the same result.

02-M11 · Recurrences ★★ M MCQ

For T(1)=1 and T(n)=T(n-1)+2n-1, what is T(n)?

  1. 2n-1
  2. n^2
  3. n(n+1)/2
  4. 2^n

Answer: B — n^2

Unrolling gives T(n)=1+3+...+(2n-1)=n^2. Substituting (n-1)^2 into the recurrence also yields (n-1)^2+2n-1=n^2.

02-M12 · GCD and number theory ★★★ E MCQ

What is gcd(84,30)?

  1. 12
  2. 3
  3. 2
  4. 6

Answer: D — 6

Euclid gives 84=2*30+24, 30=1*24+6 and 24=4*6. The last nonzero remainder is 6.

02-M13 · Linear congruences ★★ M MCQ

How many incongruent solutions modulo 15 satisfy 6x ≡ 3 (mod 15)?

  1. 3
  2. 1
  3. 6
  4. 0

Answer: A — 3

A linear congruence ax≡b mod m has gcd(a,m) solutions when that gcd divides b. Here gcd(6,15)=3 divides 3, yielding three solutions: x≡3,8,13 mod 15.

02-M14 · Modular inverses ★★ M MCQ

What is the multiplicative inverse of 7 modulo 26?

  1. 19
  2. 7
  3. 21
  4. 15

Answer: D — 15

7*15=105=4*26+1, so 15 is the inverse. The inverse exists because gcd(7,26)=1.

02-M15 · Chinese remainder theorem ★ M MCQ

Which smallest nonnegative x satisfies x≡2 (mod 3) and x≡3 (mod 5)?

  1. 13
  2. 18
  3. 8
  4. 2

Answer: C — 8

Numbers congruent to 3 modulo 5 are 3,8,13,... . Of these, 8 is congruent to 2 modulo 3. Coprime moduli give one residue class modulo 15.

02-M16 · Permutations with repetition ★★★ M MCQ

How many distinct arrangements of the letters in BANANA exist?

  1. 30
  2. 720
  3. 60
  4. 120

Answer: C — 60

There are six letters with A repeated three times and N twice. Divide the 6! labelled arrangements by 3!2!: 720/12=60.

02-M17 · Combinations ★★★ E MCQ

A committee of 3 is selected from 8 people with no roles. How many committees exist?

  1. 336
  2. 64
  3. 56
  4. 24

Answer: C — 56

Order does not matter, so use C(8,3)=8*7*6/(3*2*1)=56. The permutation count would treat the same committee in different orders as different.

02-M18 · Pigeonhole principle ★★★ E MCQ

How many people guarantee that at least two share a birth month, allowing any month?

  1. 12
  2. 7
  3. 13
  4. 24

Answer: C — 13

There are twelve month categories. With twelve people, one per month is possible. A thirteenth person must enter an already occupied category.

02-M19 · Inclusion-exclusion ★★ M MCQ

Among integers 1 through 100, how many are divisible by 2 or 5?

  1. 60
  2. 70
  3. 50
  4. 40

Answer: A — 60

There are 50 multiples of 2 and 20 multiples of 5. Their overlap is the ten multiples of 10, so the union count is 50+20-10=60.

02-M20 · Stars and bars ★★ M MCQ

How many nonnegative integer solutions satisfy x+y+z=5?

  1. 10
  2. 125
  3. 21
  4. 15

Answer: C — 21

Place five identical units into three labelled variables. Stars and bars gives C(5+3-1,3-1)=C(7,2)=21. Allowing zero is important.

02-M21 · Derangements ★ M MCQ

How many permutations of three distinct items leave no item in its original position?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 6

Answer: B — 2

For items 1,2,3 the only derangements are 231 and 312. The identity and the three transpositions each leave at least one fixed point.

02-M22 · Graph degrees ★★★ E MCQ

An undirected graph has vertex degrees 3,3,2,2,2. How many edges does it have?

  1. 7
  2. 12
  3. 6
  4. 5

Answer: C — 6

The handshake lemma says each edge contributes two to the sum of degrees. The sum is 12, hence there are six edges.

02-M23 · Trees ★★★ E MCQ

An undirected tree has 17 vertices. How many edges does it contain?

  1. 18
  2. 16
  3. 17
  4. 34

Answer: B — 16

A finite connected acyclic undirected graph has exactly |V|-1 edges. Thus a 17-vertex tree has 16. Connectivity and acyclicity are essential.

02-M24 · Bipartite graphs ★★★ M MCQ

Which condition characterizes a finite undirected bipartite graph?

  1. It must be connected.
  2. It contains no odd cycle.
  3. Every vertex has even degree.
  4. It contains no even cycle.

Answer: B — It contains no odd cycle.

A bipartition alternates sides along every cycle, forcing an even length. Conversely, a graph without odd cycles can be two-coloured component by component.

02-M25 · Euler trails ★★ M MCQ

A connected undirected graph with exactly two odd-degree vertices has:

  1. No Euler trail
  2. An Euler circuit necessarily
  3. A Hamiltonian cycle necessarily
  4. An Euler trail but no Euler circuit

Answer: D — An Euler trail but no Euler circuit

An open Euler trail begins at one odd-degree vertex and ends at the other. A closed Euler circuit requires every vertex to have even degree. This says nothing general about visiting vertices exactly once.

02-M26 · Planar graphs ★ M MCQ

A connected planar embedding has 8 vertices and 12 edges. How many faces, including the unbounded face, does it have?

  1. 4
  2. 6
  3. 5
  4. 20

Answer: B — 6

Euler’s formula is V-E+F=2 for a connected planar embedding. Therefore F=2-8+12=6. The outside region is included.

02-M27 · Conditional probability ★★★ M MCQ

Use the joint-probability table. What is P(B | A)?

Bnot B
A0.180.12
not A0.220.48
  1. 0.6
  2. 0.4
  3. 0.3
  4. 0.18

Answer: A — 0.6

P(A)=0.18+0.12=0.30. Therefore P(B|A)=P(A∩B)/P(A)=0.18/0.30=0.60. Conditioning renormalizes within the A row.

02-M28 · Bayes theorem ★★★ M MCQ

A component defect rate is 0.02. A test detects a defect with probability 0.9 and falsely flags a good component with probability 0.1. What is P(defect | flagged)?

  1. 0.02
  2. 0.9
  3. Approximately 0.8448
  4. Approximately 0.1552

Answer: D — Approximately 0.1552

True flags have probability 0.02*0.9=0.018. False flags have probability 0.98*0.1=0.098. The posterior is 0.018/(0.018+0.098)=0.15517. The low base rate matters.

02-M29 · Independence ★★★ E MCQ

If independent events A and B have probabilities 0.4 and 0.5, what is P(A∩B)?

  1. 0.1
  2. 0.2
  3. 0.9
  4. 0.45

Answer: B — 0.2

Independence gives P(A∩B)=P(A)P(B)=0.4*0.5=0.2. Mutual exclusivity is a different condition and would give an empty intersection.

02-M30 · Total probability ★★★ M MCQ

Machine A makes 60% of items with defect rate 1%; B makes 40% with defect rate 3%. What is the overall defect probability?

  1. 0.012
  2. 0.04
  3. 0.02
  4. 0.018

Answer: D — 0.018

Weight each conditional defect rate by its production share: 0.6*0.01+0.4*0.03=0.006+0.012=0.018. A simple unweighted average would be inappropriate.

02-M31 · Binomial distribution ★★★ M MCQ

Four independent fair coin flips are made. What is the probability of exactly two heads?

  1. 1/2
  2. 1/16
  3. 3/8
  4. 1/4

Answer: C — 3/8

Choose the two head positions in C(4,2)=6 ways. Each complete sequence has probability 1/16, so the total is 6/16=3/8.

02-M32 · Expectation ★★★ E MCQ

A random variable X equals 0,1,2 with probabilities 0.2,0.5,0.3. What is E[X]?

  1. 1
  2. 0.9
  3. 1.1
  4. 1.3

Answer: C — 1.1

The weighted sum is 0*0.2+1*0.5+2*0.3=1.1. Expectation need not be one of the possible outcomes.

02-M33 · Variance ★★★ M MCQ

For a Bernoulli random variable with success probability 0.3, what is its variance?

  1. 0.09
  2. 0.7
  3. 0.21
  4. 0.3

Answer: C — 0.21

For an indicator X, X^2=X. Thus Var(X)=E[X^2]-E[X]^2=p-p^2=p(1-p)=0.3*0.7=0.21.

02-M34 · Linearity of expectation ★★★ M MCQ

If E[X]=3 and E[Y]=4, what is E[2X-Y], without assuming independence?

  1. Cannot be determined without independence
  2. 2
  3. 10
  4. -1

Answer: B — 2

Expectation is linear even for dependent variables: E[2X-Y]=2E[X]-E[Y]=6-4=2. Independence is needed for some variance/product formulas, not this identity.

02-M35 · Covariance and variance ★★ M MCQ

If Var(X)=4, Var(Y)=9 and Cov(X,Y)=1, what is Var(X+Y)?

  1. 13
  2. 5
  3. 14
  4. 15

Answer: D — 15

Var(X+Y)=Var(X)+Var(Y)+2Cov(X,Y)=4+9+2=15. Simply adding variances would ignore the nonzero covariance.

02-M36 · Normal distribution ★★ E MCQ

For X with mean 50 and standard deviation 5, what is the standardized z-score of x=60?

  1. 10
  2. 0.5
  3. 2
  4. -2

Answer: C — 2

Standardization subtracts the mean and divides by standard deviation: z=(60-50)/5=2. It does not divide by variance 25.

02-M37 · Poisson distribution ★★ M MCQ

A Poisson random variable has mean 3. What is its variance?

  1. 1/3
  2. 9
  3. sqrt(3)
  4. 3

Answer: D — 3

A Poisson distribution has both mean and variance equal to its parameter λ. Its standard deviation is sqrt(λ), which is different from the variance.

02-M38 · Geometric distribution ★★ M MCQ

Independent trials succeed with probability 0.25. Let X count trials up to and including the first success. What is E[X]?

  1. 0.75
  2. 4
  3. 3
  4. 0.25

Answer: B — 4

With the stated support 1,2,..., a geometric random variable has mean 1/p=4. A variable counting only failures before success would instead have mean (1-p)/p=3.

02-M39 · Sampling without replacement ★★★ M MCQ

A bag contains 3 red and 2 blue balls. Two balls are drawn without replacement. What is P(both red)?

  1. 3/10
  2. 1/5
  3. 9/25
  4. 3/5

Answer: A — 3/10

Multiply sequential probabilities: (3/5)*(2/4)=6/20=3/10. The second probability changes because the first red ball is not replaced.

02-M40 · Descriptive statistics ★★★ E MCQ

For the observations 1,2,2,7,8, what are the mean and median?

  1. 4 and 2
  2. 5 and 2
  3. 4 and 4
  4. 2 and 4

Answer: A — 4 and 2

The sum is 20, so the mean is 20/5=4. The sorted list has middle observation 2. Large upper observations pull the mean above the median.

02-M41 · Sample variance ★★ M MCQ

For observations 2,4,6, what is the sample variance using denominator n-1?

  1. 2
  2. 8/3
  3. 8
  4. 4

Answer: D — 4

The mean is 4. Squared deviations sum to 4+0+4=8. Divide by n-1=2 to obtain 4. Dividing by n would give the population-style variance 8/3.

02-M42 · Confidence intervals ★★ M MCQ

An independent sample has n=100, mean 20 and known population standard deviation 5. Using z=1.96, what is the approximate 95% normal-theory confidence interval for the mean?

  1. [10.2, 29.8]
  2. [19.5, 20.5]
  3. [19.02, 20.98]
  4. [15, 25]

Answer: C — [19.02, 20.98]

The standard error is 5/sqrt(100)=0.5. Margin=1.96*0.5=0.98, so the interval is 20±0.98. This assumes the normal-theory interval is appropriate.

02-M43 · Hypothesis tests ★★ M MCQ

Under the null model and a specified test statistic, a p-value measures:

  1. The effect size in the original units
  2. The probability that the alternative is false
  3. The probability that the null hypothesis is true
  4. The probability of a result at least as extreme as observed, in the test-defined direction(s)

Answer: D — The probability of a result at least as extreme as observed, in the test-defined direction(s)

A p-value is a tail probability calculated under the null model. It is not a posterior probability of a hypothesis and does not by itself measure practical importance.

02-M44 · Correlation ★★ M MCQ

A correlation coefficient of zero between two variables establishes which statement?

  1. They must be independent.
  2. They cannot have any nonlinear relationship.
  3. Their linear association, as measured by that coefficient, is zero.
  4. One must be constant.

Answer: C — Their linear association, as measured by that coefficient, is zero.

Zero correlation does not generally imply independence. For example, a symmetric zero-mean variable and its square can be dependent but have zero covariance. Special distribution assumptions can change the implication.

02-M45 · Continuous distributions ★★ M MCQ

X is uniform on [2,6]. What is P(3≤X≤5)?

  1. 1/2
  2. 1/4
  3. 3/4
  4. 2

Answer: A — 1/2

For a uniform distribution, probability is interval length divided by total support length. The event has length 2 and the support length 4, giving 1/2. Single endpoints have probability zero.

02-M46 · Matrix enrichment ★ E MCQ

Enrichment: A is 2×3 and B is 3×4. What is the size of AB?

  1. 3×3
  2. 2×4
  3. 4×2
  4. The product is undefined

Answer: B — 2×4

The inner dimensions match and are summed over. The result retains the two row indices of A and four column indices of B. This is supplementary mathematical practice, not a separate named Group 1 heading.

02-M47 · Matrix enrichment ★ M MCQ

Enrichment: what is the determinant of [[2,1],[3,4]]?

  1. 8
  2. 11
  3. 5
  4. -5

Answer: C — 5

For a 2×2 matrix [[a,b],[c,d]], det=ad-bc. Here 2*4-1*3=5. A nonzero determinant means the square matrix is invertible.

02-M48 · Calculus enrichment ★ M MCQ

Enrichment: what is the derivative of f(x)=3x^2+2x at x=2?

  1. 8
  2. 12
  3. 14
  4. 16

Answer: C — 14

Differentiate term by term: f′(x)=6x+2. Substituting x=2 gives 14. Derivatives also appear in optimization and ML, but calculus is not separately listed in the Group 1 notice.

02-M49 · Numerical-method enrichment ★ M MCQ

Enrichment: one Newton step for f(x)=x^2-2 from x0=1 gives which x1?

  1. 2
  2. 1.5
  3. 1
  4. 0.5

Answer: B — 1.5

Newton’s update is x1=x0-f(x0)/f′(x0). Here f(1)=-1 and f′(1)=2, so x1=1-(-1)/2=1.5. This is an iteration, not the exact root.

02-M50 · Modular exponentiation ★★ M MCQ

What is 3^5 mod 7?

  1. 5
  2. 1
  3. 3
  4. 6

Answer: A — 5

3^2≡2 and 3^4≡4 modulo 7. Therefore 3^5≡4*3≡12≡5. Reducing intermediate products avoids unnecessary large integers.

02-W01 · Logic and truth tables ★★★ M WRITTEN

Show with a truth table that P→Q is equivalent to ¬Q→¬P. Explain why Q→P is not generally equivalent.

Worked answer

PQP→Q¬Q→¬PQ→P
TTTTT
TFFFT
FTTTF
FFTTT

The implication and its contrapositive match in all four rows. The converse Q→P differs, for example when P is false and Q is true. The implication is true in that row but the converse is false. A proof of a converse therefore requires separate justification, rather than simply reversing the original claim.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

02-W02 · Relations and Hasse diagrams ★★ M WRITTEN

On S={1,2,3,6}, define aRb when a divides b. Prove this is a partial order, draw its Hasse diagram, and identify the least and greatest elements.

Worked answer

Reflexive: every positive integer divides itself. Antisymmetric: if a divides b and b divides a for positive integers, then a=b. Transitive: if b=ka and c=lb, then c=(lk)a, so a divides c.

The cover relations are 1<2, 1<3, 2<6 and 3<6. The least element is 1 and greatest is 6. The pair 2,3 is incomparable, so this is not a total order. The direct relation 1<6 holds but is omitted from the Hasse drawing because it is transitive.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

02-W03 · Induction and sums ★★★ M WRITTEN

Prove 1+3+...+(2n-1)=n^2 for every integer n≥1 by induction. Then use the result to evaluate 21+23+...+39.

Worked answer

Base n=1: 1=1^2. Inductive hypothesis: the first k odd numbers sum to k^2. Adding the next odd number gives k^2+(2k+1)=(k+1)^2, establishing the next case. By induction the formula holds for all positive n.

The sequence ending at 39 contains the first 20 odd numbers; the sequence ending at 19 contains the first 10. Subtract their sums: 21+23+...+39=20^2-10^2=400-100=300. The proof and the numerical substitution serve different purposes: one establishes the general identity, the other applies it.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

02-W04 · Linear congruences and inverses ★★ M WRITTEN

Solve 6x≡3 (mod 15), listing all distinct residues modulo 15. Explain why ordinary division by 6 is not valid modulo 15.

Worked answer

gcd(6,15)=3 divides the right side, so solutions exist. Divide the coefficients and modulus by 3 to obtain 2x≡1 (mod 5). The inverse of 2 modulo 5 is 3, hence x≡3 (mod 5).

Lift this residue class to modulo 15: x≡3,8,13. Check: 6*3=18, 6*8=48 and 6*13=78 all leave remainder 3 modulo 15.

The number 6 has no multiplicative inverse modulo 15 because their gcd is not 1. Cancelling 6 as though operating over the real numbers would lose the modular structure. After reducing by the common divisor, cancellation by an invertible coefficient becomes valid.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

02-W05 · Counting and inclusion-exclusion ★★★ M WRITTEN

A class has 40 students. Twenty-two know Python, 18 know C, and 10 know both. How many know at least one, exactly one, and neither? Draw a Venn-style count diagram.

Worked answer

At least one: 22+18-10=30. Python only: 22-10=12. C only: 18-10=8. Exactly one: 12+8=20. Neither: 40-30=10.

The four disjoint counts 12,10,8,10 sum to 40. This check catches double counting. The intersection is subtracted once in the union formula because each set total already contains it.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

02-W06 · Combinatorial selection ★★★ M WRITTEN

From 5 women and 4 men, choose a committee of 3 containing at least one woman. Calculate the count in two different ways and explain why order is irrelevant.

Worked answer

Complement method: all committees C(9,3)=84. Subtract all-male committees C(4,3)=4. The answer is 80.

WomenMenCount
12C(5,1)C(4,2)=30
21C(5,2)C(4,1)=40
30C(5,3)C(4,0)=10

The direct sum is 30+40+10=80. These cases are disjoint and exhaustive. The committee has no named positions, so permutations would overcount each group by the number of orderings. If chair/secretary roles were introduced, a different count would be needed.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

02-W07 · Bayes theorem and probability trees ★★★ M WRITTEN

A factory uses machine A for 70% of output and B for 30%. Their defect probabilities are 0.02 and 0.05. Draw a probability tree and find P(defect) and P(B|defect).

Worked answer

A and B partition the production source. Defect probability is P(A)P(D|A)+P(B)P(D|B)=0.7*0.02+0.3*0.05=0.014+0.015=0.029.

The defective items from B have probability 0.015. Therefore P(B|D)=0.015/0.029=15/29≈0.51724. Although B makes only 30% of output, its higher defect rate makes it responsible for slightly over half of defective items. The denominator must include defects from both machines.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

02-W08 · Joint probability and independence ★★★ M WRITTEN

Use the table to find P(A), P(B), P(B|A), and determine whether A and B are independent.

Bnot B
A0.180.12
not A0.220.48

Worked answer

First check normalization: 0.18+0.12+0.22+0.48=1, with all entries nonnegative. P(A)=0.30, and P(B)=0.40. P(B|A)=0.18/0.30=0.60.

Independence would require P(A∩B)=P(A)P(B)=0.30*0.40=0.12. The actual intersection is 0.18, so the events are dependent. Equivalently P(B|A)=0.60 differs from the marginal P(B)=0.40. Mutual exclusivity also fails because the intersection is nonzero.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

02-W09 · Expectation and variance ★★★ M WRITTEN

Let X take values 0,1,2 with probabilities 0.2,0.5,0.3. Compute E[X], E[X^2], Var(X), and Var(3X+2). Show all working.

Worked answer

xP(X=x)xPx²P
00.200
10.50.50.5
20.30.61.2
Total11.11.7

E[X]=1.1 and E[X^2]=1.7. Hence Var(X)=1.7-(1.1)^2=1.7-1.21=0.49. Adding a constant does not affect variance and multiplying by 3 scales it by 9: Var(3X+2)=9*0.49=4.41. Do not use E[X^2]=(E[X])^2 unless the variance is zero.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

02-W10 · Binomial distribution and assumptions ★★★ M WRITTEN

A reliable link independently delivers each packet with probability 0.8. Five packets are sent. Find the probability that exactly four arrive and that at least four arrive. Explain when the binomial model would be inappropriate.

Worked answer

Let X count delivered packets. Under the stated independence and identical success probability, X~Binomial(5,0.8). P(X=4)=C(5,4)(0.8)^4(0.2)=5*0.4096*0.2=0.4096.

P(X=5)=(0.8)^5=0.32768. Therefore P(X≥4)=0.4096+0.32768=0.73728.

The factor C(5,4) counts which four packet positions succeed. Correlated losses caused by a common outage or strongly varying per-packet probabilities violate this simple binomial model. The arithmetic should not be applied without its model assumptions.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

02-W11 · Graph degrees and Euler trails ★★ M WRITTEN

For the undirected graph shown, list degrees, decide whether an Euler trail/circuit exists, and provide a valid Euler trail.

Worked answer

VertexDegree
A3
B3
C2
D2

The graph is connected and exactly A and B have odd degree. Hence an open Euler trail exists from A to B, but no Euler circuit exists. One trail is A-C-B-D-A-B. It uses AC, CB, BD, DA and AB once each.

The degree sum is 10, confirming 5 edges. An Euler trail visits every edge once, while a Hamiltonian path concerns vertices. Repeating vertices in the listed trail is allowed.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

02-W12 · Descriptive statistics and confidence intervals ★★ M WRITTEN

For data 2,4,6, calculate the mean and unbiased sample variance. Separately, a large independent sample of size 100 has mean 20 and known population standard deviation 5. Form a 95% normal-theory confidence interval using z=1.96 and interpret it correctly.

Worked answer

For 2,4,6: mean=4. Squared deviations are 4,0,4; their sum is 8. Sample variance s²=8/(3-1)=4.

For the separate sample, standard error=5/sqrt(100)=0.5. Margin=1.96*0.5=0.98, giving [19.02,20.98]. The two datasets must not be combined.

Under repeated sampling and the model assumptions, the interval procedure covers the fixed population mean 95% of the time. A frequentist interpretation does not assign a 95% posterior probability to this fixed parameter after this particular interval has been computed.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

02-W13 · Recurrences ★★ M WRITTEN

Solve a_n=2a_(n-1)+1 for n≥1 with a_0=0, and prove the closed form. Calculate a_5.

Worked answer

Compute the first values: 0,1,3,7,15,31. Unrolling yields a_n=2^n a_0+(1+2+...+2^(n-1))=2^n-1.

Proof by induction: n=0 gives 0=1-1. If a_k=2^k-1, then a_(k+1)=2(2^k-1)+1=2^(k+1)-1. Thus the formula holds for all n≥0. In particular a_5=32-1=31.

A recurrence that adds a constant after doubling differs fundamentally from one that merely adds two at each step. Writing the first few terms helps detect the pattern, but the induction supplies the proof.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

02-W14 · Linear algebra enrichment ★ M WRITTEN

Enrichment: solve 2x+y=5 and x-y=1 using elimination. Write the coefficient matrix, compute its determinant, and explain uniqueness.

Worked answer

The coefficient matrix is A=[[2,1],[1,-1]], the unknown vector is [x,y]^T and the right-hand side is [5,1]^T. Adding the two equations gives 3x=6, so x=2. Substituting into x-y=1 gives y=1.

det(A)=2*(-1)-1*1=-3, which is nonzero, so the linear system has a unique solution. Check: 2*2+1=5 and 2-1=1.

This is enrichment useful for quantitative reasoning and ML. The official Group 1 notice does not separately list linear algebra as its own subject, unlike Group 2.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

02-W15 · Probability of system reliability ★★ M WRITTEN

Three components independently work with probabilities 0.9,0.8,0.7. Draw series and parallel arrangements. Find the probability each arrangement functions; a series system needs all components, while a parallel system needs at least one.

Worked answer

Series reliability=P(all work)=0.9*0.8*0.7=0.504. For parallel, first calculate total failure: all components fail independently with probability 0.1*0.2*0.3=0.006. Reliability is its complement, 1-0.006=0.994.

Adding the component success probabilities would double count overlapping successes and can exceed one. Independence is essential for the products; common-cause failures would require a different model.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

03 · Data Structures & Algorithms

State representation, tie-breaking and the cost model before tracing. Graph drawings are part of the questions, not decoration. Unless specified otherwise, vertices have distinct labels, arrays are zero-indexed and arithmetic comparisons take constant time.

Coverage: Asymptotic analysis and recurrences; lists, stacks and queues; binary trees, heaps, hashing and balanced trees; searching/sorting; graph traversal, shortest paths, MST and flow; greedy, dynamic programming and tractability.

Reading references: R7
