03-M01 · Asymptotic notation ★★★ E MCQ

What is the tight asymptotic bound for 3n^2+20n+7?

  1. Theta(n^2)
  2. Theta(n^3)
  3. Theta(n)
  4. Theta(log n)

Answer: A — Theta(n^2)

The quadratic term eventually dominates the lower-order terms. Constant multipliers do not change the asymptotic growth class. A tight bound describes both an upper and a lower asymptotic bound.

03-M02 · Loop complexity ★★★ E MCQ

How many times is work() called when n is a positive power of two?

Pseudocode
i = 1
while i <= n:
    work()
    i = 2 * i
  1. n/2
  2. n
  3. 2n
  4. log2(n)+1

Answer: D — log2(n)+1

The values are 1,2,4,...,n. Their exponents range from 0 to log2(n), inclusive, so there are log2(n)+1 calls.

03-M03 · Recurrences ★★★ M MCQ

For T(n)=2T(n/2)+n with constant T(1), what is the tight bound?

  1. Theta(log n)
  2. Theta(n log n)
  3. Theta(n)
  4. Theta(n^2)

Answer: B — Theta(n log n)

There are log2 n divide levels, each with total nonrecursive work n. Adding the leaf work gives Theta(n log n). The recurrence assumes balanced halves.

03-M04 · Linked lists ★★★ M MCQ

Given a pointer to the previous node in a singly linked list, what is the time to delete its non-null next node?

  1. Theta(log n)
  2. Theta(1)
  3. Theta(n log n)
  4. Theta(n)

Answer: B — Theta(1)

Relink previous->next to the removed node’s successor and release the removed node if owned. Finding the previous node may cost linear time, but the question supplies it.

03-M05 · Stacks ★★★ E MCQ

Perform push(2), push(5), pop(), push(7), pop() on an empty stack. What is the final stack from bottom to top?

  1. [7]
  2. [2,5]
  3. [2]
  4. []

Answer: C — [2]

The first pop removes 5, and the second removes 7. The earliest item, 2, remains. A stack is last-in, first-out.

03-M06 · Queues ★★★ E MCQ

An empty queue receives enqueue(A), enqueue(B), dequeue(), enqueue(C). What is the next dequeued item?

  1. C
  2. The queue is empty
  3. A
  4. B

Answer: D — B

A was removed by the first dequeue. The remaining order is B followed by C, so B is next. A queue preserves first-in, first-out ordering.

03-M07 · Circular queues ★★ M MCQ

A circular array queue has capacity 8 and maintains a separate count. Its tail points to the next free position and equals 7. After one successful enqueue, what is tail?

  1. 6
  2. 7
  3. 0
  4. 8

Answer: C — 0

Advance the tail modulo the capacity: (7+1) mod 8=0. The separate count distinguishes full and empty, so all eight array positions may be used.

03-M08 · Expression stacks ★★★ M MCQ

What is the value of the postfix expression 5 2 3 * + ?

  1. 11
  2. 13
  3. 16
  4. 21

Answer: A — 11

Push 5,2,3; multiply the top two to get 6; add 5 and 6 to obtain 11. In postfix, an operator follows its operands.

03-M09 · Binary-tree traversals ★★★ E MCQ

What is the inorder traversal of the pictured tree?

  1. 8,12,14,10,4,6,2
  2. 8,4,2,6,12,10,14
  3. 2,4,6,8,10,12,14
  4. 2,6,4,10,14,12,8

Answer: C — 2,4,6,8,10,12,14

Inorder visits left subtree, root, right subtree. On a binary search tree with distinct keys, this produces increasing order.

03-M10 · Binary-tree traversals ★★★ M MCQ

A binary tree has preorder A,B,D,E,C and inorder D,B,E,A,C. What is its postorder?

  1. D,E,B,C,A
  2. C,E,D,B,A
  3. A,B,D,E,C
  4. D,B,E,C,A

Answer: A — D,E,B,C,A

Preorder identifies A as root. Its inorder split makes D,B,E the left subtree and C the right. The left root is B with children D,E, giving postorder D,E,B,C,A.

03-M11 · Tree height ★★ M MCQ

A perfect binary tree has 31 nodes. If height counts edges from root to deepest leaf, what is its height?

  1. 15
  2. 30
  3. 4
  4. 5

Answer: C — 4

A perfect binary tree of edge-height h contains 2^(h+1)-1 nodes. Solving 31=2^5-1 gives h=4. Counting levels would give five, so the convention matters.

03-M12 · BST operations ★★★ M MCQ

In an ordinary binary search tree of height h, search takes what worst-case time?

  1. Always Theta(1)
  2. Theta(h+1)
  3. Theta(n^2)
  4. Always Theta(log n)

Answer: B — Theta(h+1)

A search follows at most one root-to-leaf path. For a balanced tree h is logarithmic, but a skewed tree can have h=n-1. The +1 covers visiting the root of height-zero trees.

03-M13 · AVL rotations ★★ M MCQ

Insert 30,20,10 into an initially empty AVL tree. Which root appears after rebalancing?

  1. 10
  2. 20
  3. The tree cannot be balanced
  4. 30

Answer: B — 20

The insertion creates a left-left imbalance at 30. A right rotation makes 20 the root, with 10 as left child and 30 as right child.

03-M14 · Heaps ★★★ E MCQ

In a zero-indexed binary heap array, what is the left-child index of index i?

  1. 2i
  2. 2i+1
  3. i/2
  4. 2i+2

Answer: B — 2i+1

The zero-indexed layout gives children 2i+1 and 2i+2. The formula applies only when the resulting index is within the heap size.

03-M15 · Heap operations ★★★ M MCQ

Remove the maximum from max-heap [20,15,18,8,10,12] and restore order by standard sift-down. What array results?

  1. [18,15,10,8,12]
  2. [15,12,18,8,10]
  3. [12,15,18,8,10]
  4. [18,15,12,8,10]

Answer: D — [18,15,12,8,10]

Move 12 from the last position to the root. Compare children 15 and 18; swap with the larger child 18. The 12 is now at a leaf, so the heap is [18,15,12,8,10].

03-M16 · Heap construction ★★ M MCQ

Bottom-up heap construction on n elements has tight worst-case running time:

  1. Theta(log n)
  2. Theta(n)
  3. Theta(n log n)
  4. Theta(n^2)

Answer: B — Theta(n)

Most nodes are close to leaves and require little sifting. Summing node counts by height gives a linear bound. Repeatedly inserting n elements is a different construction method.

03-M17 · Hashing ★★★ M MCQ

Using h(k)=k mod 7 and linear probing in an empty table, insert 10,17,24. Which slots hold them, in insertion order?

  1. 1,2,3
  2. 3,3,3
  3. 3,4,5
  4. 3,5,0

Answer: C — 3,4,5

Each key initially hashes to 3. The second probes to 4 and the third probes through occupied positions 3 and 4 to slot 5.

03-M18 · Hashing deletion ★★ M MCQ

Why does open addressing commonly use a tombstone after deleting a key?

  1. To force the table load factor to zero
  2. To preserve the probe sequence for keys inserted after collisions
  3. To make every unsuccessful search stop earlier
  4. To sort all remaining keys

Answer: B — To preserve the probe sequence for keys inserted after collisions

An empty slot can terminate an unsuccessful search. Replacing a deleted entry with a truly empty slot can incorrectly hide later collided keys. A tombstone permits probing to continue.

03-M19 · Binary search ★★★ M MCQ

Binary search repeatedly chooses floor((low+high)/2) on [2,5,8,11,14,17,20]. How many key comparisons find 5?

  1. 1
  2. 4
  3. 2
  4. 3

Answer: C — 2

First compare the middle key 11 at index 3, then search indices 0..2. The new middle is index 1 with key 5. That is two comparisons.

03-M20 · Sorting stability ★★ M MCQ

Which standard implementation is stable when equal keys are taken from the left half first during merging?

  1. Selection sort swapping the minimum into place
  2. Usual in-place quicksort partitioning
  3. In-place heapsort
  4. Merge sort

Answer: D — Merge sort

Taking equal keys from the left half preserves the earlier relative order during merging. The named standard implementations of the other algorithms can move equal keys past one another.

03-M21 · Insertion sort ★★★ M MCQ

What is the worst-case time of ordinary insertion sort on n distinct elements?

  1. Theta(n)
  2. Theta(n^2)
  3. Theta(log n)
  4. Theta(n log n)

Answer: B — Theta(n^2)

In reverse order, the kth insertion shifts all preceding elements. The total shifts are 1+2+...+(n-1)=n(n-1)/2, giving quadratic time.

03-M22 · Quicksort ★★ M MCQ

With the first element always selected as pivot, what is the worst-case time of standard quicksort on distinct sorted input?

  1. Theta(n^2)
  2. Theta(n log n)
  3. Theta(log n)
  4. Theta(n)

Answer: A — Theta(n^2)

The pivot becomes an extreme element, producing partitions of sizes 0 and n-1. The recurrence T(n)=T(n-1)+Theta(n) sums to quadratic time.

03-M23 · Counting sort ★★ M MCQ

Counting sort for n integer keys in the range 0..k has time complexity:

  1. Theta(n^2)
  2. Theta(k log n)
  3. Theta(n log n)
  4. Theta(n+k)

Answer: D — Theta(n+k)

The algorithm counts n input keys and processes a count array of size k+1. Its usefulness depends on the key range not being excessively large relative to n.

03-M24 · Comparison lower bounds ★ M MCQ

What is the worst-case comparison lower bound for sorting n distinct keys using only comparisons?

  1. Omega(n log n)
  2. Omega(n)
  3. Omega(log n)
  4. Omega(n^2)

Answer: A — Omega(n log n)

A decision tree must distinguish n! possible permutations. Its height is at least log2(n!), which is Omega(n log n). Noncomparison algorithms use additional key structure.

03-M25 · Graph representations ★★★ E MCQ

An adjacency matrix for a graph with V vertices uses what storage, regardless of edge count?

  1. Theta(log V)
  2. Theta(V+E)
  3. Theta(V^2)
  4. Theta(E)

Answer: C — Theta(V^2)

It reserves a cell for each ordered pair of vertices. For sparse graphs, adjacency lists can use less space by storing only existing edges and vertex headers.

03-M26 · Breadth-first search ★★★ E MCQ

BFS finds a shortest path in which sense on an unweighted graph?

  1. Minimum sum of arbitrary positive weights
  2. Minimum number of vertices in the entire graph
  3. Maximum flow
  4. Minimum number of edges

Answer: D — Minimum number of edges

BFS explores vertices in increasing edge-distance layers. It does not account for arbitrary edge weights; Dijkstra or another appropriate weighted algorithm is needed there.

03-M27 · Depth-first search ★★ M MCQ

Which observation certifies a directed cycle during standard three-colour DFS?

  1. Every edge leaving the root
  2. Any edge to any previously visited black vertex
  3. A vertex of outdegree zero
  4. An edge from the current exploration to a grey ancestor

Answer: D — An edge from the current exploration to a grey ancestor

Grey vertices are active on the DFS recursion stack. An edge to a grey ancestor closes a directed cycle. An edge to a finished black vertex alone need not do so.

03-M28 · Topological ordering ★★★ E MCQ

Which graph admits a topological ordering of all vertices?

  1. Every graph with positive edge weights
  2. Every directed graph
  3. A directed acyclic graph
  4. Only a complete undirected graph

Answer: C — A directed acyclic graph

A topological order places each directed edge source before its destination. A directed cycle would force a vertex to precede itself through the cycle, which is impossible.

03-M29 · Dijkstra shortest paths ★★★ M MCQ

Using the directed weighted graph, what is the shortest distance from S to T?

  1. 11
  2. 6
  3. 8
  4. 7

Answer: D — 7

The route S→B→A→C→T has cost 1+2+1+3=7. Alternative routes S→A→C→T and S→A→T cost 8 and 11 respectively. Dijkstra’s nonnegative-weight assumption holds.

03-M30 · Shortest-path assumptions ★★★ M MCQ

Why can standard Dijkstra finalization fail with a negative edge?

  1. A later path can improve a vertex already finalized.
  2. BFS then always becomes correct.
  3. Negative edges always imply a negative cycle.
  4. Distances can no longer be added.

Answer: A — A later path can improve a vertex already finalized.

The greedy finalization relies on extending a path not decreasing its cost. A negative edge can invalidate that argument without any negative cycle. Bellman-Ford handles negative edges under suitable conditions.

03-M31 · Bellman-Ford ★★ M MCQ

After V-1 relaxation rounds, an additional relaxation still improves a distance from a reachable source. What does this indicate?

  1. A reachable negative-weight cycle
  2. A unique minimum spanning tree
  3. A disconnected graph only
  4. A positive cycle only

Answer: A — A reachable negative-weight cycle

Without a reachable negative cycle, a shortest simple path uses at most V-1 edges. Continued improvement after those rounds signals a reachable negative cycle. An unreachable negative cycle is not detected from this source.

03-M32 · Minimum spanning trees ★★★ M MCQ

What is the MST total weight of this connected undirected graph?

  1. 7
  2. 5
  3. 6
  4. 8

Answer: C — 6

Kruskal chooses AB=1, BC=2 and CD=3. These connect all four vertices without a cycle, for total 6. An MST has V-1 edges in a connected graph.

03-M33 · Union-find ★★ M MCQ

Which task does disjoint-set union support directly in Kruskal’s algorithm?

  1. Testing whether adding an edge joins different connected components
  2. Sorting edge weights without comparisons
  3. Computing all-pairs shortest paths
  4. Balancing the final spanning tree height

Answer: A — Testing whether adding an edge joins different connected components

An edge is accepted only when its endpoints have different representatives. Union then merges their components. Path compression and union by rank/size improve the data structure’s amortized cost.

03-M34 · Greedy algorithms ★★ M MCQ

For activity selection on one resource with compatible nonoverlapping intervals, which standard greedy rule maximizes the number selected?

  1. Choose the available activity with earliest finishing time
  2. Choose the longest activity first
  3. Choose the latest finishing time
  4. Choose the highest activity identifier

Answer: A — Choose the available activity with earliest finishing time

Finishing earliest leaves at least as much remaining time as any other first choice. An exchange argument establishes the optimality for maximizing the count, not arbitrary weighted profit.

03-M35 · Fractional and 0/1 knapsack ★★ M MCQ

Which problem is optimally solved by sorting items by value/weight and taking as much as possible?

  1. All negative-edge shortest paths
  2. Fractional knapsack
  3. General 0/1 knapsack
  4. Longest common subsequence

Answer: B — Fractional knapsack

Divisibility permits exchanging fractions and justifies the ratio rule. In 0/1 knapsack, indivisible item combinations can beat the ratio-based selection.

03-M36 · Dynamic programming ★★★ M MCQ

Which pair most directly motivates a dynamic-programming solution?

  1. Only a sorted input array
  2. A graph with exactly one vertex
  3. Overlapping subproblems and reusable optimal substructure
  4. Random choices and no state

Answer: C — Overlapping subproblems and reusable optimal substructure

Dynamic programming stores results for subproblems that recur and combines them according to the problem structure. Defining a valid state and recurrence matters more than just writing a table.

03-M37 · 0/1 knapsack ★★ M MCQ

Items have (weight,value) pairs (2,3),(3,4),(4,5). Capacity is 5 and items are indivisible. What is the optimum value?

  1. 6
  2. 8
  3. 7
  4. 5

Answer: C — 7

Choose the first two items for weight 2+3=5 and value 3+4=7. The weight-four item alone gives five, and no other pair fits.

03-M38 · Longest common subsequence ★★ M MCQ

What is the LCS length of ABCD and ACBD?

  1. 3
  2. 4
  3. 1
  4. 2

Answer: A — 3

ABD and ACD are both common subsequences of length three. Length four would require the same ordering of B and C in both strings, which is not present.

03-M39 · Amortized analysis ★★ M MCQ

An array-backed dynamic list doubles capacity when full. Over n appends from empty, what is the amortized cost per append, assuming constant-time element copying?

  1. Theta(log n)
  2. Theta(n)
  3. Theta(n log n)
  4. Theta(1)

Answer: D — Theta(1)

Individual expansions cost linear time, but copies over capacities 1,2,4,... sum to O(n). Including n direct writes, total cost is Theta(n), so the per-append amortized cost is constant.

03-M40 · B-trees ★ M MCQ

A B-tree has minimum degree t=3. What is the maximum number of keys in one node?

  1. 3
  2. 5
  3. 7
  4. 6

Answer: B — 5

With minimum-degree convention t, a node holds at most 2t-1 keys and at most 2t children. Here 2*3-1=5. Other definitions of order exist, so the convention is explicitly stated.

03-M41 · Tries ★ M MCQ

Searching a key of length L in a trie with constant-time child lookup takes what time?

  1. O(nL) necessarily
  2. O(L)
  3. O(1) regardless of key length
  4. Always O(log n)

Answer: B — O(L)

The traversal follows one child per key symbol. The bound depends on key length rather than the total number of stored keys, assuming the stated child-access model.

03-M42 · Maximum flow ★★ M MCQ

What is the maximum S–T flow in the shown directed network?

  1. 3
  2. 4
  3. 6
  4. 5

Answer: D — 5

Send 2 along S-A-T, 1 along S-A-B-T, and 2 along S-B-T. This gives 5. The cut leaving S has capacity 3+2=5, proving no larger flow is possible.

03-M43 · Bipartite matching ★★ M MCQ

A bipartite graph has edges L1-R1, L1-R2, L2-R2, L3-R2, L3-R3. What is the maximum matching size?

  1. 4
  2. 5
  3. 2
  4. 3

Answer: D — 3

The matching {L1-R1,L2-R2,L3-R3} uses distinct endpoints and has three edges. The left side contains only three vertices, so no larger matching is possible.

03-M44 · Algorithm correctness ★★★ M MCQ

For binary search on a sorted array, which invariant is appropriate?

  1. If the target exists, it is within the current search interval.
  2. The array becomes sorted during the search.
  3. The target is always at the midpoint.
  4. Every discarded item equals the target.

Answer: A — If the target exists, it is within the current search interval.

Comparisons discard only a half that cannot contain the target. The invariant narrows the possible location until the target is found or the interval is empty. Sorting is a precondition.

03-M45 · Order statistics ★ M MCQ

Which worst-case bound is achieved by deterministic median-of-medians selection of the kth smallest item?

  1. O(n log n) is unavoidable
  2. O(n^2)
  3. O(n)
  4. O(log n)

Answer: C — O(n)

A pivot with a guaranteed rank balance prevents the pathological partitions of naive selection. Selecting one rank needs less information than sorting every key.

03-M46 · Complexity classes ★★ M MCQ

For a decision problem, membership in NP means:

  1. The problem is definitely not polynomial-time solvable.
  2. Yes instances have polynomial-size certificates verifiable in polynomial time.
  3. All instances are solved by greedy methods.
  4. No algorithm exists.

Answer: B — Yes instances have polynomial-size certificates verifiable in polynomial time.

NP is defined by polynomial-time verification of yes certificates, equivalently nondeterministic polynomial time. P is contained in NP; the term does not mean non-polynomial.

03-M47 · Reductions and NP-hardness ★ M MCQ

If known NP-hard problem A polynomial-time reduces to B, what follows?

  1. B is NP-hard.
  2. B is necessarily in NP.
  3. B must be in P.
  4. A becomes easy automatically.

Answer: A — B is NP-hard.

An efficient solver for B would solve A using the reduction. This establishes hardness, but NP membership requires its own verification argument. The reduction direction is important.

03-M48 · Backtracking ★★ M MCQ

In an N-queens backtracking search, which partial-state test permits immediate pruning?

  1. There are unused columns.
  2. Two already placed queens attack each other.
  3. No complete solution has been printed yet.
  4. The board size is even.

Answer: B — Two already placed queens attack each other.

Future placements cannot repair an existing attack among fixed queens. Rejecting that partial state is sound. Unused columns are expected before all queens are placed.

03-M49 · Graph connectivity ★★ M MCQ

A DFS on an undirected graph is restarted at each unvisited vertex. What does the number of DFS restarts count?

  1. Simple cycles
  2. Connected components
  3. Maximum matching size
  4. Edges

Answer: B — Connected components

Each DFS reaches exactly the vertices connected to its starting vertex. Starting again at an unvisited vertex explores a different component. Isolated vertices each form a component.

03-M50 · Divide and conquer ★★ M MCQ

Merging two sorted arrays of lengths m and n into a new array takes what worst-case time?

  1. Theta(m+n)
  2. Theta(m log n)
  3. Theta(mn)
  4. Theta(log(m+n))

Answer: A — Theta(m+n)

Advance one of two pointers after each output and copy any remainder. The output has m+n elements, so writing it already requires linear time in the total input size.

03-W01 · Stacks and expression processing ★★★ M WRITTEN

Convert (A+B)*C-D to postfix, then evaluate the postfix form when A=2, B=3, C=4, D=5. Show the evaluation stack after each numeric token/operator.

Worked answer

Postfix is A B + C * D -. Substituting values gives 2 3 + 4 * 5 -.

For subtraction, pop the right operand first and the left operand second; compute left-right. The final value is 15. A single pass uses O(k) time for k tokens and O(k) worst-case stack space. Parentheses guide conversion but do not appear in postfix.

03-W02 · Queues and BFS ★★★ M WRITTEN

Write BFS pseudocode using a queue and visited set. Explain when vertices are marked visited, and give time/space bounds for adjacency lists.

Worked answer

Pseudocode
BFS(G, s):
    for each vertex v:
        visited[v] = false
        distance[v] = infinity
    visited[s] = true
    distance[s] = 0
    enqueue(Q, s)
    while Q is not empty:
        u = dequeue(Q)
        for each v in Adj[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = true
                distance[v] = distance[u] + 1
                parent[v] = u
                enqueue(Q, v)

Mark a vertex when it is enqueued, not only when dequeued, to prevent duplicate enqueues through different neighbours. BFS processes increasing distance layers, so the first discovered distance is the minimum number of edges. Time is O(V+E), with O(V) auxiliary arrays and queue. Unreachable vertices keep infinite distance.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

03-W03 · BST drawing and traversals ★★★ M WRITTEN

Insert 8,4,12,2,6,10,14 into an empty BST. Draw the final tree; give preorder, inorder, postorder and its edge-height.

Worked answer

QuantityAnswer
Preorder8,4,2,6,12,10,14
Inorder2,4,6,8,10,12,14
Postorder2,6,4,10,14,12,8
Height in edges2

Each insertion follows left for a smaller key and right for a larger key. The tree has three levels, corresponding to two edges on the longest root-to-leaf path. Inorder is increasing because this is a BST with distinct keys.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

03-W04 · AVL rebalancing ★★ M WRITTEN

Insert 30,20,10 into an empty AVL tree. Draw the tree before and after rebalancing, identify the case and rotation, and give the balance factor of the final root using height(left)-height(right).

Worked answer

Before rebalancing, 30 has left child 20, which has left child 10. This is a left-left imbalance. Rotate right at 30: 20 becomes root, 10 remains its left child, and 30 becomes its right child.

The final root has equally high one-node subtrees, so its balance factor is 0. The rotation preserves the inorder sequence 10,20,30, hence preserves BST ordering. A left-right case would require two rotations, not this single one.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

03-W05 · Heaps and sift-down ★★★ M WRITTEN

Draw the max-heap represented by [20,15,18,8,10,12]. Remove the maximum, show the array after replacing the root and after sifting, and state deletion complexity.

Worked answer

Remove 20 and move the final item 12 to the root; reduce the size: [12,15,18,8,10]. The larger root child is 18, so swap positions 0 and 2. Final array: [18,15,12,8,10].

Always select the larger child in a max-heap sift-down. Choosing 15 first would leave 18 greater than the root. The path length is O(log n), so deletion costs O(log n) time and O(1) extra space for iterative sifting.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

03-W06 · Hashing and deletion ★★ M WRITTEN

Use a size-7 table, h(k)=k mod 7, and linear probing. Insert 10,17,24,8. Show the table, delete 17 using a tombstone, and trace a search for 24 afterward.

Worked answer

Slot0123456
After insertsempty8empty101724empty
After deleting 17empty8empty10DELETED24empty

10,17 and 24 all hash to 3, so they occupy 3,4,5. The key 8 hashes to 1. To search for 24, examine slot 3 (10), continue through tombstone 4, and find 24 at 5.

Changing slot 4 to genuinely empty would permit a standard search to stop too early. A tombstone preserves search correctness but can lengthen probe chains; resizing/rehashing may be used to clean accumulated tombstones.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

03-W07 · Sorting and stability ★★★ M WRITTEN

Run insertion sort on [5,2,4,1], showing the array after each insertion. Give best/worst time bounds and explain how the usual implementation preserves stability.

Worked answer

InsertionArray
Initial5,2,4,1
Insert 22,5,4,1
Insert 42,4,5,1
Insert 11,2,4,5

Shift preceding elements only while they are strictly greater than the key. Equal keys are not shifted past the newly inserted equal key, preserving their original order. If the comparison used >= instead, this stability argument would fail.

Already sorted input requires one unsuccessful shift test per insertion, giving Theta(n) time. Reverse-sorted input causes 1+...+(n-1) shifts, giving Theta(n²). Auxiliary space is Theta(1).

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

03-W08 · Dijkstra with trace table ★★★ H WRITTEN

Run Dijkstra from S on the graph. Show settled vertices and tentative distances, then give a shortest path to T. Break any distance tie alphabetically.

Worked answer

Settled nowd(A)d(B)d(C)d(T)
S41∞∞
B316∞
A31410
C3147
T3147

Parent links yield S→B→A→C→T, with cost 1+2+1+3=7. Settling B improves A, and settling A improves C. A settled distance is final because all weights are nonnegative.

With adjacency lists and a binary heap, a standard bound is O((V+E) log V); an array-based implementation is O(V²). State the implementation rather than claiming one universal complexity.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

03-W09 · Minimum spanning trees ★★★ M WRITTEN

Apply Kruskal to the graph. Show the sorted edge list, selected edges and final MST diagram.

Worked answer

Sorted edges: AB(1), BC(2), CD(3), AC(4), BD(5). Accept AB, then BC, then CD; at that point all four vertices are connected by three edges. The total is 1+2+3=6.

AC would close cycle A-B-C-A; BD would close B-C-D-B. The cut/exchange property justifies selecting the lightest available edge joining different components. Distinct edge weights here also make the MST unique.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

03-W10 · Topological order and dependencies ★★ M WRITTEN

Give a topological order for this dependency graph. Use Kahn’s algorithm and break available-vertex ties alphabetically. State what detects a cycle.

Worked answer

StepChosenNewly zero-indegree after removal
1Anone
2BC, D
3Cnone
4DE
5Enone

The tie rule gives A,B,C,D,E. Every edge points from an earlier to a later vertex. Maintain indegrees and a collection of zero-indegree vertices. If the collection becomes empty before V vertices are output, the remaining graph contains a directed cycle. Without a tie rule multiple valid topological orders may exist.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

03-W11 · Dynamic programming: knapsack ★★ H WRITTEN

Solve 0/1 knapsack for items (weight,value)=(2,3),(3,4),(4,5) and capacity 5. Define the DP state, give its recurrence and full table, and recover the selected items.

Worked answer

Let D[i,c] be the maximum value using the first i items within capacity c. Base D[0,c]=0. If wi>c, copy D[i-1,c]. Otherwise take max(D[i-1,c], vi+D[i-1,c-wi]). Using the previous row enforces each item at most once.

Items usedc=012345
None000000
(2,3)003333
+ (3,4)003447
+ (4,5)003457

D[3,5]=7. Since it equals D[2,5], item 3 can be skipped. D[2,5] exceeds D[1,5], so choose item 2 and reduce capacity to 2. Choose item 1 there. Total weight=5, value=7. Time O(nW), table space O(nW).

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

03-W12 · Dynamic programming: LCS ★★ H WRITTEN

Find the LCS length and one LCS of ABCD and ACBD using dynamic programming. Define the recurrence and show the table.

Worked answer

Let L[i,j] be the LCS length of the first i characters of the first string and first j of the second. Empty-prefix values are zero. Matching final characters give 1+L[i-1,j-1]; otherwise use max(L[i-1,j],L[i,j-1]).

∅ACBD
∅00000
A01111
B01122
C01222
D01223

The final length is 3; ABD or ACD is a valid recovered subsequence. A subsequence may skip symbols, unlike a contiguous substring. The DP takes O(mn) time and O(mn) space when retaining the full traceback table.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

03-W13 · Maximum flow and cut proof ★★ H WRITTEN

Find a maximum flow in this network and prove optimality using a cut. Draw a diagram labelled flow/capacity.

Worked answer

Send 2 on S-A-T, 1 on S-A-B-T and 2 on S-B-T. Arc flows are SA=3, SB=2, AB=1, AT=2, BT=3. Flow conservation holds: A receives 3 and sends 1+2; B receives 2+1 and sends 3.

The flow value is 5. The cut ({S},{A,B,T}) has capacity SA+SB=3+2=5. Every feasible flow is at most any cut capacity, so the feasible value 5 meets an upper bound and is optimal. This proves the answer without needing to enumerate every augmentation.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

03-W14 · Recurrences and algorithm analysis ★★★ M WRITTEN

Solve T(n)=2T(n/2)+n with T(1)=1 for powers of two. Give an exact form and a recursion-tree explanation.

Worked answer

At level 0 the work is n; at level 1 there are two problems of size n/2 with total n; each internal level similarly costs n. There are log2 n internal levels. At the leaves there are n instances of T(1)=1, costing n.

Hence T(n)=n log2 n+n. For n=8, this gives 8*3+8=32. Check by recurrence: T(2)=4, T(4)=12 and T(8)=32.

The exact form depends on the given base and additive term. The asymptotic result is Theta(n log n). Saying two recursive calls imply Theta(2^n) would ignore that the subproblem size halves.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

03-W15 · Greedy limitations and correctness ★★ H WRITTEN

Show with a numerical counterexample why value/weight greedy is not generally correct for 0/1 knapsack. Then explain why a greedy algorithm needs a proof rather than several successful tests.

Worked answer

Capacity is 50. Items are (weight,value)=(10,60),(20,100),(30,120), with ratios 6,5,4. Ratio-greedy chooses the first two, obtaining value 160 at weight 30; the third no longer fits. Choosing the second and third yields weight 50 and value 220, strictly better.

For fractional knapsack, the remaining 20 capacity could take two-thirds of the third item, and the ratio method is appropriate. Indivisibility changes the structure.

A greedy proof normally uses an exchange or cut argument showing an optimal solution can include the greedy choice without worsening the objective. A finite collection of successful test cases cannot establish that universal claim.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

04 · Compiler and Theory of Computation

Use explicit alphabets and acceptance conventions. In automata, a double circle marks acceptance and the incoming arrow marks the start. In grammars, ε is the empty string and $ is the end-of-input marker. Compiler transformations preserve the stated language semantics; algebraic rewrites are not automatically valid under overflow or floating-point rules.

Coverage: Alphabets and languages; DFA/NFA/regular expressions; closure, minimization and pumping; CFG, PDA and parsing; lexing, FIRST/FOLLOW and LL/LR concepts; semantic analysis, intermediate code and optimization; decidability and complexity boundaries.

Reading references: R8, R9

04-M01 · Formal languages ★★★ E MCQ

Over alphabet {0,1}, how many distinct strings have length exactly 4?

  1. 8
  2. 16
  3. 15
  4. 4

Answer: B — 16

Each of four positions independently has two choices, yielding 2^4=16. The empty string has length zero and is not included.

04-M02 · Formal languages ★★★ E MCQ

Which statement about ε is correct?

  1. It is an alphabet symbol in every language.
  2. It is the unique string of length zero.
  3. It is the empty set of strings.
  4. It has length one.

Answer: B — It is the unique string of length zero.

ε denotes an empty sequence of symbols. The language {ε} contains one string; the empty language ∅ contains no strings. Confusing them changes concatenation and closure results.

04-M03 · Regular expressions ★★★ E MCQ

Over {0,1}, which regular expression denotes all strings ending in 01?

  1. 01(0|1)*
  2. (0|1)*01
  3. 0*1*
  4. (01)*

Answer: B — (0|1)*01

An arbitrary prefix over the alphabet is followed by the required final symbols 01. The expression beginning with 01 instead constrains the prefix, not the suffix.

04-M04 · Regular expressions ★★★ E MCQ

Which language is denoted by a*b*?

  1. Zero or more a symbols followed by zero or more b symbols
  2. All strings beginning in b and ending in a
  3. All strings with equal numbers of a and b
  4. Only nonempty alternating strings

Answer: A — Zero or more a symbols followed by zero or more b symbols

The two repetitions occur in a fixed order and each may be empty. Thus ε, aaa, bbb and aabb are included, but ba is not.

04-M05 · DFA semantics ★★★ M MCQ

A complete DFA transition function has which type?

  1. Q × Σ → Q
  2. Q × Σ → 2^Q
  3. Q → Σ*
  4. Σ* → 2^Q

Answer: A — Q × Σ → Q

A deterministic transition chooses exactly one next state for every state-symbol pair. A transition function returning a set of states describes nondeterministic behaviour.

04-M06 · NFA equivalence ★★★ M MCQ

Compared with DFAs, finite NFAs recognize:

  1. Strictly more languages
  2. All context-free languages
  3. Exactly the regular languages, just as DFAs do
  4. Only finite languages

Answer: C — Exactly the regular languages, just as DFAs do

Subset construction converts any finite NFA into an equivalent DFA. Nondeterminism may reduce the number of states in a description but does not expand finite-automaton language power.

04-M07 · NFA subset construction ★★ M MCQ

An NFA has n states. A direct subset-construction DFA has at most how many states?

  1. n^2
  2. n
  3. 2n
  4. 2^n

Answer: D — 2^n

Each DFA state represents a subset of NFA states. There are 2^n such subsets, including the empty subset. Some subsets may be unreachable, so this is an upper bound.

04-M08 · Epsilon closure ★★ M MCQ

An ε-closure of state q includes:

  1. Only accepting states
  2. Every state in every NFA
  3. Only states reached after one input symbol
  4. q and every state reachable using only zero or more ε-transitions

Answer: D — q and every state reachable using only zero or more ε-transitions

Zero transitions include q itself. Epsilon moves consume no input, and the closure follows any number of them. Symbol-labelled transitions are not used when computing this closure.

04-M09 · DFA tracing ★★★ M MCQ

For the displayed DFA, is input 1101 accepted?

  1. No; the final state is q0.
  2. The automaton cannot read 1.
  3. Yes; the final state is q2.
  4. No; the final state is q1.

Answer: C — Yes; the final state is q2.

The state path is q0 --1→ q0 --1→ q0 --0→ q1 --1→ q2. The final state accepts, consistent with the suffix 01.

04-M10 · Minimal DFAs ★★ M MCQ

What is the minimum number of states in a complete DFA over {0,1} that recognizes an even number of 1s, including zero?

  1. 4
  2. 3
  3. 1
  4. 2

Answer: D — 2

Two states record parity: even and odd. Reading 0 leaves parity unchanged; reading 1 toggles it. Both states are distinguishable because ε is accepted from even but not odd.

04-M11 · DFA distinguishability ★★ M MCQ

Two DFA states p and q are distinguishable when:

  1. They both have self-loops.
  2. They have different names.
  3. Their outgoing labels are printed in different orders.
  4. Some suffix leads to acceptance from exactly one of them.

Answer: D — Some suffix leads to acceptance from exactly one of them.

Equivalent states must agree on acceptance for every possible remaining input string. A single suffix that separates acceptance proves they cannot be merged.

04-M12 · Regular-language closure ★★ M MCQ

Which operation preserves regularity for arbitrary regular languages L1 and L2?

  1. Requiring equal unbounded counts in every result
  2. An arbitrary operation producing any chosen language
  3. Replacing the language by the halting problem
  4. Intersection

Answer: D — Intersection

A product DFA can track both input automata and accept when both components accept. Regular languages are also closed under union, complement, concatenation and Kleene star.

04-M13 · Complement of DFA language ★★ M MCQ

For a complete DFA, how is an automaton for the complement language obtained?

  1. Reverse every edge only.
  2. Exchange alphabet symbols only.
  3. Exchange accepting and nonaccepting states.
  4. Delete the initial state.

Answer: C — Exchange accepting and nonaccepting states.

A complete DFA produces exactly one terminal state for each input. Flipping final-state status flips acceptance for every string. Incompleteness must be repaired with a sink state first.

04-M14 · Pumping lemma ★★ M MCQ

What can the regular-language pumping lemma directly help prove?

  1. Two arbitrary programs are equivalent.
  2. A language is not regular by contradiction.
  3. A particular program always terminates.
  4. Every language satisfying a chosen example is regular.

Answer: B — A language is not regular by contradiction.

Assume regularity and its pumping length, then choose a string for which every allowed decomposition fails under some pumping exponent. Merely finding a pumpable example proves nothing about the whole language.

04-M15 · Nonregular languages ★★★ M MCQ

Which language is not regular?

  1. {a^n b^n : n≥0}
  2. a*b*
  3. (ab)*
  4. All binary strings of even length

Answer: A — {a^n b^n : n≥0}

A finite automaton cannot remember an unbounded count of a symbols to match exactly that many b symbols. A stack-based machine can recognize this context-free language.

04-M16 · Context-free grammars ★★★ M MCQ

Which grammar generates {a^n b^n : n≥0}?

  1. S → abS | ε
  2. S → aSb | ε
  3. S → aS | b
  4. S → aS | bS | ε

Answer: B — S → aSb | ε

Each application of aSb adds one a on the left and one b on the right. Ending with ε produces equal counts in the required order. The base case includes n=0.

04-M17 · Derivations ★★★ E MCQ

In a leftmost derivation, which symbol is expanded at each step?

  1. The leftmost terminal
  2. The shortest symbol name
  3. The rightmost nonterminal
  4. The leftmost nonterminal

Answer: D — The leftmost nonterminal

The derivation order specifies which nonterminal is replaced next. Terminals are never expanded by grammar productions. Leftmost and rightmost derivations may describe the same parse tree.

04-M18 · Grammar ambiguity ★★★ M MCQ

A CFG is ambiguous if:

  1. It has more than one production.
  2. Some generated string has two distinct parse trees.
  3. It uses ε.
  4. Its alphabet has two symbols.

Answer: B — Some generated string has two distinct parse trees.

Two different derivation orders for the same tree do not establish ambiguity. Distinct parse trees, or distinct leftmost derivations for the same string, do.

04-M19 · Expression ambiguity ★★★ M MCQ

For E→E+E | E*E | id, which string demonstrates the missing precedence/association structure?

  1. ε
  2. +id
  3. id
  4. id+id*id

Answer: D — id+id*id

The string can parse as (id+id)*id or id+(id*id). Since the grammar does not prioritize * over +, the two parse trees are both permitted.

04-M20 · Left recursion ★★★ M MCQ

Which production pattern has immediate left recursion?

  1. A → Aα | β
  2. A → αAβ, where α begins with a terminal
  3. A → Bα, with no other rules specified
  4. A → aA | b

Answer: A — A → Aα | β

Immediate left recursion means the right-hand side begins with the same nonterminal as the left-hand side. It is troublesome for naive recursive-descent parsing.

04-M21 · Removing left recursion ★★★ M MCQ

For E→E+T | T, which replacement removes immediate left recursion?

  1. E→ET′; T′→+T | ε
  2. E→+TE | T
  3. E→T+E only
  4. E→TE′; E′→+TE′ | ε

Answer: D — E→TE′; E′→+TE′ | ε

The replacement begins with T and repeats +T through E′. It preserves the sequence language of terms separated by plus signs while eliminating immediate left recursion.

04-M22 · Left factoring ★★ M MCQ

Left-factor S→if E then S else S | if E then S. What common prefix is extracted?

  1. E else
  2. if only cannot be factored further
  3. else S
  4. if E then S

Answer: D — if E then S

Both alternatives share the full prefix if E then S. The factored remainder chooses else S or ε. Factoring alone does not necessarily resolve dangling-else ambiguity.

04-M23 · FIRST sets ★★★ E MCQ

For A→aA | ε, what is FIRST(A)?

  1. {ε}
  2. {a}
  3. {A,a}
  4. {a, ε}

Answer: D — {a, ε}

A can begin with terminal a or derive the empty string. A nonterminal name itself does not appear as an input terminal in FIRST(A).

04-M24 · FIRST sets ★★★ M MCQ

If FIRST(A)={a,ε} and FIRST(B)={b}, what is FIRST(AB)?

  1. {a,b}
  2. {b,ε}
  3. {a,b,ε}
  4. {a,ε}

Answer: A — {a,b}

A may produce a first, or disappear and expose the first symbol b from B. AB cannot derive ε because B cannot, so ε is excluded.

04-M25 · FOLLOW sets ★★★ E MCQ

Which marker is placed in FOLLOW of the grammar start symbol?

  1. ε
  2. Every nonterminal
  3. $
  4. The start symbol itself

Answer: C — $

$ represents end of input. FOLLOW contains terminals that can occur after a nonterminal, plus the end marker where appropriate. ε is not a FOLLOW symbol.

04-M26 · Predictive parsing ★★★ M MCQ

A grammar is LL(1) only if its predictive parse table has:

  1. No ε-productions anywhere
  2. Only left-recursive productions
  3. At most one production in each occupied cell
  4. Exactly two productions per cell

Answer: C — At most one production in each occupied cell

One lookahead token and the current nonterminal must determine the production uniquely. A conflicting cell means this grammar is not LL(1), even if another grammar for the language might be.

04-M27 · Parsing-table epsilon entries ★★ M MCQ

If A→ε is a production, where is it entered in an LL(1) table?

  1. In every column indiscriminately
  2. In columns belonging to FOLLOW(A)
  3. Only under an ε input column
  4. Only under nonterminal A

Answer: B — In columns belonging to FOLLOW(A)

Because ε consumes no token, the parser may use the production when the lookahead can legally follow A. There is no ordinary ε input token to consume.

04-M28 · Bottom-up parsing ★★ M MCQ

A shift-reduce parser constructs which derivation in reverse?

  1. A rightmost derivation
  2. No derivation
  3. A leftmost derivation always
  4. A random derivation unrelated to grammar

Answer: A — A rightmost derivation

A handle reduction replaces a substring corresponding to the most recent step of a rightmost derivation. Reversing the reduction sequence reveals that derivation.

04-M29 · LR items ★★ M MCQ

The LR item A→α·β means:

  1. β has already been recognized.
  2. α has been recognized and β remains to be recognized.
  3. A is necessarily the start symbol.
  4. The production has been deleted.

Answer: B — α has been recognized and β remains to be recognized.

The dot marks parser progress through the production. A dot at the end indicates a completed right-hand side that may permit reduction, subject to the parser’s lookahead rules.

04-M30 · Parsing conflicts ★★ M MCQ

A shift/reduce conflict means the parser table permits:

  1. Two lexical token names for one variable only
  2. Only two reductions, with no shift
  3. Both shifting the lookahead and reducing in the same situation
  4. No possible action anywhere

Answer: C — Both shifting the lookahead and reducing in the same situation

A shift/reduce conflict concerns competing action types. A reduce/reduce conflict is different: two reductions compete. Grammar rewriting or explicit precedence rules may resolve particular conflicts.

04-M31 · Compiler phases ★★★ E MCQ

Which phase groups characters into tokens such as identifiers and numbers?

  1. Code generation
  2. Lexical analysis
  3. Linking
  4. Register allocation

Answer: B — Lexical analysis

Lexical analysis recognizes token patterns in source characters. Parsing then checks how those tokens combine according to grammar. Symbol meaning and types are later semantic concerns.

04-M32 · Tokens and lexemes ★★★ E MCQ

In the source x = 125;, what is the lexeme of the numeric-literal token?

  1. 125
  2. The token’s memory address
  3. NUMBER
  4. x

Answer: A — 125

A token category might be NUMBER; its actual source substring is the lexeme 125. Keeping category and spelling distinct helps explain scanner output.

04-M33 · Lexical maximal munch ★★ M MCQ

A scanner supports tokens > and >= and uses longest-match scanning. For input >=, which token is returned first?

  1. The > token followed by =
  2. An identifier
  3. An empty token
  4. The >= token

Answer: D — The >= token

Maximal munch chooses the longest prefix matching a token pattern. Rule-order tie breaking matters only when candidate matches have equal length.

04-M34 · Identifier recognition ★★ E MCQ

Using the identifier rule [A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*, which string is a valid identifier?

  1. 2sum
  2. sum space
  3. _sum2
  4. sum-2

Answer: C — _sum2

The first character may be a letter or underscore; subsequent characters may additionally be digits. A hyphen or space is not allowed by this stated lexical rule.

04-M35 · Symbol tables ★★★ M MCQ

A compiler symbol table most directly stores:

  1. Bindings such as names, types and scope-related attributes
  2. Every pixel of the program editor
  3. Only final machine opcodes
  4. Only comments removed by the scanner

Answer: A — Bindings such as names, types and scope-related attributes

The compiler needs information about declarations and their uses. Scope-aware bindings permit resolving identifiers and checking types; the exact attributes depend on the language and phase.

04-M36 · Semantic analysis ★★★ E MCQ

Which error is primarily semantic rather than a failure to match expression grammar?

  1. Using an undeclared variable in an otherwise syntactically valid expression
  2. A missing required operand
  3. An illegal token character
  4. An unmatched closing parenthesis

Answer: A — Using an undeclared variable in an otherwise syntactically valid expression

An identifier token can fit grammar syntax without referring to a valid declaration. Name resolution and type rules belong to semantic analysis.

04-M37 · Type checking ★★ M MCQ

For a statically typed language forbidding string-plus-integer, x = "a" + 3 is rejected mainly by:

  1. Type checking
  2. Whitespace removal
  3. Register spilling
  4. Assembly linking only

Answer: A — Type checking

The token sequence and expression shape can be grammatical, but the operand types violate the operator rules. The stated language restriction matters because some languages define string concatenation with conversion.

04-M38 · Intermediate code ★★★ M MCQ

How many binary arithmetic operations are needed by straightforward three-address code for x = a + b*c?

  1. 3
  2. 2
  3. 4
  4. 1

Answer: B — 2

First compute t1=b*c, then t2=a+t1, then copy t2 to x. The copy is not a binary arithmetic operation. Operator precedence must be preserved.

04-M39 · Common subexpression elimination ★★ M MCQ

In t1=a+b; t2=a+b, with unchanged a,b and pure nontrapping arithmetic, which replacement is valid?

  1. Delete both assignments regardless of later uses
  2. t1=t2 before t2 exists
  3. t2=t1
  4. t2=a-b

Answer: C — t2=t1

The second expression has the same operands and semantics as the first. Reuse its value only when operands have not changed and evaluation side effects/exceptions do not invalidate the substitution.

04-M40 · Dead code ★★ M MCQ

A computed local value is never used, and its computation has no observable side effects or traps. Which optimization may remove it?

  1. Symbol-name interning only
  2. Lexical analysis
  3. Left factoring
  4. Dead-code elimination

Answer: D — Dead-code elimination

Removing an unobservable computation preserves the specified program behaviour. A seemingly unused call with I/O or possible exceptions is not automatically removable.

04-M41 · Constant folding ★★ E MCQ

Replacing 3*4 in source-independent intermediate code with 12 is an example of:

  1. Register spilling
  2. Constant folding
  3. Loop unrolling
  4. Syntax error recovery

Answer: B — Constant folding

The compiler evaluates a constant expression under the target language’s arithmetic rules. It does not require a runtime multiplication for that expression.

04-M42 · Liveness ★ M MCQ

A variable is live at a program point when:

  1. Its name begins with a capital letter
  2. It occupies the first register
  3. Its current value may be read on a future path before being overwritten.
  4. It was declared in the first source line

Answer: C — Its current value may be read on a future path before being overwritten.

Liveness is a backward data-flow property relevant to register allocation and dead stores. A variable can be in scope yet not live if its current value will never be used.

04-M43 · Register allocation ★ M MCQ

In a register-interference graph, an edge between two values means:

  1. Their live ranges overlap, so they cannot share one register at that overlap.
  2. They must always share a register
  3. They are equal constants
  4. They occur in unrelated source files

Answer: A — Their live ranges overlap, so they cannot share one register at that overlap.

Graph colouring models assigning different registers to simultaneously live values. If too few registers exist, some values may spill to memory.

04-M44 · Pushdown automata ★★★ E MCQ

A pushdown automaton adds which unbounded storage structure to finite control?

  1. A fixed two-bit counter only
  2. A read-only second alphabet
  3. A stack
  4. A finite transition table only

Answer: C — A stack

A stack can match nested structures and unbounded counts such as a^n b^n. General nondeterministic pushdown automata recognize the context-free languages.

04-M45 · Context-free limits ★★ M MCQ

Which language is not context-free?

  1. {a^n b^n c^n : n≥0}
  2. {a^n b^n : n≥0}
  3. a*b*c*
  4. All balanced parentheses

Answer: A — {a^n b^n c^n : n≥0}

Matching three independent unbounded block counts requires more than an ordinary single-stack context-free mechanism. The language is a standard non-context-free example, though it is decidable.

04-M46 · Turing machines ★★ M MCQ

Compared with a finite automaton, a standard Turing machine has:

  1. An oracle for every undecidable problem
  2. An unbounded read/write tape and movable tape head
  3. No transition function
  4. Only a larger fixed finite state list

Answer: B — An unbounded read/write tape and movable tape head

The unbounded tape provides a general memory mechanism. It does not grant a solution to all mathematically definable decision problems; undecidability still exists.

04-M47 · Decidability ★★ E MCQ

A decider for a language must:

  1. Halt on every input and answer membership correctly
  2. Halt only on accepted inputs
  3. Use constant memory
  4. Accept every string

Answer: A — Halt on every input and answer membership correctly

A recognizer may loop on nonmembers. A decider must provide a correct accept/reject result and terminate for every input. This is a termination requirement, not just recognition.

04-M48 · Halting problem ★★ M MCQ

The general halting problem for arbitrary programs and inputs is:

  1. Solved by faster hardware alone
  2. Regular
  3. Undecidable
  4. Decidable by testing every program for one minute

Answer: C — Undecidable

No algorithm can correctly decide termination for every arbitrary program-input pair. Bounded testing answers only bounded cases; it cannot conclude nontermination from a finite lack of progress.

04-M49 · Context-free closure ★ M MCQ

Context-free languages are closed under intersection with:

  1. Every decidable language
  2. A regular language
  3. Every recursively enumerable language
  4. Every context-free language

Answer: B — A regular language

A product construction can combine PDA control with DFA state. Arbitrary intersections of two context-free languages need not remain context-free.

04-M50 · Scope and environments ★★ M MCQ

In a lexically scoped language, an identifier use is resolved primarily using:

  1. The surrounding source-code declaration structure
  2. The current CPU clock rate
  3. The most recently called unrelated function
  4. The shortest variable name

Answer: A — The surrounding source-code declaration structure

Lexical scope follows program nesting, with nearer bindings shadowing outer ones under language rules. Dynamic scope would instead use the active call chain.

04-W01 · DFA construction ★★★ M WRITTEN

Construct and draw a complete DFA over {0,1} accepting exactly strings ending in 01. Provide a transition table, accepting states and traces for 101 and 010.

Worked answer

Stateon 0on 1Accept?
q0 (start)q1q0No
q1q1q2No
q2q1q0Yes

q0 has no useful suffix; q1 means the last symbol is 0; q2 means the last two symbols are 01. Trace 101: q0→q0→q1→q2, accepted. Trace 010: q0→q1→q2→q1, rejected. The empty string is rejected. Every state has a defined transition for both symbols.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

04-W02 · NFA subset construction ★★★ H WRITTEN

Convert this NFA for strings ending in 01 to a DFA. Start at q0; only q2 accepts. Show all reachable subsets and their transitions.

Worked answer

Let A={q0}, B={q0,q1}, C={q0,q2}. The NFA has no ε-transitions, so the initial subset is {q0}. A subset accepts if it contains q2.

DFA subseton 0on 1Accept?
A={q0}BANo
B={q0,q1}BCNo
C={q0,q2}BAYes

From B on 0, q0 contributes q0,q1 and q1 contributes nothing. From B on 1, q0 contributes q0 and q1 contributes q2. Only three subsets are reachable; the theoretical 2^3 upper bound does not mean all eight must appear. This DFA has the same shape as the previous three-state construction.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

04-W03 · DFA minimization ★★ H WRITTEN

Minimize the complete DFA described by this table. Start A; accepting states B and D. Draw the minimized machine.

State01
ACB
BDA
CAD
DBC

Worked answer

Initial partition separates nonfinal {A,C} from final {B,D}. A and C both go on 0 to the nonfinal block and on 1 to the final block. B and D both go on 0 to the final block and on 1 to the nonfinal block. No refinement splits either block.

The start block E is nonaccepting and the final block O accepts. Reading 0 preserves the block; reading 1 toggles it. The two states cannot merge because ε distinguishes their acceptance. Thus the machine recognizes an odd number of 1s.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

04-W04 · Regular-language nonmembership proof ★★ H WRITTEN

Use the pumping lemma to prove L={a^n b^n:n≥0} is not regular. Specify the quantifier-dependent choice of string and address every permitted decomposition.

Worked answer

Assume L is regular with pumping length p. Choose s=a^p b^p, which belongs to L and has length at least p. For any decomposition s=xyz with |xy|≤p and |y|>0, both x and y lie inside the first a block. Thus y=a^k for some k≥1.

Pump with i=0: xz=a^(p-k)b^p. The a count is smaller than the b count, so xz is not in L. This contradicts the lemma’s requirement that all pumping exponents preserve membership for an appropriate decomposition.

The proof treats every allowed decomposition because the length restriction forces y to consist only of a symbols. Choosing one convenient decomposition would not be sufficient.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

04-W05 · Grammar ambiguity and parse trees ★★★ M WRITTEN

For E→E+E | E*E | id, draw two parse trees for id+id*id and explain the ambiguity. Give an unambiguous precedence grammar with * higher than +.

Worked answer

One root operator is + with right child id*id, representing id+(id*id). The other root is * with left child id+id, representing (id+id)*id. They are distinct parse trees for the same string.

Grammar
E → E + T | T
T → T * F | F
F → id | ( E )

Separate nonterminals constrain multiplication to form a term before addition combines terms. The left recursion also represents left association of repeated operators; predictive parsing would require a suitable transformation.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

04-W06 · Left recursion and left factoring ★★★ M WRITTEN

Remove immediate left recursion from E→E+T | T. Separately left-factor S→abC | abD | aE. Explain why the two transformations solve different parsing issues.

Worked answer

Transformed grammars
E  → T E′
E′ → + T E′ | ε

S  → a X
X  → b Y | E
Y  → C | D

In the second grammar C, D and E are nonterminals; this structural factoring does not by itself prove LL(1), because their FIRST/FOLLOW sets may still conflict. Left-recursion removal stops a top-down routine from immediately calling itself without consuming input.

Left factoring postpones a choice until a shared prefix has been consumed. Neither operation alone proves that a language or grammar is unambiguous. Semantic actions may need adaptation when the tree structure changes.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

04-W07 · FIRST, FOLLOW and LL(1) ★★★ H WRITTEN

For S→AB, A→aA|ε, B→bB|c, compute FIRST and FOLLOW and construct the predictive table. State whether the grammar is LL(1).

Worked answer

NonterminalFIRSTFOLLOW
S{a,b,c}{$}
A{a,ε}{b,c}
B{b,c}{$}
abc$
SS→ABS→ABS→ABerror
AA→aAA→εA→εerror
BerrorB→bBB→cerror

A can disappear, so FIRST(S) includes FIRST(B) in addition to a. Since B follows A and is not nullable, FOLLOW(A)={b,c}. B ends the start production, giving FOLLOW(B)={$}. Every occupied table cell contains one production, so this grammar is LL(1). Its strings consist of zero or more a symbols, then zero or more b symbols, then a final c.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

04-W08 · Lexing and compiler pipeline ★★★ M WRITTEN

Tokenize total>=10 && count!=0 under conventional identifier/integer/operator rules. Draw a compiler-phase pipeline and describe the output of lexical, syntax and semantic analysis.

Worked answer

The tokens are ID(total), GE(>=), INT(10), AND(&&), ID(count), NE(!=), INT(0): seven tokens. Whitespace separates but is not a token in this rule set. Longest-match recognition keeps >= and != intact.

Lexical analysis returns token categories and attributes. Syntax analysis creates a parse tree or AST according to expression grammar. Semantic analysis resolves total/count and checks that comparisons and logical composition satisfy type rules. A grammatically valid undeclared name is a semantic error, not a tokenization success that guarantees a correct program.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

04-W09 · Three-address code and optimization ★★★ M WRITTEN

Translate x=(a+b)*(a+b)-c into three-address code, then eliminate the common subexpression. Assume a,b,c do not change and arithmetic is pure with no overflow/trap concerns. State why those assumptions matter.

Worked answer

Straightforward TAC
t1 = a + b
t2 = a + b
t3 = t1 * t2
t4 = t3 - c
x = t4
After common-subexpression elimination and copy simplification
t1 = a + b
t3 = t1 * t1
x = t3 - c

The repeated a+b has the same value because its operands do not change. Reusing t1 removes one addition. If evaluation could observe different mutable state, have side effects, or affect trapping behaviour, this reasoning could fail. General floating-point reassociation is not licensed merely by algebra over real numbers.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

04-W10 · Basic blocks and control-flow graphs ★★ M WRITTEN

Identify basic blocks and draw a CFG for this code. Edges represent possible control transfers.

TAC
1: i = 0
2: sum = 0
3: if i >= n goto 7
4: sum = sum + i
5: i = i + 1
6: goto 3
7: print sum

Worked answer

Leaders are line 1, target line 3, line 4 following the conditional jump, and target line 7. The blocks are B1={1,2}, B2={3}, B3={4,5,6}, B4={7}.

Edges: B1→B2, B2→B4 when i≥n, B2→B3 otherwise, B3→B2. A basic block is entered at its first instruction and has no internal transfer except at its end. The diagram preserves control flow rather than the physical line spacing of the source.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

04-W11 · Syntax-directed semantics ★★ M WRITTEN

For expression 2+3*4, give a precedence-respecting syntax tree and evaluate it bottom up. Distinguish the roles of parsing and evaluation.

Worked answer

The leaf values are 2,3,4. The multiplication node returns 3*4=12. The root addition returns 2+12=14. The tree groups the expression as 2+(3*4).

Parsing identifies the structure under the grammar and precedence rules. Evaluation then interprets operators on operand values. A different ambiguous parse (2+3)*4 would yield 20, illustrating why syntactic structure has semantic consequences. A compiler may fold this constant tree at compile time when the target arithmetic rules permit.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

04-W12 · CFG and PDA intuition ★★ M WRITTEN

Give a CFG for balanced parentheses and explain a stack algorithm for recognizing the language. Trace (()()) and state what causes rejection.

Worked answer

Grammar
S → ( S ) S | ε

Scan left to right. Push an opening parenthesis. On a closing parenthesis, reject if the stack is empty; otherwise pop one opening parenthesis. After all input, accept only if the stack is empty.

Prefix readStack depth
(1
((2
(()1
(()(2
(()()1
(()())0

The sample never needs to pop an empty stack and ends at depth zero, so it accepts. A prefix with excess closing parentheses rejects immediately; excess openings reject at the end. Time O(n), worst-case stack O(n).

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

04-W13 · Deciders and recognizers ★★ M WRITTEN

Distinguish a decider from a recognizer. Explain why simulating an arbitrary program for one million steps cannot decide its general halting behaviour.

Worked answer

A decider halts on every input and gives the correct yes/no membership answer. A recognizer must halt and accept members, but may reject or run forever on nonmembers. Decidable languages are therefore recognizable, but recognizability alone does not establish decidability.

If simulation halts within the bound, termination has been demonstrated for that input. If it does not, the program may halt at step 1,000,001 or much later, or never. A bounded simulation cannot distinguish those cases.

The general halting problem is undecidable; this does not prevent proving termination for specific programs or deciding restricted finite-state cases.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

04-W14 · Liveness and dead stores ★ M WRITTEN

Assuming ordinary local arithmetic with no traps and only the final print observable, identify removable assignments and give equivalent simplified code.

TAC
1: x = 2
2: x = 3
3: y = x + 4
4: z = y * 0
5: print y

Worked answer

Line 1 is a dead store: x is overwritten at line 2 before being read. Line 4 computes z that is never read and has no observable effect under the assumptions. Lines 2 and 3 can be constant-propagated.

Fully simplified code under the stated assumptions
print 7

A less aggressive equivalent form retains x=3; y=x+4; print y. Dead-code removal concerns uses and effects, not simply whether a variable was declared. If z computation called an observable function or could trap, deleting it would require additional analysis.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

04-W15 · Comparing machine models ★★ M WRITTEN

Compare DFA, NFA, nondeterministic PDA and Turing-machine recognizers by memory and language family. Give one example that separates finite automata from PDA and one that exceeds context-free recognition.

Worked answer

ModelMemory beyond finite controlFamily
DFANoneRegular
NFANone; nondeterministic choicesRegular
NPDAUnbounded stackContext-free
TM recognizerUnbounded read/write tapeRecognizable languages

{a^n b^n:n≥0} is context-free but not regular, separating PDA power from finite automata. {a^n b^n c^n:n≥0} is not context-free but is decidable by a Turing machine.

A Turing-machine recognizer can loop on nonmembers. Requiring every run to terminate with a membership decision gives the decidable languages, a proper subfamily of recognizable languages. Machine power and time complexity are separate comparisons.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

05 · Database

SQL examples use conventional SQL semantics unless a dialect is stated. Tables have only the rows shown; duplicate rows are possible unless keys prohibit them. NULL denotes unknown/missing, not zero. Normalization problems state functional dependencies explicitly, rather than inferring permanent rules from one sample table.

Coverage: Relational model and algebra; keys and dependencies; ER modelling; SQL joins, aggregates, subqueries and NULL; normalization and decomposition; indexes and query processing; transactions, serializability, recovery and isolation.

Reading references: R10, R11
