05-M01 · Relational keys ★★★ E MCQ

A candidate key is:

  1. A field that may identify some but not all rows
  2. A minimal set of attributes that uniquely determines each tuple
  3. Every superkey, including redundant attributes
  4. Any column containing a numeric value

Answer: B — A minimal set of attributes that uniquely determines each tuple

Minimal means no proper subset is still a superkey. A relation can have several candidate keys; choosing one as the primary key does not invalidate the others.

05-M02 · Primary and foreign keys ★★★ M MCQ

A foreign key normally enforces which condition for its non-null values?

  1. They cannot repeat across child rows.
  2. They must be unique within the referencing table.
  3. They must be consecutive integers.
  4. They match a referenced candidate/unique key value.

Answer: D — They match a referenced candidate/unique key value.

Many child rows can reference the same parent. Referential integrity prevents non-null references to nonexistent referenced values. Nullability and composite-key matching rules depend on the declared constraint.

05-M03 · Functional dependencies ★★★ M MCQ

What does A→B mean in a relation schema?

  1. Any two valid tuples agreeing on A must agree on B.
  2. A and B must each be single columns.
  3. A values can occur only once.
  4. B is numerically larger than A.

Answer: A — Any two valid tuples agreeing on A must agree on B.

A functional dependency is a constraint on all valid relation instances. It does not necessarily make A a key for the whole relation unless A determines every attribute.

05-M04 · Attribute closure ★★★ M MCQ

For R(A,B,C,D) with F={A→B, B→C}, what is A+?

  1. {A}
  2. {B,C}
  3. {A,B,C}
  4. {A,B,C,D}

Answer: C — {A,B,C}

Start with A, add B using A→B, then C using B→C. No dependency yields D, so D is not in the closure. A is not a key of this four-attribute schema.

05-M05 · Candidate keys ★★★ M MCQ

For R(A,B,C) and F={A→B, B→C}, which is the only candidate key?

  1. {A}
  2. {C}
  3. {A,B}
  4. {B}

Answer: A — {A}

A determines B and C, hence the full relation. B cannot determine A; C cannot determine either. AB is a superkey but not minimal, because A alone suffices.

05-M06 · Armstrong axioms ★★ M MCQ

From A→B and B→C, infer A→C using:

  1. Transitivity
  2. Symmetric reversal
  3. Statistical correlation
  4. Attribute deletion from every determinant

Answer: A — Transitivity

Transitivity is an Armstrong inference rule. Functional dependencies are not generally reversible; B→A cannot be inferred simply from A→B.

05-M07 · Relational algebra selection ★★★ E MCQ

Which relational-algebra operation filters tuples by a condition?

  1. Projection π
  2. Cartesian product ×
  3. Selection σ
  4. Attribute renaming ρ only

Answer: C — Selection σ

Selection retains rows satisfying a predicate. Projection chooses attributes/columns. Mixing these names often causes incorrect algebra expressions.

05-M08 · Relational algebra projection ★★★ E MCQ

Under classical set-based relational algebra, projection does what to duplicate resulting tuples?

  1. Removes duplicates
  2. Rejects the query
  3. Always preserves all duplicates
  4. Adds a row count column

Answer: A — Removes duplicates

Relations in classical algebra are sets. SQL SELECT without DISTINCT often uses bag semantics instead, so an SQL projection may retain duplicates. State the semantic model.

05-M09 · Union compatibility ★★ M MCQ

For the union of two relations in basic relational algebra, they must have:

  1. Compatible attribute counts and corresponding domains
  2. Disjoint values only
  3. The same number of tuples
  4. A common foreign key

Answer: A — Compatible attribute counts and corresponding domains

Union combines tuples of the same schema shape. Tuple counts need not match. Names can be aligned by renaming under the notation used.

05-M10 · SQL aggregates ★★★ E MCQ

For this column, what are COUNT(*) and COUNT(score)?

score
10
NULL
20
  1. 2 and 3
  2. 3 and 3
  3. 2 and 2
  4. 3 and 2

Answer: D — 3 and 2

COUNT(*) counts rows, including the NULL row. COUNT(score) counts non-null values of that expression, giving two.

05-M11 · SQL aggregates ★★★ M MCQ

What is AVG(score) for the shown rows?

score
10
NULL
20
  1. 30
  2. NULL
  3. 15
  4. 10

Answer: C — 15

AVG ignores null inputs rather than treating them as zero. The average of the two non-null values is (10+20)/2=15.

05-M12 · NULL and three-valued logic ★★★ M MCQ

In SQL, the comparison NULL = NULL evaluates to:

  1. UNKNOWN
  2. A syntax error
  3. FALSE in every context
  4. TRUE

Answer: A — UNKNOWN

An ordinary comparison involving NULL produces UNKNOWN. Use IS NULL to test nullness. WHERE retains only TRUE rows, not FALSE or UNKNOWN.

05-M13 · NULL tests ★★★ E MCQ

Which predicate selects rows whose salary is missing?

  1. salary IS NULL
  2. salary = 0
  3. salary = NULL
  4. salary == NULL

Answer: A — salary IS NULL

IS NULL is the dedicated null predicate. A zero salary is a known value and not the same as a missing value. The equality comparison with NULL does not evaluate TRUE.

05-M14 · WHERE and HAVING ★★★ E MCQ

Which clause filters groups after aggregation?

  1. ORDER BY
  2. FROM
  3. HAVING
  4. WHERE

Answer: C — HAVING

WHERE filters input rows before grouping. HAVING can filter group results, such as HAVING COUNT(*) >= 3. ORDER BY changes output ordering, not group membership.

05-M15 · GROUP BY ★★★ M MCQ

Which query returns each department and its employee count?

  1. SELECT COUNT(dept) FROM Employee ORDER BY dept;
  2. SELECT dept, COUNT(*) FROM Employee;
  3. SELECT dept FROM Employee HAVING COUNT(*) > 0;
  4. SELECT dept, COUNT(*) FROM Employee GROUP BY dept;

Answer: D — SELECT dept, COUNT(*) FROM Employee GROUP BY dept;

Grouping by dept forms one group per department and evaluates COUNT(*) in each group. The other queries do not provide the same grouped result under conventional SQL rules.

05-M16 · INNER JOIN multiplicity ★★★ M MCQ

Table A has two rows with key k=1 and B has three rows with k=1. An inner equijoin on k produces how many matching rows for k=1?

  1. 2
  2. 6
  3. 5
  4. 3

Answer: B — 6

Every matching A row pairs with every matching B row: 2*3=6. SQL join multiplicity depends on actual constraints; repeated keys are explicitly allowed here.

05-M17 · LEFT JOIN ★★★ E MCQ

A LEFT JOIN has no matching right-side row for one left-side tuple. What happens?

  1. The query fails.
  2. The left tuple disappears.
  3. The left tuple appears with NULLs for right-side columns.
  4. An all-zero right tuple is inserted permanently.

Answer: C — The left tuple appears with NULLs for right-side columns.

A left outer join preserves unmatched left rows using null-extended result columns. It does not create an actual row in the right-hand base table.

05-M18 · Outer-join filtering ★★ M MCQ

After L LEFT JOIN R ON L.id=R.id, adding WHERE R.status='active' generally does what to unmatched L rows?

  1. Guarantees every L row matches.
  2. Always preserves them.
  3. Converts right NULLs to active.
  4. Removes them because the predicate is UNKNOWN.

Answer: D — Removes them because the predicate is UNKNOWN.

For unmatched rows R.status is NULL, so the equality is UNKNOWN and WHERE removes the row. Place a right-side restriction in ON when the intention is to preserve all left rows.

05-M19 · NOT IN and NULL ★★★ M MCQ

For non-null value 5, what is the truth value of 5 NOT IN (1,NULL)?

  1. TRUE
  2. UNKNOWN
  3. FALSE because 5 equals 1
  4. A syntax error

Answer: B — UNKNOWN

5 is unequal to 1, but the comparison with the unknown value is unknown. Negating the IN result remains UNKNOWN. A WHERE clause therefore does not select the row.

05-M20 · Correlated subqueries ★★ M MCQ

Which is a correlated subquery?

  1. Any query using COUNT
  2. A query containing only constants
  3. An inner query that references a column from its outer query
  4. Every query returning one row

Answer: C — An inner query that references a column from its outer query

The inner expression depends on the current outer binding. An optimizer may transform its execution, so correlation does not force one particular physical evaluation strategy.

05-M21 · EXISTS ★★ E MCQ

EXISTS(subquery) is TRUE when:

  1. The subquery produces at least one row
  2. The selected number equals 1
  3. Its first selected value is non-null
  4. The subquery produces exactly one row

Answer: A — The subquery produces at least one row

EXISTS tests row existence, not the selected value. A returned row containing NULL still makes EXISTS true.

05-M22 · SQL set operations ★★ M MCQ

What is the usual difference between UNION and UNION ALL?

  1. UNION ALL requires disjoint inputs.
  2. UNION removes duplicate result rows; UNION ALL retains them.
  3. UNION ALL sorts automatically.
  4. UNION keeps only common rows.

Answer: B — UNION removes duplicate result rows; UNION ALL retains them.

UNION implements duplicate elimination for compatible result rows. UNION ALL concatenates bags and avoids that elimination step. Neither promises a display order without ORDER BY.

05-M23 · SQL ordering ★★ M MCQ

Without ORDER BY, which result-order assumption is portable?

  1. Rows are always random by specification.
  2. No particular order is guaranteed.
  3. Rows always follow insertion order.
  4. Rows always follow primary-key order.

Answer: B — No particular order is guaranteed.

The implementation may choose different access plans that return the same logical result in different orders. No particular ordering contract exists without an appropriate ORDER BY.

05-M24 · First normal form ★★★ M MCQ

In the conventional relational teaching model, first normal form requires:

  1. The table has exactly one candidate key.
  2. Every nonkey attribute depends on every other.
  3. Every determinant is a superkey.
  4. Attributes contain atomic values from their declared domains.

Answer: D — Attributes contain atomic values from their declared domains.

First normal form excludes repeating groups in the usual relational presentation. The meaning of atomic is relative to the model/domain; it does not by itself establish 2NF, 3NF or BCNF.

05-M25 · Second normal form ★★★ M MCQ

A 1NF relation violates 2NF when:

  1. It has more than three columns
  2. A nonprime attribute depends on a proper subset of a candidate key
  3. Its candidate key is a single attribute
  4. It contains a foreign key

Answer: B — A nonprime attribute depends on a proper subset of a candidate key

Second normal form removes partial dependencies of nonprime attributes on candidate keys. A nonprime attribute belongs to no candidate key. Composite keys make such violations possible.

05-M26 · Third normal form ★★★ M MCQ

For a nontrivial dependency X→A, 3NF permits it when:

  1. Every row has no NULL values
  2. X is any single attribute
  3. A is always nonprime
  4. X is a superkey or A is a prime attribute

Answer: D — X is a superkey or A is a prime attribute

The prime-attribute exception distinguishes 3NF from BCNF. BCNF instead requires the determinant to be a superkey for every nontrivial functional dependency.

05-M27 · BCNF ★★★ M MCQ

R(A,B,C) has F={A→B,B→C}, with A the only candidate key. Which dependency violates BCNF?

  1. B→C
  2. AB→C
  3. A→B
  4. A→A

Answer: A — B→C

B is not a superkey because it does not determine A. B→C is nontrivial, so it violates BCNF. Dependencies with superkey determinants do not cause this violation.

05-M28 · 3NF versus BCNF ★★ M MCQ

R(A,B,C) has F={AB→C,C→B}. Candidate keys are AB and AC. Which statement is correct?

  1. It is in 3NF but not BCNF.
  2. C is a candidate key.
  3. It cannot be in 1NF.
  4. It is in BCNF.

Answer: A — It is in 3NF but not BCNF.

C→B violates BCNF because C does not determine A. But B is prime because it belongs to key AB, satisfying the 3NF exception. Both attributes B and C are prime across the two keys.

05-M29 · Lossless decomposition ★★★ M MCQ

R(A,B,C) with A→B is decomposed into AB and AC. Is the decomposition lossless under the dependency?

  1. No, every decomposition is lossy.
  2. Yes only if B=C.
  3. Yes, because common attribute A determines AB.
  4. No because C is not a key.

Answer: C — Yes, because common attribute A determines AB.

For a binary decomposition, losslessness holds when the common attributes determine one component. The intersection is A and A→AB follows from A→B and reflexivity.

05-M30 · Dependency preservation ★★ M MCQ

A dependency-preserving decomposition allows:

  1. Dropping all constraints
  2. Guaranteeing every component is BCNF
  3. Checking the original dependencies using constraints within component relations without joining them
  4. Recovering rows without any join keys

Answer: C — Checking the original dependencies using constraints within component relations without joining them

Dependency preservation concerns enforcing the original rules locally across the projected dependency sets. It is a different property from lossless reconstruction.

05-M31 · Fourth normal form ★ M MCQ

Fourth normal form primarily addresses:

  1. Only duplicate primary-key values
  2. Only two-valued logic
  3. Only misspelled SQL keywords
  4. Nontrivial multivalued dependencies whose determinants are not superkeys

Answer: D — Nontrivial multivalued dependencies whose determinants are not superkeys

Independent multivalued facts can create redundant cross products even without problematic ordinary functional dependencies. 4NF imposes a superkey condition on nontrivial multivalued dependencies.

05-M32 · ER cardinality ★★★ E MCQ

A student can enrol in many courses and a course can contain many students. The relationship is:

  1. Many-to-one from course to student only
  2. An attribute with no relationship
  3. One-to-one
  4. Many-to-many

Answer: D — Many-to-many

A linking relation such as Enrollment(StudentID,CourseID,...) normally represents the relationship. Relationship attributes like grade belong naturally there.

05-M33 · Weak entities ★★ M MCQ

In ER modelling, a weak entity typically requires:

  1. No participation in any relationship
  2. A globally unique standalone key by definition
  3. No identifier of any kind
  4. An owner key combined with its partial key for identification

Answer: D — An owner key combined with its partial key for identification

A dependent entity can be identified only in the context of its owner, such as an order line identified by OrderID and LineNumber. Its existence/identifying relation is modeled accordingly.

05-M34 · B+ tree indexes ★★★ M MCQ

Why are B+ trees well suited to range queries?

  1. Ordered leaf entries and linked leaves support sequential range traversal.
  2. They hash adjacent keys to adjacent buckets invariably.
  3. Every range query reads only one page.
  4. They store no keys.

Answer: A — Ordered leaf entries and linked leaves support sequential range traversal.

A search locates the first qualifying leaf entry, then follows ordered leaves through the range. The cost includes the number of leaf/data pages needed; not every range fits in one page.

05-M35 · Hash indexes ★★ M MCQ

Which lookup is a basic hash index primarily designed to support efficiently?

  1. Every join condition with no key information
  2. Equality on the indexed key
  3. All ordered range scans without extra structure
  4. ORDER BY on any column

Answer: B — Equality on the indexed key

Hashing maps a search key to a bucket. It does not maintain global key order, so range queries generally need other structures or additional scanning.

05-M36 · Index tradeoffs ★★ M MCQ

Adding an index to a frequently updated table can:

  1. Guarantee zero locking
  2. Remove the need for constraints
  3. Improve some reads while adding maintenance cost to writes
  4. Make all operations faster with no extra storage

Answer: C — Improve some reads while adding maintenance cost to writes

The extra structure occupies storage and must reflect inserted, deleted or changed indexed values. Index selection depends on the workload and selectivity.

05-M37 · Query optimization ★★ M MCQ

For an inner join, pushing a filter on only one input below the join is useful mainly because it can:

  1. Change the required query answer intentionally
  2. Remove all duplicates automatically
  3. Reduce the number of tuples participating in the join
  4. Guarantee a constant-time query

Answer: C — Reduce the number of tuples participating in the join

Under valid relational equivalences, early filtering preserves the result while reducing intermediate work. Outer joins and null semantics require additional care.

05-M38 · ACID ★★★ E MCQ

Which ACID property makes a transaction appear all-or-nothing?

  1. Atomicity
  2. Consistency alone
  3. Durability
  4. Isolation

Answer: A — Atomicity

Atomicity requires partial effects not to remain as a committed partial transaction. Isolation concerns interaction with concurrent work; durability concerns surviving a committed result.

05-M39 · ACID ★★★ E MCQ

Which property concerns persistence of a successfully committed transaction despite a subsequent crash, under the system’s durability guarantees?

  1. Durability
  2. Atomicity
  3. Normalization
  4. Foreign-key cardinality

Answer: A — Durability

Durability is implemented using persistent recovery mechanisms such as logs and storage protocols. A mere response in volatile memory would not establish the promised persistence.

05-M40 · Conflicting operations ★★★ M MCQ

Which operations conflict when issued by different transactions on the same item X?

  1. r1(X) and a computation using no database item
  2. r1(X) and w2(X)
  3. r1(X) and w2(Y), X≠Y
  4. r1(X) and r2(X)

Answer: B — r1(X) and w2(X)

Different transactions conflict on the same item when at least one operation is a write. Two reads alone do not constrain conflict-equivalent serial ordering.

05-M41 · Conflict serializability ★★★ M MCQ

A schedule is conflict serializable exactly when its precedence graph is:

  1. Complete
  2. Undirected
  3. Connected with a cycle
  4. Acyclic

Answer: D — Acyclic

An acyclic precedence graph admits a topological order of transactions preserving every conflict order. A directed cycle makes such a serial order impossible.

05-M42 · Two-phase locking ★★★ M MCQ

Basic two-phase locking requires:

  1. No possibility of deadlock
  2. No new lock acquisitions after the first lock release
  3. Only shared locks for all writes
  4. Releasing every lock immediately after each read

Answer: B — No new lock acquisitions after the first lock release

A growing phase acquires locks; a shrinking phase releases them. Standard 2PL ensures conflict serializability when properly followed, but can still produce deadlocks.

05-M43 · Strict schedules ★★ M MCQ

A strict schedule prevents another transaction from reading or writing X until:

  1. The table is sorted
  2. Any unrelated transaction commits
  3. The transaction that last wrote X commits or aborts
  4. The first transaction merely finishes its CPU burst

Answer: C — The transaction that last wrote X commits or aborts

Keeping uncommitted writes inaccessible simplifies recovery and prevents cascading effects. Under strict 2PL, exclusive locks are retained until commit/abort.

05-M44 · Dirty reads ★★★ E MCQ

A dirty read occurs when:

  1. A query reads a disk page instead of cache
  2. Two transactions read the same committed value
  3. A column contains NULL
  4. A transaction reads another transaction’s uncommitted write

Answer: D — A transaction reads another transaction’s uncommitted write

The value may later be rolled back, leaving the reader dependent on an effect that never committed. Isolation/recovery rules determine whether such reads are allowed.

05-M45 · Lost updates ★★★ M MCQ

Two transactions read balance 100; each independently adds 10 and writes 110. The final balance is 110. What anomaly is illustrated?

  1. Phantom row only
  2. Referential integrity failure necessarily
  3. Lost update
  4. A correct serial increment result

Answer: C — Lost update

One update overwrites the other because both used the old balance. A correct serial execution of both increments would produce 120. Atomic database updates or appropriate concurrency control prevent this pattern.

05-M46 · Write-ahead logging ★★ M MCQ

A key write-ahead logging rule is:

  1. Checkpoints remove all recovery requirements.
  2. Data pages must always be written before log records.
  3. Logs are needed only for SELECT.
  4. Relevant log records reach stable storage before the modified data page is flushed.

Answer: D — Relevant log records reach stable storage before the modified data page is flushed.

The log must be available to recover or undo changes whose pages may already be persistent. Commit and checkpoint details vary by recovery design.

05-M47 · Recoverable schedules ★★ M MCQ

If T2 reads a value written by T1, recoverability requires:

  1. All transactions use the same SQL query.
  2. T2 must not commit before T1 commits.
  3. T2 must always abort.
  4. T1 must never commit.

Answer: B — T2 must not commit before T1 commits.

If T2 committed first and T1 later aborted, a committed result would depend on invalid data. Recoverability restricts commit ordering; cascadeless and strict schedules impose stronger conditions.

05-M48 · SQL constraints ★★ M MCQ

A CHECK(amount>0) constraint by itself usually rejects:

  1. All rows where amount is NULL automatically
  2. Rows where amount>0 is FALSE
  3. All rows where amount>0 is UNKNOWN
  4. Every duplicate amount

Answer: B — Rows where amount>0 is FALSE

Conventional SQL CHECK accepts TRUE or UNKNOWN and rejects FALSE. A separate NOT NULL constraint is needed when null amounts must also be forbidden.

05-M49 · Transactions and isolation ★ M MCQ

Under snapshot isolation, why can write skew still occur?

  1. Every pair of writes to the same row is ignored.
  2. Transactions may write different rows after reading a shared invariant from old snapshots.
  3. Snapshot isolation always reads uncommitted writes.
  4. Snapshots prohibit all concurrency.

Answer: B — Transactions may write different rows after reading a shared invariant from old snapshots.

Preventing direct write-write conflicts does not necessarily serialize decisions based on related rows. Two individually valid-looking updates can jointly violate a cross-row invariant.

05-M50 · Distributed commit ★ M MCQ

In two-phase commit, a participant votes YES during prepare primarily when it can:

  1. Commit later if instructed, after durably recording the prepared state
  2. Guarantee the coordinator never fails
  3. Ignore the final decision
  4. Discard all transaction information immediately

Answer: A — Commit later if instructed, after durably recording the prepared state

A prepared participant promises to follow the coordinated commit/abort outcome. It may block awaiting a decision after failures; atomic distributed commit is not the same as nonblocking consensus in every failure model.

05-W01 · SQL joins and aggregation ★★★ M WRITTEN

Using the tables, write SQL returning every department and its employee count, including zero. Show the result ordered by DeptID.

DeptIDDeptName
1CSE
2EEE
3Math

Department

EmpIDDeptID
101
111
122

Employee

Worked answer

SQL
SELECT d.DeptID, d.DeptName, COUNT(e.EmpID) AS n
FROM Department AS d
LEFT JOIN Employee AS e ON e.DeptID = d.DeptID
GROUP BY d.DeptID, d.DeptName
ORDER BY d.DeptID;
DeptIDDeptNamen
1CSE2
2EEE1
3Math0

Assume EmpID is non-null. Count e.EmpID rather than COUNT(*): the outer join creates one null-extended row for Math, which COUNT(*) would count as one. Grouping both displayed department columns avoids dialect-dependent reliance on functional-dependency shortcuts.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

05-W02 · SQL grouped filtering ★★★ M WRITTEN

For Sales below, return customers whose total amount exceeds 100, with their total in descending order. Show the result.

CustomerAmount
A60
A50
B80
C70
C40

Worked answer

SQL
SELECT Customer, SUM(Amount) AS total
FROM Sales
GROUP BY Customer
HAVING SUM(Amount) > 100
ORDER BY total DESC, Customer ASC;
Customertotal
A110
C110

WHERE Amount>100 would discard every input row and is not equivalent: the condition concerns the group total, not individual amounts. The secondary ORDER BY establishes a deterministic tie order for the two equal totals. B is excluded because its group sum is only 80.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

05-W03 · SQL anti-joins and NULL ★★★ M WRITTEN

Find products with no inventory row. Product.ProductID is a primary key; Inventory.ProductID is nullable. Write a robust query, then explain the danger of NOT IN.

ProductID
1
2
3

Product

ProductID
1
NULL

Inventory

Worked answer

SQL
SELECT p.ProductID
FROM Product AS p
WHERE NOT EXISTS (
    SELECT 1
    FROM Inventory AS i
    WHERE i.ProductID = p.ProductID
)
ORDER BY p.ProductID;

The result is 2 and 3. For those values, no inventory row has a TRUE equality match. The NULL row does not match either product.

ProductID NOT IN (SELECT ProductID FROM Inventory) includes a NULL comparison and yields UNKNOWN for unmatched non-null values, so WHERE would discard them. Filtering NULLs inside the subquery is another repair, but NOT EXISTS states the absence-of-match intention clearly.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

05-W04 · Keys and attribute closure ★★★ M WRITTEN

For R(A,B,C,D,E) with F={A→BC,C→D,D→E}, calculate A+, identify all candidate keys, and explain why BC is not a key.

Worked answer

Start A+={A}. A→BC adds B and C. C→D adds D; D→E adds E. Thus A+={A,B,C,D,E}, so A is a superkey and is minimal.

No dependency has A on its right-hand side, so every key must contain A. Since A alone determines everything, it is the only candidate key. Any strict superset of A is a nonminimal superkey.

(BC)+={B,C,D,E}; it cannot produce A. Therefore BC is not a key. Deriving many attributes does not establish a key until the closure contains every attribute of the relation.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

05-W05 · Normalization to BCNF ★★★ H WRITTEN

R(StudentID,StudentName,DeptID,DeptName) has StudentID→StudentName,DeptID and DeptID→DeptName. Identify the key and redundancy, then produce a lossless dependency-preserving BCNF decomposition.

Worked answer

StudentID determines every attribute and is the candidate key. DeptID→DeptName is a transitive dependency through the key and violates BCNF because DeptID does not determine StudentID.

RelationPrimary keyNontrivial rule
Student(StudentID,StudentName,DeptID)StudentIDStudentID→StudentName,DeptID
Department(DeptID,DeptName)DeptIDDeptID→DeptName

The intersection is DeptID, which determines the entire Department relation, proving this binary decomposition lossless. Both original rules are enforced in individual components, so it is dependency preserving. Each component’s stated nontrivial determinant is a key, giving BCNF. Repeated department names no longer need updating in every student row.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

05-W06 · 3NF versus BCNF tradeoff ★★ H WRITTEN

For R(A,B,C), F={AB→C,C→B}, show candidate keys and explain 3NF versus BCNF. Decompose on C→B and discuss dependency preservation.

Worked answer

AB determines C and is a key. AC determines B through C→B and is also a key. Neither A, B nor C alone determines everything. All three attributes are prime because each appears in a candidate key.

C→B violates BCNF: C is not a superkey. It satisfies 3NF because B is prime. Decompose into R1(C,B) and R2(A,C). Their intersection C determines R1, so the decomposition is lossless.

The dependency AB→C is not enforceable from the projected component dependencies alone: there is no rule deriving C from AB in their union. Thus this BCNF decomposition sacrifices dependency preservation. Losslessness and preservation must be checked separately.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

05-W07 · ER diagram and relational mapping ★★★ M WRITTEN

Model students enrolling in courses. A student may take many courses and a course may have many students. Each enrolment has a grade. Draw an ER-style schema and map it to relations with keys and foreign keys.

Worked answer

Relational schema
Student(StudentID PRIMARY KEY, Name)
Course(CourseID PRIMARY KEY, Title)
Enrollment(
    StudentID REFERENCES Student,
    CourseID REFERENCES Course,
    Grade,
    PRIMARY KEY (StudentID, CourseID)
)

This assumes a student takes a particular course at most once. If repeats across terms are allowed, add Term to the enrolment identity or introduce a course-offering entity. Grade depends on the enrolment, not solely the student or course. Parent rows may have zero enrolments unless minimum-participation constraints state otherwise.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

05-W08 · Relational algebra and SQL ★★ M WRITTEN

Given Student(SID,Name) and Enroll(SID,CID), express the names of students enrolled in course C1 using relational algebra and SQL. Explain duplicate handling.

Worked answer

Set relational algebra: π_Name(Student ⋈_(Student.SID=Enroll.SID) σ_(CID='C1')(Enroll)). The selection can be applied before the join because its predicate uses only Enroll attributes.

SQL
SELECT DISTINCT s.Name
FROM Student AS s
JOIN Enroll AS e ON e.SID = s.SID
WHERE e.CID = 'C1';

DISTINCT matches set projection on names. Two different students may share a name, so this query intentionally returns unique name values, not necessarily one row per student. Include SID when student identity rather than a name set is desired. This distinction prevents silently changing the question’s output meaning.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

05-W09 · Conflict-serializability graph ★★★ M WRITTEN

For schedule r1(X), w1(X), r2(X), w2(Y), w1(Y), draw the precedence graph and decide conflict serializability. Assume both transactions complete afterward.

Worked answer

w1(X) precedes r2(X), creating T1→T2. w2(Y) precedes w1(Y), creating T2→T1. These are same-item conflicts across different transactions with at least one write.

Because the graph is cyclic, no serial ordering preserves every conflict order; the schedule is not conflict serializable. Commit timing is not needed for this particular graph test. Recoverability is a separate property that would require commit/read-from analysis, not just the absence or presence of this cycle.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

05-W10 · Concurrency and lost updates ★★★ M WRITTEN

Two transactions each add 10 to an account balance initially 100 using separate reads and writes. Give a lost-update schedule, explain the incorrect final value, and provide a safe implementation approach.

Worked answer

StepT1T2
1read b1=100
2read b2=100
3write 110
4write 110

The final balance is 110 although the combined intended effect is +20, giving 120. Both computations used the same old value.

SQL for each increment
BEGIN;
UPDATE Account
SET Balance = Balance + 10
WHERE AccountID = 7;
COMMIT;

Use the database’s atomic update and appropriate transactional locking/serialization guarantees rather than read-modify-write in unprotected application memory. A read-for-update lock held until the transaction ends is another pattern where supported. The exact isolation requirements depend on the whole invariant and DBMS.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

05-W11 · Recovery and write-ahead logging ★★ M WRITTEN

Explain write-ahead logging using a transaction changing X from 10 to 20. Discuss a crash before commit and a crash after a durable commit record, under a recovery design supporting undo/redo.

Worked answer

A log record records the transaction and change information needed for recovery. Before a modified data page can become durable, its relevant log record must already be durable. A commit response is tied to the system’s durable commit protocol.

Crash pointRecovery intent
Before commitUndo uncommitted effects that reached data pages
After durable commitRedo committed effects missing from data pages

This describes a conventional undo/redo design; the exact log format and recovery phases vary. A checkpoint reduces the amount of history scanned but does not make all logging unnecessary. The ordering matters because persistent data without recovery information could leave an unrecoverable partial transaction.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

05-W12 · Index capacity and search cost ★★ M WRITTEN

A B+ tree index has leaf capacity 100 entries and internal fanout 100. Assume every page is full except possibly the last at each level, 1,000,000 entries, and no cached pages. Find the number of leaf pages, internal pages by level, and root-to-leaf page reads.

Worked answer

Leaf pages=ceil(1,000,000/100)=10,000. Their parents require ceil(10,000/100)=100 pages. The root needs ceil(100/100)=1 page. Total index pages=10,000+100+1=10,101.

An index descent reads root, one internal page and one leaf: three page reads. Fetching a separate heap data page may add another read; the question asks only index descent. Real occupancy, key size, page overhead and caching change practical counts, so the given capacities must not be replaced with unstated defaults.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

05-W13 · Query optimization with assumptions ★★ M WRITTEN

Explain why σ_(Dept='CSE')(Employee ⋈ Department) can often filter Employee before an inner join when Dept belongs only to Employee. Give the equivalent expression and discuss why outer joins require caution.

Worked answer

Equivalent expression: (σ_(Employee.Dept='CSE')(Employee)) ⋈ Department, with the original join condition retained. The predicate depends only on Employee, so rejecting an Employee row before pairing cannot remove any required qualifying join row.

The benefit is smaller intermediate input: if Employee has 100,000 rows but only 2,000 qualify, fewer tuples may enter the join. This does not guarantee a particular speedup; indexes, join method and data distribution matter.

Outer joins create null-extended rows. Moving a predicate between WHERE and ON or below an outer join can change which unmatched rows survive. Prove the relevant equivalence under null and outer-join semantics rather than blindly applying an inner-join rule.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

05-W14 · Relational division and all conditions ★★ H WRITTEN

Write SQL to find students enrolled in every course listed in RequiredCourse(CID), using Student(SID,Name) and Enroll(SID,CID). Explain what happens when RequiredCourse is empty.

Worked answer

SQL
SELECT s.SID, s.Name
FROM Student AS s
WHERE NOT EXISTS (
    SELECT 1 FROM RequiredCourse AS r
    WHERE NOT EXISTS (
        SELECT 1 FROM Enroll AS e
        WHERE e.SID = s.SID AND e.CID = r.CID
    )
);

The inner NOT EXISTS identifies a required course missing for the current student. The outer NOT EXISTS requires there to be no such missing course. This is the logical pattern “for every requirement, a matching enrolment exists.” Assume the relevant identifiers are non-null.

If RequiredCourse is empty, no missing required course exists, so every student qualifies by vacuous truth. If a business rule wants no students in that case, it must add a separate nonempty-requirement condition.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

05-W15 · Integrity and schema constraints ★★ M WRITTEN

Design SQL-style constraints for an OrderLine identified by (OrderID,LineNo), with an existing parent Order, strictly positive non-null Quantity, and nonnegative non-null UnitPrice. Explain why CHECK alone is insufficient for missing values.

Worked answer

SQL
CREATE TABLE OrderLine (
    OrderID INTEGER NOT NULL,
    LineNo INTEGER NOT NULL,
    Quantity INTEGER NOT NULL CHECK (Quantity > 0),
    UnitPrice DECIMAL(12,2) NOT NULL CHECK (UnitPrice >= 0),
    PRIMARY KEY (OrderID, LineNo),
    FOREIGN KEY (OrderID) REFERENCES Orders(OrderID)
);

The composite primary key prevents duplicate line numbers within an order while permitting line 1 in many different orders. The foreign key rejects nonexistent parent orders, subject to transaction/constraint timing.

Conventional CHECK semantics reject FALSE but accept UNKNOWN. Since a comparison involving NULL is UNKNOWN, Quantity>0 alone would not prohibit NULL. Explicit NOT NULL expresses that additional requirement. Domain sizes and deletion policies would be selected separately for the application.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

06 · Computer Network & Security

Networking calculations explicitly separate bits/bytes, transmission/propagation delay and decimal/binary units. Protocol traces state simplified assumptions. Cryptographic toy calculations illustrate mathematics only and are not secure parameter recommendations. Security exercises focus on authorized defensive analysis.

Coverage: Layering and application protocols; IPv4/IPv6, subnetting and forwarding; link access, errors, reliability and TCP; delay/throughput; confidentiality, authentication, cryptography, access control and common defensive controls.

Reading references: R12, R13, R14

06-M01 · Network layers ★★★ E MCQ

Which Internet-layer function is primarily associated with IP?

  1. Mapping domain names to addresses
  2. Best-effort packet delivery across interconnected networks
  3. Encrypting every application message
  4. Reliable ordered byte-stream delivery

Answer: B — Best-effort packet delivery across interconnected networks

IP handles addressing and packet forwarding without guaranteeing delivery, order or retransmission. TCP supplies a reliable byte-stream service above IP when used.

06-M02 · TCP and UDP ★★★ E MCQ

Which transport service does TCP provide to applications?

  1. Broadcast delivery to every LAN host
  2. Guaranteed bounded latency for every message
  3. An application-visible preservation of every write boundary
  4. A reliable ordered byte stream

Answer: D — A reliable ordered byte stream

TCP presents ordered bytes with retransmission/reassembly mechanisms. It does not preserve application write boundaries or guarantee a latency bound. Applications need their own message framing.

06-M03 · Application protocols ★★ E MCQ

Which application protocol resolves domain names using resource records?

  1. DNS
  2. ARP
  3. ICMP
  4. Ethernet

Answer: A — DNS

DNS provides a distributed naming system with records such as address and mail-routing data. ARP maps IPv4 next-hop addresses to link-layer addresses on a local link.

06-M04 · HTTP semantics ★★ M MCQ

An HTTP response status in the 4xx class generally indicates:

  1. A DNS server failure code
  2. A transport retransmission count
  3. A client-error class of response
  4. Successful completion in every case

Answer: C — A client-error class of response

HTTP status classes distinguish response categories. A 4xx result is application-protocol information, not a TCP-level signal that a packet was lost.

06-M05 · Address resolution ★★★ M MCQ

For an IPv4 packet sent through a local default gateway, which MAC address normally appears as the local Ethernet destination?

  1. The gateway interface MAC address
  2. The sender MAC address
  3. A MAC derived by hashing the destination IP
  4. The remote Internet server MAC address

Answer: A — The gateway interface MAC address

The frame crosses only the local link to the next hop. The IP destination can remain the remote host while the link-layer destination changes at each routed hop.

06-M06 · IPv4 subnetting ★★★ M MCQ

What is the network address of 192.168.10.77/26?

  1. 192.168.10.128
  2. 192.168.10.0
  3. 192.168.10.77
  4. 192.168.10.64

Answer: D — 192.168.10.64

A /26 has blocks of 64 addresses. The last-octet value 77 lies in block 64..127, so its network address ends in 64.

06-M07 · IPv4 subnetting ★★★ E MCQ

How many conventional usable host addresses are in an IPv4 /27 subnet, excluding network and broadcast?

  1. 32
  2. 31
  3. 64
  4. 30

Answer: D — 30

Five host bits give 2^5=32 addresses. Under the stated conventional host allocation, subtract the network and broadcast addresses to get 30. Special /31 rules are not involved.

06-M08 · IPv4 subnetting ★★★ M MCQ

What is the broadcast address of 172.16.8.130/25?

  1. 172.16.8.255
  2. 172.16.9.255
  3. 172.16.8.128
  4. 172.16.8.127

Answer: A — 172.16.8.255

A /25 splits the last octet into 0..127 and 128..255. Address 130 is in the second block, whose all-ones host address is 255.

06-M09 · VLSM ★★ M MCQ

At least 100 conventional usable host addresses are required. What is the longest suitable IPv4 prefix?

  1. /27
  2. /25
  3. /26
  4. /24

Answer: B — /25

A /26 gives only 62 usable addresses. A /25 gives 126, meeting the requirement with the smaller address block than /24. The question asks for the longest suitable prefix.

06-M10 · Longest-prefix matching ★★★ M MCQ

Which output is selected for destination 10.1.2.9?

PrefixOutput
10.0.0.0/8A
10.1.0.0/16B
10.1.2.0/24C
0.0.0.0/0D
  1. A
  2. C
  3. D
  4. B

Answer: B — C

All listed routes match, but /24 has the most matching prefix bits. Forwarding uses the longest matching prefix, not necessarily the first displayed row.

06-M11 · IPv6 addressing ★★ E MCQ

What is the IPv6 address length?

  1. 128 bits
  2. 64 bits
  3. 256 bits
  4. 32 bits

Answer: A — 128 bits

IPv6 addresses contain 128 bits, commonly written as hexadecimal groups with compression notation. Address length does not by itself specify a subnet prefix length.

06-M12 · NAT ★★ M MCQ

Port-address translation allows several internal hosts to share one external IPv4 address primarily by tracking:

  1. No per-flow or mapping state
  2. Only Ethernet cable lengths
  3. Mappings involving internal/external addresses and transport ports
  4. Only a single global DNS name

Answer: C — Mappings involving internal/external addresses and transport ports

Port translation distinguishes otherwise overlapping conversations using mapped ports and protocol/address information. NAT is not, by itself, a complete security policy or end-to-end authentication mechanism.

06-M13 · Transmission delay ★★★ E MCQ

How long does it take to transmit a 1500-byte packet onto a 10 Mbps link, ignoring headers beyond the stated size?

  1. 120 ms
  2. 0.15 ms
  3. 1.2 ms
  4. 12 ms

Answer: C — 1.2 ms

Convert 1500 bytes to 12,000 bits. Transmission delay L/R=12,000/10,000,000 seconds=0.0012 s=1.2 ms.

06-M14 · Propagation delay ★★★ M MCQ

A 1000 km link propagates at 2×10^8 m/s. What is propagation delay?

  1. 5 ms
  2. 50 ms
  3. 500 ms
  4. 0.005 ms

Answer: A — 5 ms

Convert 1000 km to 10^6 m. Delay d/v=10^6/(2×10^8)=0.005 s=5 ms. This depends on distance and propagation speed, not packet size.

06-M15 · Store-and-forward delay ★★★ M MCQ

One packet crosses three store-and-forward links. Each transmission takes 1 ms and each propagation takes 2 ms; ignore all other delays. What is end-to-end time for the full packet?

  1. 5 ms
  2. 6 ms
  3. 9 ms
  4. 3 ms

Answer: C — 9 ms

For one isolated packet, each of the three links contributes both transmission and propagation: 3*(1+2)=9 ms. Multi-packet pipelining is a different calculation.

06-M16 · Throughput ★★★ E MCQ

A long transfer crosses serial links of 20,8,50 Mbps, with no other bottlenecks. What is the maximum steady-state throughput?

  1. 8 Mbps
  2. 50 Mbps
  3. 78 Mbps
  4. 20 Mbps

Answer: A — 8 Mbps

The bottleneck link limits the end-to-end steady-state rate. Link rates do not add when the same traffic must traverse every link.

06-M17 · Bandwidth-delay product ★★ M MCQ

A 10 Mbps path has RTT=50 ms. What is its RTT bandwidth-delay product?

  1. 50,000 bits
  2. 5,000,000 bits
  3. 500 bits
  4. 500,000 bits

Answer: D — 500,000 bits

Multiply rate by RTT in seconds: 10,000,000*0.05=500,000 bits, or 62,500 bytes. This quantity estimates the in-flight data needed to fill a path under the stated RTT model.

06-M18 · Stop-and-wait ★★ M MCQ

Data transmission time is 2 ms and round-trip propagation is 18 ms; ACK transmission is negligible. What is stop-and-wait utilization?

  1. 1.0
  2. 0.90
  3. 0.10
  4. 0.111...

Answer: C — 0.10

One cycle lasts 2+18=20 ms with 2 ms of useful data transmission. Utilization is 2/20=0.10. The given RTT is propagation only, so the data transmission time must be added.

06-M19 · ARQ window limits ★★ M MCQ

For a standard Selective Repeat protocol with a k-bit sequence field and equal send/receive window sizes, what is the maximum safe window size?

  1. k
  2. 2^k-1
  3. 2^k
  4. 2^(k-1)

Answer: D — 2^(k-1)

Restricting the window to half the sequence space avoids confusing delayed old frames with new frames after wraparound. Go-Back-N has a different usual window bound.

06-M20 · Error detection ★★★ E MCQ

A CRC generator polynomial has degree r. How many CRC remainder bits are appended?

  1. r+1
  2. 2r
  3. Exactly 8 regardless of generator
  4. r

Answer: D — r

Polynomial division by a degree-r generator leaves a remainder of degree below r, represented in r bits. The generator bit string itself has r+1 bits.

06-M21 · Ethernet access ★★ M MCQ

In classic shared half-duplex Ethernet, CSMA/CD includes:

  1. A collision-free schedule known permanently
  2. Guaranteed simultaneous use by every sender
  3. Encrypting frames before carrier sensing
  4. Sensing the medium and detecting collisions during transmission

Answer: D — Sensing the medium and detecting collisions during transmission

CSMA/CD governs contention on a shared half-duplex medium. Modern switched full-duplex Ethernet does not use collision detection in the same way; the context in the question is deliberate.

06-M22 · Link-layer switching ★★ M MCQ

An Ethernet learning switch learns which mapping from an arriving frame?

  1. Every remote MAC in the Internet
  2. Source MAC address to incoming port
  3. Destination IP to DNS name
  4. Source TCP port to process ID

Answer: B — Source MAC address to incoming port

The switch observes where a source MAC is reachable. Forwarding then uses the destination MAC table entry, flooding appropriately when unknown under normal switching rules.

06-M23 · TTL and hop limits ★★★ M MCQ

Why does IPv4 TTL help prevent persistent forwarding loops?

  1. It guarantees a shortest path
  2. It encrypts the routing table
  3. It increases at each hop
  4. Routers decrement it, and discard packets when it expires.

Answer: D — Routers decrement it, and discard packets when it expires.

TTL bounds a packet’s forwarding lifetime by decrementing along the path. It does not prevent a routing loop from occurring, but prevents one packet from circulating indefinitely.

06-M24 · TCP sequence numbers ★★★ M MCQ

A TCP segment has sequence number 1000 and carries 200 data bytes, with no SYN or FIN. What ACK number confirms receipt of all those bytes with no earlier gap?

  1. 1200
  2. 1001
  3. 200
  4. 1199

Answer: A — 1200

TCP acknowledgments identify the next byte expected. Bytes 1000 through 1199 were received, so the next expected byte is 1200.

06-M25 · TCP handshake ★★★ E MCQ

A SYN uses initial sequence number 100. What ACK number acknowledges that SYN?

  1. 100
  2. 101
  3. 200
  4. 0

Answer: B — 101

SYN consumes one sequence number even though it is not application data. The acknowledgment is therefore initial sequence plus one.

06-M26 · Flow versus congestion control ★★★ M MCQ

TCP’s advertised receive window primarily protects:

  1. The DNS cache lifetime
  2. The receiver’s available buffer capacity
  3. The sender’s file permissions
  4. Only the network router queues

Answer: B — The receiver’s available buffer capacity

Flow control prevents overwhelming the receiving endpoint. Congestion control addresses the network path’s capacity and congestion. Both may limit the actual sending window.

06-M27 · Simplified slow start ★★ M MCQ

In an idealized round-based slow start model, cwnd begins at 1 MSS and doubles each successful RTT. What is cwnd after three such RTTs?

  1. 3 MSS
  2. 16 MSS
  3. 8 MSS
  4. 4 MSS

Answer: C — 8 MSS

The sequence is initial 1, then 2,4,8. This is the explicit simplified model, not a claim that every deployed TCP with delayed ACKs follows exact round doubling.

06-M28 · TCP RTT estimation ★★ M MCQ

An estimator uses newRTT=(1-α)oldRTT+α sample, with α=0.125, oldRTT=100 ms and sample=140 ms. What is newRTT?

  1. 135 ms
  2. 120 ms
  3. 95 ms
  4. 105 ms

Answer: D — 105 ms

Compute 0.875*100+0.125*140=87.5+17.5=105 ms. The weighted average smooths changes instead of replacing the estimate with the latest sample.

06-M29 · Routing protocols ★★ M MCQ

Which description matches link-state routing?

  1. Each router knows only a destination MAC table
  2. Routers use link information to compute routes over a topology graph
  3. It requires one fixed static route forever
  4. It is identical to an application-layer DNS lookup

Answer: B — Routers use link information to compute routes over a topology graph

Link-state information is distributed so routers can run a graph shortest-path calculation. Distance-vector approaches instead exchange distance/next-hop information with neighbours.

06-M30 · DNS caching ★★ M MCQ

A cached DNS record’s TTL primarily controls:

  1. The number of TCP retransmissions
  2. How long that cached record may be reused before expiration
  3. How many routers an IP packet may cross
  4. The maximum label length in bits

Answer: B — How long that cached record may be reused before expiration

DNS TTL is a cache lifetime, distinct from IPv4 packet TTL. Similar terminology refers to different protocol objects, so identify the layer before interpreting it.

06-M31 · Security goals ★★★ E MCQ

Which goal is most directly violated when an attacker changes a stored exam mark without authorization?

  1. Availability only
  2. Compression
  3. Confidentiality only
  4. Integrity

Answer: D — Integrity

Integrity concerns preventing/detecting unauthorized modification. Reading a secret without authorization concerns confidentiality; preventing legitimate access concerns availability.

06-M32 · Symmetric encryption ★★★ E MCQ

In a symmetric encryption scheme, communicating parties ordinarily rely on:

  1. The secrecy of every algorithm specification
  2. No keys
  3. A shared secret key for the relevant encryption/decryption operation
  4. A publicly revealed private key

Answer: C — A shared secret key for the relevant encryption/decryption operation

The security assumption centres on appropriate secret-key management and the scheme’s security, not merely hiding the algorithm. Real protocols also need integrity and nonce handling.

06-M33 · Public-key encryption ★★★ M MCQ

To send confidential data to Bob using a suitable public-key encryption scheme, Alice encrypts to:

  1. Alice’s private signing key only
  2. Bob’s publicly disclosed private key
  3. Any key labelled Bob without verifying its binding
  4. Bob’s authenticated public key

Answer: D — Bob’s authenticated public key

Bob uses his corresponding private decryption key. Authenticating the public-key binding matters; otherwise an attacker can substitute a key. Public-key encryption is distinct from signing.

06-M34 · Cryptographic hashes ★★★ M MCQ

Collision resistance means it should be computationally infeasible to:

  1. Find two distinct messages with the same digest
  2. Encrypt a digest with a public key
  3. Store the hash output
  4. Recover any message from its digest in every case

Answer: A — Find two distinct messages with the same digest

Collision resistance concerns finding any two distinct colliding inputs. Preimage resistance concerns finding an input for a specified digest. They are related but different properties.

06-M35 · HMAC ★★★ M MCQ

An HMAC primarily provides which service when its secret key is protected?

  1. Confidentiality of the message
  2. Message integrity and origin authentication among key holders
  3. Public verifiability by anyone without the key
  4. Guaranteed legal nonrepudiation

Answer: B — Message integrity and origin authentication among key holders

A keyed MAC detects unauthorized modifications and authenticates possession of the shared secret. Every verifier holding the key can also generate tags, unlike ordinary public-verifiable signatures.

06-M36 · Digital signatures ★★★ M MCQ

A digital signature is ordinarily created using the signer’s:

  1. Private signing key
  2. Plaintext password as the whole signature
  3. Recipient’s public encryption key only
  4. Public verification key

Answer: A — Private signing key

Verification uses the corresponding authenticated public key. A signature authenticates signed content under the scheme; it does not automatically encrypt the message or prove real-world identity without key binding.

06-M37 · Replay protection ★★★ M MCQ

Which protocol feature directly helps detect reuse of an old valid authenticated request?

  1. Using the same fixed nonce forever
  2. Base64 encoding
  3. Removing all timestamps and state
  4. A fresh nonce or sequence number included in the authenticated data

Answer: D — A fresh nonce or sequence number included in the authenticated data

A valid signature or MAC alone can authenticate an old message. Freshness values and appropriate validation/state distinguish an accepted new request from a replay.

06-M38 · Password storage ★★ M MCQ

Which design is appropriate in principle for verifying stored passwords?

  1. Plaintext storage
  2. Fast unsalted hashing for every account
  3. A salted, deliberately expensive password-hashing/KDF scheme
  4. Reversible encryption with the key beside the database as the only protection

Answer: C — A salted, deliberately expensive password-hashing/KDF scheme

Unique salts prevent identical-password hashes from trivially matching and frustrate precomputed tables. Deliberate cost raises the expense of offline guessing. This is a design principle, not a current parameter recommendation.

06-M39 · Authentication factors ★★ M MCQ

Which pair represents two distinct authentication-factor categories?

  1. A password and a separately controlled hardware security key
  2. A password and a remembered PIN only
  3. A username and display name
  4. Two different passwords

Answer: A — A password and a separately controlled hardware security key

Knowledge plus possession are different factors. Two memorized secrets remain the same category, and a username is usually an identifier rather than an authentication secret.

06-M40 · Least privilege ★★★ E MCQ

Least privilege means:

  1. Grant only the permissions required for the intended task
  2. Allow access whenever a rule is unclear
  3. Give every user administrator rights
  4. Disable audit logs to reduce work

Answer: A — Grant only the permissions required for the intended task

Limiting permissions reduces unnecessary exposure and damage from mistakes or compromised accounts. It does not mean denying all useful access.

06-M41 · Access control ★★ M MCQ

Role-based access control primarily associates permissions with:

  1. Roles assigned to users
  2. Random packet identifiers
  3. The length of usernames
  4. Only IP hop counts

Answer: A — Roles assigned to users

Roles group job-related permissions, and users receive authorized roles. This is conceptually different from deciding access solely by object ownership or mandatory security labels.

06-M42 · SQL injection defence ★★★ M MCQ

Which is the primary coding control against treating untrusted input as SQL syntax?

  1. Parameterized queries with correctly bound values
  2. Hiding the database product name
  3. Concatenating user input into SQL
  4. Escaping only space characters

Answer: A — Parameterized queries with correctly bound values

Parameter binding separates values from SQL structure. It does not mean every dynamic identifier can be a value parameter; those cases require controlled query construction and allowlists.

06-M43 · Buffer safety ★★ M MCQ

A defensive control directly addressing a fixed-size buffer copy is:

  1. Hide the source code only
  2. Validate bounds and use a correctly sized destination with checked operations
  3. Disable all compiler warnings
  4. Assume every input is shorter than the buffer

Answer: B — Validate bounds and use a correctly sized destination with checked operations

The program must ensure the full write, including terminators where needed, fits within the object. Platform mitigations are useful layers but do not make out-of-bounds writes correct.

06-M44 · Firewall limitations ★★ M MCQ

A network firewall that permits HTTPS traffic does not by itself guarantee:

  1. That a port-filter rule can be applied
  2. That the permitted application requests are authorized and harmless
  3. That network segmentation is possible
  4. That some traffic can be blocked

Answer: B — That the permitted application requests are authorized and harmless

Allowed encrypted transport can carry malicious or unauthorized application actions. Application authentication, authorization, input handling and monitoring remain necessary.

06-M45 · TLS and certificates ★★ M MCQ

Why should a client validate the server certificate/identity in a TLS connection?

  1. To prevent all application bugs
  2. To guarantee the server never stores data
  3. To bind the secure channel to the intended server rather than an impersonator
  4. To increase the Ethernet frame size

Answer: C — To bind the secure channel to the intended server rather than an impersonator

Encryption without authenticating the endpoint can protect a channel to the wrong party. Certificate validation includes checking the relevant identity and trust conditions, not merely accepting any certificate presented.

06-M46 · Toy RSA arithmetic ★★ M MCQ

Toy RSA uses p=5,q=11,e=3. What is n?

  1. 55
  2. 15
  3. 33
  4. 40

Answer: A — 55

RSA’s modulus is the product pq=55. The totient here is (p-1)(q-1)=40, a different quantity used to find the private exponent. These tiny values are for arithmetic practice only.

06-M47 · Toy Diffie-Hellman ★ M MCQ

For toy Diffie-Hellman with p=23,g=5 and Alice’s secret exponent a=6, what is her public value g^a mod p?

  1. 8
  2. 19
  3. 15
  4. 6

Answer: A — 8

5^2≡2, 5^4≡4 and 5^6≡4*2≡8 modulo 23. The exponent is secret; the modular-power result is exchanged. These tiny parameters offer no practical security.

06-M48 · Availability controls ★★ M MCQ

Which control most directly supports recovery after destructive data loss?

  1. Only hiding error messages
  2. Only using longer usernames
  3. Tested backups with an appropriate recovery procedure
  4. Only changing a logo

Answer: C — Tested backups with an appropriate recovery procedure

A backup that cannot be restored is not evidence of recoverability. Backup isolation, restoration testing and recovery requirements matter; backup does not replace all prevention controls.

06-M49 · Threat, vulnerability and risk ★★ M MCQ

An unpatched input-validation flaw is best described as:

  1. A complete risk assessment
  2. A business recovery objective
  3. A guaranteed successful attack
  4. A vulnerability

Answer: D — A vulnerability

A vulnerability is a weakness that may be exploited. Risk additionally considers threat, likelihood, consequences and existing controls rather than just the existence of a flaw.

06-M50 · Authentication versus authorization ★★★ E MCQ

A user successfully signs in but is denied access to another user’s private record. Which distinction is illustrated?

  1. Authorization means password checking only
  2. Authentication gives access to every record
  3. Authentication succeeded; authorization denied that action.
  4. The system must have a network failure

Answer: C — Authentication succeeded; authorization denied that action.

Identity verification and permission to perform a particular action are separate decisions. Every sensitive action should be evaluated against the relevant access policy.

06-W01 · Subnetting and address ranges ★★★ M WRITTEN

For host 192.168.10.77/26, calculate mask, network, broadcast, usable host range and usable count. Draw a simple subnet/gateway diagram, using the first usable address for the gateway.

Worked answer

A /26 mask is 255.255.255.192, leaving six host bits. Blocks have 64 addresses; 77 belongs to 64..127.

ItemValue
Network192.168.10.64
Broadcast192.168.10.127
Usable range192.168.10.65–192.168.10.126
Usable count2^6-2=62
Chosen gateway192.168.10.65

The host and gateway share the network prefix. The host sends off-subnet traffic to the gateway’s local-link address while keeping the remote destination in the IP header.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

06-W02 · VLSM address planning ★★★ M WRITTEN

Allocate subnets for 100,50 and 20 usable hosts from 192.168.20.0/24. Use largest-first contiguous allocations. Give networks, prefixes, broadcasts and remaining block.

Worked answer

NeedNetwork/prefixBroadcastUsable range
100192.168.20.0/25192.168.20.127.1–.126
50192.168.20.128/26192.168.20.191.129–.190
20192.168.20.192/27192.168.20.223.193–.222

The required block sizes are 128,64,32, because usable counts are 126,62,30. Each start is aligned to its block size and no ranges overlap. The remaining range is 192.168.20.224–255, represented by 192.168.20.224/27.

Count usable addresses after reserving network and broadcast. Allocating exactly 100,50,20 addresses would not create valid CIDR blocks. These conventional subnet calculations exclude special point-to-point address rules.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

06-W03 · Delay and units ★★★ M WRITTEN

One 1500-byte packet crosses three store-and-forward links of 10 Mbps. Each link has 2 ms propagation delay. Ignore processing, queueing and ACKs. Calculate transmission delay per link and total time for the complete packet to arrive.

Worked answer

Packet length is 1500*8=12,000 bits. Per-link transmission delay is L/R=12,000/10,000,000=0.0012 s=1.2 ms.

ContributionCalculationDelay
Transmission3*1.2 ms3.6 ms
Propagation3*2 ms6 ms
Total3.6+69.6 ms

Store-and-forward means each intermediate router receives the packet before beginning its next-link transmission in this model. For a stream of packets, links may pipeline different packets; that throughput calculation should not be confused with one-packet latency. Mbps is treated as decimal megabits per second.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

06-W04 · Stop-and-wait and window sizing ★★ M WRITTEN

A data packet has 1000 bytes and the link is 1 Mbps. Round-trip propagation is 20 ms; ACK size and processing are negligible. Find stop-and-wait utilization and the minimum number of packets in flight needed for full utilization under this idealized model.

Worked answer

Transmission time is 8000/1,000,000=8 ms. A stop-and-wait cycle lasts 8+20=28 ms. Utilization is 8/28=2/7≈0.2857, or 28.57%.

With a sliding window W, the sender can stay busy when W*8 ms ≥28 ms. Thus W≥3.5 and the minimum integer window is 4 packets. The four transmissions occupy 32 ms, by which time the first ACK can return.

The calculation assumes no loss, negligible ACK transmission, immediate acknowledgments and the stated propagation interpretation. It is not a universal TCP window formula without those assumptions.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

06-W05 · TCP sequence-number diagram ★★★ M WRITTEN

Draw a TCP three-way handshake with client ISN=100 and server ISN=500. Then show the client sending 40 bytes and the server acknowledging them. Assume no loss and no server data.

Worked answer

Client sends SYN seq=100. Server sends SYN+ACK seq=500, ack=101. Client completes with ACK seq=101, ack=501. The subsequent client data has seq=101, length=40, ack=501. It covers sequence numbers 101..140.

The server ACK is seq=501, ack=141. A pure ACK consumes no sequence number; SYN and FIN each consume one. Byte sequence numbers are not packet counts. The diagram is a simplified trace of the requested exchange.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

06-W06 · Longest-prefix forwarding ★★★ M WRITTEN

For the forwarding table, give outputs for 10.1.2.9, 10.1.8.4, 10.9.0.1 and 192.0.2.7. Explain the selection rule.

PrefixInterface
10.0.0.0/8A
10.1.0.0/16B
10.1.2.0/24C
0.0.0.0/0D

Worked answer

DestinationSelected prefixInterface
10.1.2.910.1.2.0/24C
10.1.8.410.1.0.0/16B
10.9.0.110.0.0.0/8A
192.0.2.70.0.0.0/0D

Compare all matching prefixes and select the one with the greatest prefix length. The default /0 matches every destination but loses to any more-specific match. If equal-prefix alternatives exist, routing/forwarding policies may choose among them; this table has no such ambiguity.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

06-W07 · CRC polynomial arithmetic ★★ H WRITTEN

Using binary polynomial arithmetic, append a CRC to data 1101 with generator 1011. Show the remainder and verify the transmitted codeword is divisible by the generator.

Worked answer

The generator has degree 3, so append three zero bits before division: 1101000. Divide using XOR subtraction.

XOR long division
1101000 XOR 1011000 = 0110000
0110000 XOR 0101100 = 0011100
0011100 XOR 0010110 = 0001010
0001010 XOR 0001011 = 0000001

The three-bit remainder is 001. Append it to the original data to obtain codeword 1101001. Dividing this codeword by 1011 gives remainder 000, verifying construction. A zero remainder at the receiver is a consistency check under the code, not proof that no possible error occurred.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

06-W08 · IPv4 fragmentation ★★ H WRITTEN

An IPv4 datagram has 4000 payload bytes and a 20-byte header. It must cross an MTU-1500 link; fragmentation is permitted and no options exist. Give each fragment’s payload, total length, fragment offset and MF flag.

Worked answer

Maximum nonfinal payload is floor((1500-20)/8)*8=1480 bytes. The first two fragments carry 1480 each, leaving 1040 for the last.

FragmentPayloadTotal lengthOffset in 8-byte unitsMF
11480150001
2148015001851
3104010603700

Offsets count original payload positions: 1480/8=185 and 2960/8=370. Each fragment has its own 20-byte header, and all share the identification needed for reassembly. The last fragment’s MF is zero. The problem explicitly allows fragmentation, so a DF-prohibited case is not being assumed.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

06-W09 · Security mechanisms and goals ★★★ M WRITTEN

Compare encryption, unkeyed hashing, HMAC and digital signatures. State what each does and does not establish. Draw a simple sign/verify flow.

Worked answer

MechanismMain contributionImportant limitation
EncryptionConfidentiality under its security modelPlain encryption need not authenticate modifications
Unkeyed hashDigest for comparison under hash propertiesAnyone can recompute it after changing data
HMACIntegrity/origin authentication for secret-key holdersNo confidentiality or public verification
SignaturePublic-verifiable authentication of signed contentNeeds authenticated public-key binding; not encryption

A receiver must verify the relevant context as well as the cryptographic operation: who owns the key, which message fields were signed, and whether freshness is required. For shared-key MACs, another key holder can generate a valid tag, so public attribution differs from a signature.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

06-W10 · Toy RSA ★★ H WRITTEN

For educational RSA, take p=5,q=11,e=3 and plaintext integer m=7. Compute n, φ(n), a valid private exponent d, ciphertext c and recovered plaintext. Explain why these parameters are not for real security.

Worked answer

n=5*11=55 and φ(n)=4*10=40. Choose d=27 because 3*27=81≡1 mod 40. Encrypt c=7^3 mod55=343 mod55=13.

Decrypt using modular powers: 13²≡4, 13⁴≡16, 13⁸≡36 and 13¹⁶≡31 mod55. Since 27=16+8+2+1, 13²⁷≡31*36*4*13≡7 mod55. The plaintext is recovered.

These tiny factors are trivial to discover. The exercise omits secure encoding/padding and real protocol requirements; textbook arithmetic alone is not a deployable encryption construction.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

06-W11 · Toy Diffie-Hellman and authentication ★ H WRITTEN

For p=23,g=5, Alice uses a=6 and Bob b=15. Compute their public values and shared secret. Explain why unauthenticated exchange is vulnerable to an active intermediary.

Worked answer

Alice sends A=5^6 mod23=8. Bob sends B=5^15 mod23=19. Alice computes B^6 mod23: 19²≡16, 19⁴≡3, so 19⁶≡3*16≡2. Bob computes A^15 mod23=2 as well.

An active intermediary can substitute public values and establish separate secrets with each endpoint when the exchange lacks authentication. The mathematical agreement alone does not establish who the peer is. Authenticate the transcript/identities through an appropriate protocol design.

The numbers here are intentionally tiny for hand calculation and provide no practical cryptographic security.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

06-W12 · Authentication, authorization and replay ★★★ M WRITTEN

Design the checks for a sensitive “change delivery address” request in an authenticated application. Explain authentication, object-level authorization, input validation and replay/freshness concerns without assuming that HTTPS alone solves them.

Worked answer

Authenticate the session or request credential. Authorize the action on the specific order: the authenticated user must be permitted to modify that order, and its state must allow changes. Validate the new address against the application’s data rules and use parameterized database operations.

Protect the request against relevant cross-site request and credential threats under the actual application model. For a signed or externally replayable request, bind a nonce/sequence number or appropriate freshness information into the authenticated message and validate it. A one-time authorization may also need an idempotency mechanism.

HTTPS protects the channel under its authentication and encryption assumptions, but it does not decide whether this user owns this order or whether a repeated valid request is acceptable. Log the event without storing unnecessary secrets.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

06-W13 · Defensive threat modelling ★★ M WRITTEN

A student portal has a web server, database and administrator interface. Draw trust boundaries and propose five defensive controls, each linked to a threat or failure.

Worked answer

Threat/failureControl
Unauthorized record accessObject-level authorization checks
SQL injectionParameterized queries and least-privilege DB account
Administrator credential compromiseStrong MFA and restricted admin access
Data interceptionAuthenticated encrypted transport
Destructive lossIsolated tested backups and recovery plan

Controls should be layered and tested. A database kept off the public network still requires safe queries and access rules from the application. Record security events and review permissions; the diagram does not guarantee that any proposed deployment is secure merely because it has boxes and firewalls.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

06-W14 · TCP flow and congestion limits ★★ M WRITTEN

A simplified sender is limited by receive window 32 KiB and congestion window 48 KiB. RTT is 64 ms. Find the effective window and the window-limited throughput bound, assuming no loss or other bottleneck.

Worked answer

The usable in-flight limit is min(rwnd,cwnd)=min(32,48) KiB=32 KiB=32,768 bytes. The receive window is the active constraint.

Throughput bound=W/RTT=32,768/0.064=512,000 bytes/s. In bits this is 4,096,000 bit/s=4.096 Mbps. A slower physical bottleneck would lower the actual throughput further.

rwnd is receiver flow control; cwnd is congestion control. A large advertised receive window does not permit the sender to exceed its congestion window, and vice versa. The calculation is an idealized upper bound, not a latency guarantee.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

06-W15 · Risk and incident reasoning ★★ M WRITTEN

A database backup is publicly readable by mistake. Distinguish the vulnerability, a threat, a plausible impact and immediate defensive response. Explain why deleting the public copy alone is not a complete assessment.

Worked answer

Vulnerability: incorrect access permissions expose the backup. Threat: an unauthorized party may discover and download it. Impact depends on its contents: personal-data disclosure, secret exposure and further account compromise may follow.

Immediately restrict access while preserving necessary incident evidence. Determine what was exposed and for how long; inspect available access logs with their limitations; rotate exposed credentials; assess affected data and required notifications through the responsible organization. Verify other backups and storage policies.

Deleting the copy stops that particular endpoint from serving it, but cannot retrieve already downloaded copies or undo credential exposure. Avoid claiming definite non-access simply because logs show no event when logging may have been incomplete.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

07 · Software Engineering and Information System Design

Prefer precise scenarios over slogan memorization. UML figures are simplified but keep the relevant notation (multiplicity, generalization and composition). Practice scores and project estimates are illustrative, not BUET marking rules. Quality properties require explicit workload and measurement definitions.

Coverage: Requirements and traceability; lifecycle models and agile work; modular design, architecture and UML/DFD; testing techniques, coverage and defects; project networks and estimation; configuration, deployment, maintenance, risk and quality metrics.

Reading references: R15, R16, R17
