07-M01 · Requirements ★★★ E MCQ

Which is a functional requirement?

  1. 95% of requests shall complete within two seconds.
  2. The service shall have 99.9% monthly availability.
  3. The application shall use at most 500 MB under a stated load.
  4. The system shall allow a student to download a transcript.

Answer: D — The system shall allow a student to download a transcript.

The download requirement specifies a capability the system must provide. The others specify measurable quality or resource constraints rather than the capability itself.

07-M02 · Measurable requirements ★★★ E MCQ

Which performance requirement is most testable?

  1. The interface must be modern.
  2. For 200 concurrent users, 95% of search responses finish within 2 seconds on the defined dataset.
  3. Search should never feel slow.
  4. The system must be very fast.

Answer: B — For 200 concurrent users, 95% of search responses finish within 2 seconds on the defined dataset.

The measurable statement specifies workload, response percentile, time bound and data context. A reproducible environment and timing boundaries would complete the test definition.

07-M03 · Requirements traceability ★★ M MCQ

A requirements traceability matrix principally connects:

  1. Every screen to a colour code
  2. Requirements to design, implementation and verification evidence
  3. Source files to line length
  4. Only employee names to salaries

Answer: B — Requirements to design, implementation and verification evidence

Traceability helps evaluate change impact and whether requirements have corresponding implementation/tests. A matrix alone does not prove the linked tests are adequate.

07-M04 · Verification and validation ★★★ M MCQ

Which activity is primarily validation?

  1. Renaming variables to follow a style guide
  2. Counting source files
  3. Checking code against its detailed specification only
  4. Checking with users that the delivered workflow solves their intended problem

Answer: D — Checking with users that the delivered workflow solves their intended problem

Validation concerns fitness for intended use and stakeholder needs. Verification checks conformance to specified requirements or design artefacts. Both can uncover important failures.

07-M05 · Prototyping ★★ M MCQ

A throwaway prototype is most directly useful for:

  1. Replacing all security requirements permanently
  2. Clarifying uncertain requirements before committing to production design
  3. Proving that users never change their minds
  4. Guaranteeing production scalability without testing

Answer: B — Clarifying uncertain requirements before committing to production design

A prototype can expose misunderstandings and gather feedback cheaply. Throwaway means it is not automatically treated as production-quality implementation.

07-M06 · Lifecycle models ★★ M MCQ

Which characteristic best fits an incremental development approach?

  1. Delivering functionality in successive usable additions
  2. Releasing only after every possible feature is complete
  3. Prohibiting feedback between iterations
  4. Removing all planning

Answer: A — Delivering functionality in successive usable additions

Incremental delivery adds capability in stages. It can be combined with iterative refinement, but the two terms emphasize additions versus repeated improvement.

07-M07 · Spiral model ★★ M MCQ

The spiral development model particularly emphasizes:

  1. A fixed ban on prototypes
  2. Only code formatting
  3. Eliminating stakeholder evaluation
  4. Risk-driven cycles

Answer: D — Risk-driven cycles

Each cycle considers objectives, alternatives and risks before further development/evaluation. The model is not simply a waterfall drawn as a circle.

07-M08 · Agile principles ★★ M MCQ

Which practice most directly supports responding to learning during development?

  1. Measuring progress only by documents produced
  2. Frequent feedback and reprioritization of planned work
  3. Refusing stakeholder input after the first meeting
  4. Treating a prototype as an immutable contract

Answer: B — Frequent feedback and reprioritization of planned work

Feedback can reveal that needs or assumptions changed. Responsiveness does not mean ignoring all documentation, quality controls or commitments.

07-M09 · Scrum accountabilities ★ M MCQ

In the Scrum Guide framework, who is accountable for ordering the Product Backlog?

  1. The Product Owner
  2. Every external customer independently
  3. Only the compiler
  4. The network administrator by default

Answer: A — The Product Owner

The Product Owner is accountable for effective Product Backlog management, including ordering. This does not mean no collaboration or delegation occurs; accountability remains clear.

07-M10 · User stories and acceptance ★★ M MCQ

Which item makes a user story more verifiable?

  1. Replacing user goals with a technology brand
  2. Only increasing its word count
  3. Removing all examples
  4. Concrete acceptance criteria describing observable outcomes

Answer: D — Concrete acceptance criteria describing observable outcomes

Acceptance criteria specify what must be demonstrated for the story. They should address relevant edge cases and constraints, not merely repeat the story title.

07-M11 · Cohesion ★★★ E MCQ

High cohesion in a module means its responsibilities are:

  1. Closely related to a focused purpose
  2. Dependent on every other module
  3. Unrelated but stored in one long file
  4. Duplicated across all modules

Answer: A — Closely related to a focused purpose

A cohesive module groups related responsibilities, making its purpose easier to understand and change. High cohesion is distinct from high coupling.

07-M12 · Coupling ★★★ E MCQ

Which design generally reduces coupling?

  1. Read another module’s private fields directly everywhere
  2. Depend on a small stable interface rather than another module’s internal representation
  3. Duplicate internal assumptions in many callers
  4. Use global mutable state for every interaction

Answer: B — Depend on a small stable interface rather than another module’s internal representation

A stable contract limits the details other modules must know. Lower coupling supports independent change, though interface design still has to preserve required behaviour.

07-M13 · Information hiding ★★★ M MCQ

Information hiding asks a module to conceal:

  1. All error conditions without reporting them
  2. Every useful operation from every user
  3. Only source-code indentation
  4. Design decisions likely to change behind a defined interface

Answer: D — Design decisions likely to change behind a defined interface

The goal is to prevent clients from depending on volatile internals. It is a modularity principle, not a requirement for secrecy or an excuse to suppress observable failures.

07-M14 · Single responsibility ★★ M MCQ

A class handles payroll calculation, printer drivers and login authentication. The most direct design concern is:

  1. Automatic memory leaks in every language
  2. No possible need for tests
  3. Unrelated reasons to change are mixed in one component
  4. Too much cohesion

Answer: C — Unrelated reasons to change are mixed in one component

These responsibilities change for different business/technical reasons. Separating them can improve cohesion and testability, without claiming one universal class count for all applications.

07-M15 · Dependency inversion ★ M MCQ

Which statement best expresses dependency inversion?

  1. Every class must inherit from a database table.
  2. A user interface must directly modify disk sectors.
  3. All abstractions should depend on concrete drivers.
  4. High-level policy and low-level details should depend on suitable abstractions.

Answer: D — High-level policy and low-level details should depend on suitable abstractions.

An abstraction lets policy use capabilities without embedding low-level implementation choices. It is not the same as adding interfaces indiscriminately to every small function.

07-M16 · Layered architecture ★★ M MCQ

In a conventional layered application, a presentation layer primarily handles:

  1. User interaction and presentation concerns
  2. Raw disk scheduling
  3. CPU instruction decoding
  4. Every database transaction log record

Answer: A — User interaction and presentation concerns

Layering separates concerns, such as presentation, application/domain logic and persistence. Real systems may use different arrangements, so the question states the conventional model.

07-M17 · UML class diagrams ★★★ M MCQ

In a UML-style class diagram, a hollow triangular arrow points toward:

  1. The generalized superclass/interface being inherited
  2. The most recently created object
  3. The first method alphabetically
  4. The owned component in composition

Answer: A — The generalized superclass/interface being inherited

Generalization is drawn from specific to general, with a hollow triangle at the general end. A filled diamond instead denotes the whole end of composition.

07-M18 · UML composition ★★★ E MCQ

A filled diamond in a UML association marks:

  1. Every optional relationship
  2. An arbitrary method call
  3. A database foreign key automatically
  4. The composite whole end

Answer: D — The composite whole end

Composition models strong whole-part ownership with associated lifetime semantics in the model. The diamond belongs at the whole, not the part.

07-M19 · UML multiplicity ★★★ M MCQ

A multiplicity 0..* at the Course end of a Student–Course association means:

  1. Every student must have at least one course
  2. There can be only zero courses globally
  3. One student may be linked to zero or more courses.
  4. Every course has exactly one student

Answer: C — One student may be linked to zero or more courses.

Multiplicity at an association end describes how many instances at that end relate to one instance at the opposite end. Read the end placement carefully.

07-M20 · UML sequence diagrams ★★★ E MCQ

A sequence diagram primarily shows:

  1. Only network cable distances
  2. Only a database’s candidate keys
  3. A sorted list of class names
  4. Interactions/messages between participants over time

Answer: D — Interactions/messages between participants over time

Participants have lifelines and messages ordered along the time direction. This is useful for explaining a scenario, not every possible system behaviour at once.

07-M21 · UML use cases ★★ M MCQ

A use-case actor is best understood as:

  1. A database row inside the system
  2. An external role interacting with the system boundary
  3. Only a human individual’s legal name
  4. A private helper method

Answer: B — An external role interacting with the system boundary

An actor can be a person-role or an external system. The system boundary helps distinguish outside interactions from internal implementation responsibilities.

07-M22 · Use-case relationships ★ M MCQ

A UML include relationship is used to:

  1. Guarantee an optional extension always occurs
  2. Represent class inheritance only
  3. Factor behaviour that a base use case invokes as part of its specified behaviour
  4. Store an SQL join condition

Answer: C — Factor behaviour that a base use case invokes as part of its specified behaviour

Include directs from the including case to the included behaviour. Extend describes conditional extension of a base case at specified extension points; the two should not be used interchangeably.

07-M23 · Data-flow diagrams ★★ M MCQ

In a conventional DFD, an external entity sending data directly to a data store without a process is:

  1. A modelling problem because transformation/access should pass through a process
  2. A UML inheritance arrow
  3. The only valid DFD connection
  4. A proof of database normalization

Answer: A — A modelling problem because transformation/access should pass through a process

DFDs distinguish external entities, processes, stores and flows. Under the usual rules, flows to/from stores are mediated by processes rather than directly by external actors.

07-M24 · Testing levels ★★★ E MCQ

A test focused on interactions between modules is primarily:

  1. Integration testing
  2. Code compilation only
  3. Typography testing
  4. Unit testing of one isolated function only

Answer: A — Integration testing

Integration tests target interfaces and collaborations. Unit tests focus on a smaller component; system tests consider the complete integrated system.

07-M25 · Black-box testing ★★★ E MCQ

Black-box test design is based primarily on:

  1. CPU register allocation
  2. The exact internal branch graph only
  3. Specified externally visible behaviour
  4. Variable names only

Answer: C — Specified externally visible behaviour

Inputs and expected outputs come from requirements or interface contracts without relying on internal structure. White-box techniques deliberately use that structure.

07-M26 · Boundary-value analysis ★★★ M MCQ

An integer age is valid from 18 through 60 inclusive. Which test set best targets both boundaries and immediate neighbours?

  1. {25,30,35}
  2. {18,30,60} only
  3. {0,100,200}
  4. {17,18,19,59,60,61}

Answer: D — {17,18,19,59,60,61}

Values immediately below, at and above each endpoint exercise off-by-one and boundary-check defects. Interior and input-type tests may still be needed beyond this set.

07-M27 · Equivalence partitioning ★★★ M MCQ

For integer input valid in [1,100], a basic value partitioning is:

  1. Below 1, 1..100, above 100
  2. Every number in one identical partition
  3. Only the value 100
  4. Only odd versus even

Answer: A — Below 1, 1..100, above 100

Each partition groups inputs expected to have similar validity behaviour. Additional categories such as noninteger or missing input require separate handling if the interface permits them.

07-M28 · Decision-table testing ★★ M MCQ

Decision-table testing is particularly useful when:

  1. Only a single arithmetic constant exists
  2. Several conditions combine to determine actions
  3. Only screen colours matter
  4. There are no conditions at all

Answer: B — Several conditions combine to determine actions

A table makes combinations explicit and helps detect missing rules or inconsistent actions. Infeasible combinations should be identified rather than counted blindly.

07-M29 · Statement coverage ★★★ E MCQ

A suite executes 30 of 40 executable statements. What is statement coverage?

  1. 75%
  2. 40%
  3. 30%
  4. 133.3%

Answer: A — 75%

Coverage is exercised items divided by total items: 30/40=0.75. It measures execution of the chosen structural items, not overall correctness.

07-M30 · Branch coverage ★★★ M MCQ

For a single if statement with both outcomes feasible, which test requirement gives full branch coverage?

  1. Execute only the final return
  2. Execute any one statement once
  3. Execute both the true and false outcomes.
  4. Test only the true outcome

Answer: C — Execute both the true and false outcomes.

Branch coverage concerns outcomes of control decisions. A suite can execute every statement while missing a branch, for example an if without an else.

07-M31 · Condition versus decision coverage ★★ M MCQ

For decision A OR B, testing (T,F) and (F,T) achieves which result?

  1. Both tests make the decision false
  2. Each condition is both true and false, but the decision is never false.
  3. Full decision coverage necessarily
  4. No condition coverage at all

Answer: B — Each condition is both true and false, but the decision is never false.

Across the tests, A and B each change truth value, but OR is true in both cases. This demonstrates that simple condition coverage does not guarantee decision coverage.

07-M32 · Cyclomatic complexity ★★ M MCQ

A connected control-flow graph has E=9 edges and N=7 nodes. What is cyclomatic complexity using E-N+2?

  1. 2
  2. 1
  3. 16
  4. 4

Answer: D — 4

For one connected program graph, M=9-7+2=4. The measure describes independent control-flow paths under the graph model, not a guarantee of four tests finding all bugs.

07-M33 · Regression and confirmation tests ★★★ M MCQ

After fixing a defect, confirmation testing primarily checks:

  1. That no feature ever changes again
  2. Only unrelated performance statistics
  3. Only the size of the source repository
  4. That the specific reported defect is fixed

Answer: D — That the specific reported defect is fixed

Confirmation retests the defect. Regression testing looks for unintended effects on previously working behaviour. Both can be required after a change.

07-M34 · Static versus dynamic testing ★★★ E MCQ

Which is a static quality-assurance activity?

  1. Executing a unit test
  2. Reviewing requirements without executing the software
  3. Load testing a running service
  4. Running an API test

Answer: B — Reviewing requirements without executing the software

Static activities inspect artefacts without running the program. Dynamic testing exercises executable behaviour. Static review can find ambiguities before code exists.

07-M35 · Test doubles ★★ M MCQ

In top-down integration, a stub commonly substitutes for:

  1. The operating system clock frequency
  2. A called lower-level component not yet available
  3. A production customer
  4. The high-level caller only

Answer: B — A called lower-level component not yet available

The stub provides controlled responses through the callee interface. A driver instead invokes a component under test and is associated with bottom-up integration.

07-M36 · Defect reports ★★★ E MCQ

Which defect report element is most useful for reproducibility?

  1. Only the developer’s name
  2. Only a screenshot with no context
  3. Exact steps, input, environment, expected and actual results
  4. Only “it does not work”

Answer: C — Exact steps, input, environment, expected and actual results

Reproduction details allow another person to observe and isolate the failure. Evidence should be relevant and protect sensitive data. Severity and priority are separate additional fields.

07-M37 · Severity and priority ★★ M MCQ

A severe defect and its repair priority differ because:

  1. All severe defects have identical deadlines
  2. Severity is always the same as line count
  3. Impact and scheduling urgency are related but separate judgments
  4. Priority never depends on business context

Answer: C — Impact and scheduling urgency are related but separate judgments

Severity describes consequences; priority considers urgency, exposure, dependencies and delivery context. A rarely reachable severe issue and a frequent moderate issue may require different scheduling decisions.

07-M38 · Version control ★★ M MCQ

A merge conflict indicates:

  1. All work on both branches is lost
  2. The repository is necessarily corrupted
  3. Changes cannot be automatically reconciled under the tool’s merge rules
  4. The compiler has proven semantic equivalence

Answer: C — Changes cannot be automatically reconciled under the tool’s merge rules

A developer must resolve the competing changes and test the result. A conflict-free textual merge is also not proof that the merged program behaves correctly.

07-M39 · Continuous integration ★★ M MCQ

Continuous integration most directly encourages:

  1. Deleting version history after each commit
  2. Frequent integration with automated build/test feedback
  3. Deploying without any tests by definition
  4. Keeping every branch isolated until release day

Answer: B — Frequent integration with automated build/test feedback

Short feedback cycles reveal integration problems earlier. CI is not identical to continuous production deployment, which involves additional release decisions and controls.

07-M40 · Refactoring ★★★ M MCQ

Refactoring is intended to change:

  1. The required business behaviour without any validation
  2. Every public interface arbitrarily
  3. Internal structure while preserving externally observable behaviour
  4. Only the customer’s identity

Answer: C — Internal structure while preserving externally observable behaviour

Tests and careful reasoning help guard the behaviour-preserving goal. Refactoring can improve maintainability but is not a substitute for separately specifying intended feature changes.

07-M41 · Technical debt ★★ M MCQ

Technical debt most usefully refers to:

  1. Any use of an older computer
  2. Every financial loan a company holds
  3. Only comments in source code
  4. Design/implementation choices that create future maintenance or change costs

Answer: D — Design/implementation choices that create future maintenance or change costs

Debt can be intentional or accidental and has consequences when work builds on it. The metaphor should be tied to concrete risks/costs rather than used as a vague label for disliked code.

07-M42 · Maintenance categories ★★ M MCQ

Updating a program to support a changed operating environment is mainly:

  1. An initial requirements interview
  2. Adaptive maintenance
  3. Deleting all tests
  4. Corrective maintenance only

Answer: B — Adaptive maintenance

Adaptive maintenance responds to changed platforms or external conditions. Corrective work fixes faults; perfective work improves capabilities or qualities, and preventive work reduces future problems.

07-M43 · Critical-path analysis ★★ M MCQ

In the activity-on-node network, what is the project duration?

  1. 10 days
  2. 9 days
  3. 12 days
  4. 14 days

Answer: C — 12 days

Path A-B-D takes 3+4+5=12 days; A-C-D takes 3+2+5=10. The longer dependency path controls the finish, assuming unlimited resources and the stated durations.

07-M44 · PERT estimation ★★ M MCQ

Using (optimistic + 4*most_likely + pessimistic)/6, estimate an activity with times 2,5,14 days.

  1. 7 days
  2. 21 days
  3. 6 days
  4. 5 days

Answer: C — 6 days

The weighted estimate is (2+4*5+14)/6=36/6=6. It is an estimate under the specified model, not a deadline guarantee.

07-M45 · Risk exposure ★★ M MCQ

If an event has probability 0.2 and loss 50,000 units when it occurs, what is its simple expected loss?

  1. 50,000 units
  2. 0.2 units
  3. 10,000 units
  4. 250,000 units

Answer: C — 10,000 units

Expected loss under this two-outcome model is probability times conditional loss: 0.2*50,000=10,000. This ignores risk aversion and other consequences unless separately modeled.

07-M46 · Availability ★★ M MCQ

A repairable service has MTBF=100 hours and MTTR=4 hours. Under availability=MTBF/(MTBF+MTTR), what is availability?

  1. Approximately 96.15%
  2. 100%
  3. 25%
  4. 4%

Answer: A — Approximately 96.15%

Compute 100/(100+4)=100/104≈0.961538. The meaning depends on consistent definitions of uptime-between-failures and repair time in the chosen model.

07-M47 · Defect density ★★ M MCQ

A module has 24 confirmed defects across 12 KLOC. What is defect density?

  1. 0.5 defects/KLOC
  2. 12 defects/KLOC
  3. 288 defects/KLOC
  4. 2 defects/KLOC

Answer: D — 2 defects/KLOC

Divide the defect count by size: 24/12=2. Comparing teams using this metric alone can be misleading because counting rules, testing effort and system context differ.

07-M48 · Acceptance testing ★★★ E MCQ

Acceptance testing primarily evaluates:

  1. Only the compiler’s syntax parser
  2. Only individual variable assignments
  3. Whether every developer used the same keyboard
  4. Whether agreed acceptance conditions for the intended use are met

Answer: D — Whether agreed acceptance conditions for the intended use are met

Acceptance criteria should reflect stakeholder requirements and relevant operating conditions. Acceptance is not automatically established by passing unit tests alone.

07-M49 · Idempotent operations ★★ M MCQ

An idempotent operation has which property for its intended effect?

  1. It never performs any work
  2. It cannot return an error
  3. It must be faster on every retry
  4. Repeating the same operation has the same effect as applying it once.

Answer: D — Repeating the same operation has the same effect as applying it once.

Idempotency helps control retry effects, such as setting an address to a specified value. Incrementing a balance without a deduplication key is not inherently idempotent.

07-M50 · Design for concurrency ★★ M MCQ

Two customers attempt to buy the final item simultaneously. Which design directly protects the stock invariant?

  1. Checking stock in the browser only
  2. A transactional conditional decrement that succeeds only when stock remains positive
  3. Showing a warning after accepting unlimited orders
  4. Caching stock forever with no coordination

Answer: B — A transactional conditional decrement that succeeds only when stock remains positive

The check and update must be coordinated atomically under the data system’s guarantees. Separate unprotected reads followed by writes allow both callers to act on the same old stock value.

07-W01 · Requirements and acceptance criteria ★★★ M WRITTEN

Replace “the admission portal should be fast and secure” with two measurable requirements and matching acceptance tests. State assumptions rather than claiming universal security.

Worked answer

Performance requirement: on a defined dataset of 50,000 applications and 200 concurrent authenticated users, at least 95% of status queries complete within 2 seconds measured from request receipt to response completion, over a stated 30-minute test.

Access-control requirement: an applicant may view only applications authorized for that account; an unauthorized identifier must return a denial without disclosing another applicant’s data. Administrative access is separately role-controlled.

RequirementAcceptance test
PerformanceRun the defined workload; measure p95 with timing boundaries and environment recorded
AuthorizationAttempt own, another user’s and nonexistent identifiers; inspect responses and audit records

These are examples of specific guarantees, not proof of total security. Availability, privacy, recovery and other threats need separate requirements and tests.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

07-W02 · Boundary-value and partition tests ★★★ M WRITTEN

An API accepts integer age 18..60 inclusive and rejects all other values. Design equivalence classes and boundary tests. Add tests for missing/noninteger input if the interface can receive them.

Worked answer

ClassRepresentativeExpected
Below range17Reject
Valid range30Accept
Above range61Reject
Missingabsent fieldReject under stated API policy
Noninteger"twenty" or 18.5Reject under integer-only policy

Boundary set: 17,18,19,59,60,61. Expected results are reject, accept, accept, accept, accept, reject. Specify whether a numeric string such as "18" is parsed or rejected; do not silently assume coercion.

Partitioning samples behaviours; boundary testing targets transitions between them. Both may miss defects unrelated to the chosen classes, so include error handling, authorization and integration tests where relevant.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

07-W03 · Decision-table testing ★★ M WRITTEN

A shop grants free delivery when order value is at least 100 OR the customer is a premium member. Build the complete Boolean decision table and select tests at the amount boundary.

Worked answer

Premium?Amount≥100?Free delivery?Example amount
NoNoNo99
NoYesYes100
YesNoYes99
YesYesYes100

The rule is Premium OR HighAmount. Add amounts just above the boundary and invalid-input tests separately if needed. A test suite containing only premium members never checks the amount decision for nonpremium customers.

The table is complete for the two Boolean conditions: 2²=4 combinations. Real monetary precision, currency and whether taxes count toward the threshold must be specified by the requirement before implementation.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

07-W04 · Control-flow graph and coverage ★★★ H WRITTEN

Draw a CFG for the pseudocode. Give the cyclomatic complexity and a small set of tests achieving branch coverage; treat each if as one decision.

Pseudocode
if x > 0:
    y = 1
else:
    y = -1
if x % 2 == 0:
    y = y + 2
return y

Worked answer

There are two binary decisions, so cyclomatic complexity is 2+1=3. Branch coverage does not require exercising every combination of the two decisions. Tests x=2 and x=-1 cover true/true and false/false, respectively, and therefore each branch outcome.

InputFirst decisionSecond decisionOutput
2TrueTrue3
-1FalseFalse-1

An additional test x=1 exercises true/false, and x=-2 exercises false/true. Full path combinations and basis-path criteria differ from branch coverage; do not equate the minimum branch-covering test count with cyclomatic complexity.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

07-W05 · UML class design ★★★ M WRITTEN

Design a library borrowing model with Member, BookCopy and Loan. A member has many loans over time; each loan links exactly one member and one copy. Draw a class diagram with multiplicities and state a rule that multiplicities alone do not express.

Worked answer

Member has MemberID; BookCopy has CopyID; Loan has LoanID, issuedAt and returnedAt. A member has 0..* historical loans, and a copy has 0..* historical loans. Each Loan refers to exactly one Member and one BookCopy.

The rule “a book copy has at most one active loan” depends on returnedAt being absent and is not captured by unrestricted historical multiplicities alone. Enforce it with a suitable invariant/transactional constraint. Book title and physical copy should not be confused when several copies of one title exist.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

07-W06 · UML sequence diagram ★★★ M WRITTEN

Draw a sequence diagram for successful course registration involving Student, Portal, RegistrationService and Database. Include authorization/capacity checking and explain where the seat check and reservation must be coordinated.

Worked answer

Student submits a course choice; the portal forwards the authenticated identity. RegistrationService checks eligibility and starts a transaction that verifies/reserves capacity and inserts the enrolment. The database commits; the service returns confirmation through the portal.

An independent “read remaining seats” followed much later by an unprotected insert permits overbooking under concurrency. Seat validation and reservation must be atomic under the selected database design. Rejections and retry/error paths are omitted from this successful-path diagram and should be modeled separately.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

07-W07 · Data-flow diagram ★★ M WRITTEN

Draw a level-0-style DFD for submitting and checking an admission application. Include Applicant, Process Application, Application Store and Admissions Office. Label data flows; explain why the diagram is not a timing diagram.

Worked answer

Applicant sends application data to Process Application. The process stores/retrieves records in Application Store and returns acknowledgment/status. Admissions Office supplies review decisions and receives application summaries through the process.

A DFD describes information movement and transformation. It does not specify the exact order or timing of message exchanges; use sequence/activity models for those concerns. Direct entity-to-store shortcuts should be avoided under the conventional DFD modelling rules used here.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

07-W08 · Project scheduling and critical path ★★ M WRITTEN

For the shown network, calculate ES, EF, LS, LF and slack for all tasks. A starts at day 0; durations are in days, and resource limits are ignored.

Worked answer

TaskESEFLSLFSlack
A03030
B37370
C35572
D7127120

Forward pass: D starts at max(EF_B,EF_C)=max(7,5)=7. Backward pass: D must start by 7; B and C must finish by 7. Slack is LS-ES. Critical path A-B-D lasts 12 days. C has two days of slack under this schedule.

A resource-constrained schedule or uncertain durations may change the completion estimate. A critical path is a dependency calculation, not a guarantee of actual completion.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

07-W09 · PERT and expected risk ★★ M WRITTEN

An activity has optimistic=2, most likely=5, pessimistic=14 days. Using PERT mean (a+4m+b)/6 and variance ((b-a)/6)^2, calculate both. Separately estimate expected loss for a 20% risk of a 50,000-unit impact.

Worked answer

PERT mean=(2+4*5+14)/6=36/6=6 days. Standard-deviation estimate=(14-2)/6=2 days, and variance is 4 days².

Expected loss=0.20*50,000=10,000 units. The monetary expected loss is separate from the activity’s duration variance; their units must not be mixed.

These formulas are modelling approximations. Project-wide estimates require dependence and path considerations; adding variances assumes independence in the relevant approximation. A low expected loss can still conceal a rare catastrophic outcome requiring additional judgment.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

07-W10 · Test strategy and integration ★★★ M WRITTEN

For an online payment confirmation flow, propose unit, integration, system and acceptance tests. Distinguish a payment-provider stub from a production payment, and include one retry scenario.

Worked answer

LevelExample
UnitValidate amount/currency and order-state transition logic
IntegrationExercise signed provider callback parsing with DB update
SystemPlace an order and complete a sandbox payment end to end
AcceptanceDemonstrate agreed customer receipt and failure behaviours

A provider stub returns controlled responses and can simulate timeout, duplicate callback and invalid signature. It does not prove the real provider integration behaves identically, so controlled sandbox integration is also needed.

Retry test: deliver the same valid callback twice and verify the order is marked paid once and no duplicate fulfilment occurs. Use a stable event identifier and atomic deduplication/state transition. Never use real customer funds merely as an uncontrolled test shortcut.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

07-W11 · Regression and defect reporting ★★★ M WRITTEN

A fix for accepting age 17 accidentally rejects age 18. Write a concise defect report and propose confirmation and regression tests.

Worked answer

Title: lower-bound fix rejects valid age 18. Environment: specify build, endpoint and configuration. Preconditions: authenticated user and otherwise valid payload. Steps: submit age=18. Expected: accepted under inclusive 18..60 rule. Actual: rejected as out of range. Include response and sanitized evidence.

Confirmation of this new defect: resubmit age 18 after repair and verify acceptance. Regression around the original fix: 17 must remain rejected; 19,59,60 accepted; 61 rejected. Include unrelated valid fields and supported input encodings as appropriate.

Severity describes user/business impact; priority reflects scheduling urgency. Avoid treating a screenshot alone as a reproducible report or including unnecessary personal data in attached logs.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

07-W12 · Refactoring and cohesion ★★ M WRITTEN

A single component validates student identity, computes GPA, formats PDFs and sends emails. Propose a modular redesign and an incremental refactoring strategy that preserves behaviour.

Worked answer

Separate IdentityValidation, GradeCalculation, TranscriptRendering and Notification behind small interfaces. Keep a workflow service to coordinate them. This groups responsibilities by purpose and likely reason for change rather than simply moving arbitrary lines into new files.

First characterize existing behaviour with tests, especially edge cases. Extract one responsibility at a time, preserve its interface contract, run tests, then remove duplicated paths. Compare generated documents and messages with appropriate normalization where nondeterministic timestamps exist.

Use dependency injection or explicit parameter passing where it improves testability. Do not silently alter grading rules while calling the change a refactor; an intended behaviour change needs separate requirements and acceptance checks.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

07-W13 · Information-system transaction design ★★ M WRITTEN

Design the operation “reserve the final seat” so two simultaneous valid applicants cannot both obtain a single remaining seat. Give SQL-like pseudocode and the invariant.

Worked answer

Transactional pseudocode
BEGIN;
UPDATE Offering
SET RemainingSeats = RemainingSeats - 1
WHERE OfferingID = :id AND RemainingSeats > 0;
if affected_rows != 1:
    ROLLBACK; return FULL;
INSERT INTO Enrollment(ApplicantID, OfferingID)
VALUES (:applicant, :id);
COMMIT;

Invariant: RemainingSeats never becomes negative and successful enrolment corresponds to one reserved seat. Use a unique constraint on the intended enrolment identity to prevent duplicates, and roll back the decrement if insertion fails.

The DBMS must provide appropriate atomic update/concurrency guarantees. Handle conflicts and retry policy explicitly. Reading the count in application code and decrementing later without coordination does not enforce the invariant.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

07-W14 · Quality metrics and interpretation ★★ M WRITTEN

A service has 100 hours mean uptime between failures and 4 hours mean repair time. A 12-KLOC module has 24 confirmed defects. Compute availability and defect density, then explain two limitations of comparing products only through these numbers.

Worked answer

Using the stated availability model: 100/(100+4)=0.961538≈96.15%. Defect density=24/12=2 defects per KLOC.

Limitation 1: counting and exposure differ. More testing may discover more defects without meaning the product is intrinsically worse; LOC is not a uniform functionality measure across languages/designs. Limitation 2: impact differs. One severe authorization defect can matter more than many minor cosmetic issues.

Availability also depends on what counts as downtime, measurement windows and operating conditions. Report definitions with metrics and combine them with user/business consequences rather than turning a single figure into an overall quality verdict.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

07-W15 · Change impact and traceability ★★ M WRITTEN

An admission requirement changes from “one programme application” to “multiple applications with a preference order.” Describe the impact analysis across requirements, data, UI, testing and deployment. Do not assume this is an actual new BUET policy.

Worked answer

Requirements: define maximum choices, editing deadlines, payment rules and allocation behaviour. Data: represent applicant-to-programme applications and an ordered preference list with integrity constraints. UI/API: collect and validate choices without accidental duplicates.

Tests: cover one/multiple choices, duplicate selections, invalid programme eligibility, reordering, concurrent edits, payment associations and final submission. Trace each changed rule to its implementation and verification evidence.

Deployment: plan schema migration, backward compatibility, audit logging and recovery/rollback. Existing applicants need a defined transition policy. This is an original system-design scenario, not an administrative claim about the current university application process.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

08 · Digital Logic Design, Microprocessors and Microcontrollers

Boolean diagrams use named gates and labelled wires. A prime or overbar means NOT; minterm indices use A as the most significant variable unless stated otherwise. Processor questions identify the model. Generic timer problems explicitly state clock division rather than assuming every microcontroller behaves like one legacy family.

Coverage: Number systems and codes; Boolean algebra, truth tables and K-maps; combinational circuits; flip-flops, timing, counters, registers and FSMs; memory/addressing; 8086 basics; microcontroller timers, interrupts, ADC and peripherals.

Reading references: R18, R19, R20

08-M01 · Number systems ★★★ E MCQ

What is hexadecimal B6 in decimal?

  1. 186
  2. 182
  3. 176
  4. 166

Answer: B — 182

B represents 11, so B6=11*16+6=182. Positional weights are powers of 16.

08-M02 · Binary fractions ★★ M MCQ

What is binary 0.101 in decimal?

  1. 0.5
  2. 0.625
  3. 0.75
  4. 0.101

Answer: B — 0.625

The weights after the binary point are 1/2,1/4,1/8. Thus 0.101₂=1/2+0+1/8=0.625.

08-M03 · Two’s complement ★★★ E MCQ

What range is represented by 4-bit two’s-complement integers?

  1. -8 through 7
  2. -8 through 8
  3. -7 through 7
  4. 0 through 15

Answer: A — -8 through 7

For n bits, the range is -2^(n-1) through 2^(n-1)-1. With n=4 this is -8..7. There is only one representation of zero.

08-M04 · Two’s-complement conversion ★★★ M MCQ

What is the 8-bit two’s-complement representation of -5?

  1. 11111010
  2. 10000101
  3. 11111011
  4. 00000101

Answer: C — 11111011

Write +5 as 00000101, invert to 11111010 and add one to get 11111011. The most significant bit has negative weight in two’s complement.

08-M05 · Signed overflow ★★★ M MCQ

In 4-bit two’s complement, adding 0111 and 0011 gives bit pattern 1010. What happened?

  1. No overflow; 1010 represents +10 in this signed format.
  2. Only subtraction can overflow.
  3. Both inputs were negative.
  4. Signed overflow; the mathematical sum 10 is outside -8..7.

Answer: D — Signed overflow; the mathematical sum 10 is outside -8..7.

Two positive signed operands produced a negative-sign result because 10 exceeds the maximum 7. The bit pattern interpreted in this format is -6, not the correct mathematical sum.

08-M06 · Unsigned carry ★★★ M MCQ

An 8-bit unsigned adder adds 250 and 10. What are the low 8-bit result and carry-out?

  1. 255 and 1
  2. 260 and 0
  3. 4 and 1
  4. 4 and 0

Answer: C — 4 and 1

The exact sum is 260=256+4. Modulo 256 the result is 4 and the ninth bit is the carry. Unsigned carry and signed overflow are different flags.

08-M07 · BCD codes ★★ M MCQ

What is the 8-bit packed BCD representation of decimal 59?

  1. 0011 1011
  2. 0101 1001
  3. 0101 1010
  4. 1001 0101

Answer: B — 0101 1001

Encode each decimal digit separately: 5→0101 and 9→1001. The binary encoding of the whole number is different from packed BCD.

08-M08 · Gray codes ★★ M MCQ

Convert binary 1011 to reflected Gray code using g=b XOR (b shifted right by 1).

  1. 0101
  2. 1101
  3. 1010
  4. 1110

Answer: D — 1110

1011 XOR 0101 = 1110. Reflected Gray code changes one bit between consecutive encoded values, which is useful in certain position/counter interfaces.

08-M09 · Boolean algebra ★★★ E MCQ

Simplify A + AB, where + is OR and juxtaposition is AND.

  1. AB
  2. A+B
  3. B
  4. A

Answer: D — A

By absorption, if A is true then the output is true regardless of B; if A is false, both terms are false. Thus A+AB=A.

08-M10 · De Morgan laws ★★★ E MCQ

What is NOT(A AND B)?

  1. A OR B
  2. A AND (NOT B)
  3. (NOT A) OR (NOT B)
  4. (NOT A) AND (NOT B)

Answer: C — (NOT A) OR (NOT B)

De Morgan’s law complements each operand and exchanges AND with OR. Checking A=B=1 also rules out the alternatives.

08-M11 · Boolean simplification ★★★ E MCQ

Simplify AB + AB′.

  1. A+B
  2. A
  3. B
  4. 0

Answer: B — A

Factor A: A(B+B′)=A*1=A. B and its complement cover both cases.

08-M12 · Exclusive OR ★★★ E MCQ

A two-input XOR gate outputs 1 when:

  1. At least one input is 1, including both
  2. Exactly one input is 1
  3. Both inputs are 1
  4. Both inputs are 0

Answer: B — Exactly one input is 1

XOR detects unequal Boolean values. OR also accepts the both-one case, so the two operations are not interchangeable.

08-M13 · Universal gates ★★★ M MCQ

Which gate type alone can implement every Boolean function?

  1. A wire only
  2. A one-input buffer only
  3. NAND
  4. XOR only

Answer: C — NAND

NAND can form NOT by tying inputs, then AND and OR through combinations and De Morgan’s law. NOR is also universal, but is not among the other options.

08-M14 · Minterms and truth tables ★★★ M MCQ

For variables A,B,C, F=Σm(1,3,5,7). What is F?

  1. A
  2. B
  3. C′
  4. C

Answer: D — C

Every listed minterm has least-significant variable C=1 while A and B cover all combinations. The function therefore depends only on C.

08-M15 · K-map grouping ★★★ M MCQ

A valid K-map group contains how many cells?

  1. A power of two, with adjacent cells including wraparound where appropriate
  2. Exactly three cells
  3. Any odd count
  4. Only diagonal pairs

Answer: A — A power of two, with adjacent cells including wraparound where appropriate

Groups represent combinations in which variables can be eliminated. Sizes 1,2,4,8,... correspond to powers of two. Diagonal proximity alone is not Boolean adjacency.

08-M16 · K-map minimization ★★★ M MCQ

For A,B,C, minimize F=Σm(0,1,4,5).

  1. A′
  2. B′
  3. AB
  4. C

Answer: B — B′

The four cells cover both values of A and C but always have B=0. Their group leaves only B′. The minterm convention matters.

08-M17 · Multiplexers ★★★ E MCQ

How many select bits does an 8-to-1 multiplexer require?

  1. 4
  2. 3
  3. 2
  4. 8

Answer: B — 3

Three bits encode eight distinct input selections, because 2^3=8. This does not count the eight data inputs or an optional enable pin.

08-M18 · MUX implementation ★★ M MCQ

For a 2-to-1 MUX, output is A′I0+AI1. To implement A XOR B using select A, choose:

  1. I0=A, I1=A′
  2. I0=B, I1=B′
  3. I0=B, I1=B
  4. I0=0, I1=1

Answer: B — I0=B, I1=B′

When A=0, XOR must equal B; when A=1, it must equal B′. These are exactly the two selected data inputs.

08-M19 · Decoders ★★★ E MCQ

A 3-to-8 active-high decoder receives input 101 with enable asserted. Which output is high?

  1. D3
  2. D5
  3. D6
  4. D7

Answer: B — D5

Binary 101 equals decimal 5, so the corresponding decoded output is D5. The active-high and enable assumptions are explicitly given.

08-M20 · Half adders ★★★ E MCQ

For a half adder with inputs A,B, what are sum and carry?

  1. A+B, and A XOR B
  2. A XOR B, and AB
  3. A XNOR B, and A′B′
  4. AB, and A+B

Answer: B — A XOR B, and AB

The sum is the low bit of adding two single bits, hence XOR. A carry is generated only when both inputs are one.

08-M21 · Full adders ★★★ M MCQ

For a full adder, which expression gives carry-out?

  1. ABCin only
  2. A XOR B XOR Cin
  3. AB + ACin + BCin
  4. A+B+Cin

Answer: C — AB + ACin + BCin

Carry-out is one when at least two of the three input bits are one. The XOR of all three instead gives the sum output.

08-M22 · Ripple carry delay ★★ M MCQ

An 8-stage ripple-carry path has 20 ns carry propagation per stage. Ignoring other delays, what is the worst-case carry-chain delay?

  1. 80 ns
  2. 160 ns
  3. 20 ns
  4. 400 ns

Answer: B — 160 ns

A carry may propagate through all eight stages, contributing 8*20=160 ns under the stated simple model. Real timing distinguishes input-to-carry and sum delays.

08-M23 · Combinational versus sequential ★★★ E MCQ

A sequential circuit differs from a combinational circuit because it can depend on:

  1. Stored state as well as current inputs
  2. Only the number of outputs
  3. Only the physical wire colour
  4. No inputs at all in every case

Answer: A — Stored state as well as current inputs

Memory/state lets past events influence present behaviour. A combinational circuit’s logical output depends only on current inputs, apart from propagation transients.

08-M24 · Latches and flip-flops ★★★ M MCQ

A level-sensitive latch and an edge-triggered flip-flop differ mainly in:

  1. Whether Boolean algebra applies
  2. Whether binary values exist
  3. Whether any output wire is possible
  4. When their state can respond to an input under the controlling signal

Answer: D — When their state can respond to an input under the controlling signal

A latch can be transparent during an active level. An edge-triggered flip-flop samples around a designated clock edge, subject to setup/hold constraints.

08-M25 · D flip-flops ★★★ E MCQ

For an ideal positive-edge D flip-flop without active asynchronous controls, Q after the active edge equals:

  1. Q XOR D always
  2. The complement of D always
  3. The previous clock level
  4. D sampled at that edge

Answer: D — D sampled at that edge

D specifies the next stored bit at the sampling edge. Between edges the stored output remains unchanged in the ideal model.

08-M26 · T flip-flops ★★★ E MCQ

A T flip-flop has T=1 at each active clock edge. Its Q:

  1. Copies the clock level continuously
  2. Always becomes 1 and stays there
  3. Always becomes 0
  4. Toggles at each edge

Answer: D — Toggles at each edge

The next-state equation is Qnext=Q XOR T. With T=1 it complements each time, producing a divide-by-two waveform under regular clocking.

08-M27 · JK flip-flops ★★★ M MCQ

For an ideal JK flip-flop, J=K=1 at an active edge produces:

  1. Hold
  2. Set only
  3. Reset only
  4. Toggle

Answer: D — Toggle

The characteristic table gives hold for 00, reset for 01, set for 10, and toggle for 11. Edge-triggered/ideal assumptions avoid level-triggered race-around complications.

08-M28 · SR latches ★★ M MCQ

For a cross-coupled NOR active-high SR latch, which input pair is forbidden in the usual truth table?

  1. S=1,R=0
  2. S=1,R=1
  3. S=0,R=0
  4. S=0,R=1

Answer: B — S=1,R=1

Asserting both NOR latch inputs drives both outputs low and violates their complementary interpretation. Releasing simultaneously can yield an indeterminate final state. NAND active-low latches use a different forbidden pair.

08-M29 · Setup and hold timing ★★ M MCQ

Setup time is the interval during which data must be stable:

  1. Only after the edge
  2. Before the sampling clock edge
  3. Only when power is off
  4. For exactly one entire clock period in every device

Answer: B — Before the sampling clock edge

Hold time is the corresponding required interval after the edge. Violating either can prevent reliable sampling and may cause metastability.

08-M30 · Metastability ★★ M MCQ

A two-flip-flop synchronizer for a single asynchronous control signal primarily:

  1. Mathematically eliminates all failure probability
  2. Reduces the probability that metastability propagates into downstream logic
  3. Makes the input clock faster
  4. Synchronizes arbitrary multi-bit buses without a protocol

Answer: B — Reduces the probability that metastability propagates into downstream logic

Additional settling time reduces failure probability for the intended signal use. Multi-bit data transfers require coherence protocols; independent bit synchronizers do not guarantee a consistent word.

08-M31 · Counters ★★★ E MCQ

What is the minimum number of binary storage bits needed to encode 10 distinct counter states?

  1. 5
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 10

Answer: C — 4

Three bits encode only eight states; four encode sixteen. A mod-10 design uses ten of them and must define handling of unused states.

08-M32 · Counter frequency division ★★ M MCQ

A 3-bit binary counter advances once per 1 kHz clock cycle. What is the frequency of its most significant bit?

  1. 125 Hz
  2. 1000 Hz
  3. 250 Hz
  4. 500 Hz

Answer: A — 125 Hz

The three bits divide clock frequency by 2,4,8. The MSB therefore has 1000/8=125 Hz, assuming the ordinary repeating binary sequence.

08-M33 · Shift registers ★★ M MCQ

A 4-bit register contains 1010. It shifts right once, inserting serial input 1 at the left. What is its new content?

  1. 1011
  2. 1101
  3. 0101
  4. 0110

Answer: B — 1101

Move the existing upper three bits right and place the new bit at the most significant position: 1 followed by 101 gives 1101. The old rightmost zero is discarded.

08-M34 · Finite-state machines ★★★ E MCQ

A Mealy-machine output can depend on:

  1. Current state and current input
  2. No transition conditions
  3. Only the current state by definition
  4. Only the total number of states

Answer: A — Current state and current input

Moore outputs depend only on state. Mealy outputs can change with input within a state, which affects timing and design choices.

08-M35 · State encoding ★★ M MCQ

A binary encoding for 5 states needs at least how many flip-flops?

  1. 5
  2. 1
  3. 2
  4. 3

Answer: D — 3

The number of representable bit patterns must be at least five: ceil(log2 5)=3. One-hot encoding would use five flip-flops, but the question asks binary encoding.

08-M36 · ROM implementation ★★ M MCQ

A ROM realizes a combinational function with 4 inputs and 3 outputs. How many stored output bits are required?

  1. 48
  2. 16
  3. 12
  4. 64

Answer: A — 48

Four input bits address 2^4=16 words. Each word stores three output bits, giving 16*3=48 bits, ignoring physical overhead.

08-M37 · Memory addressing ★★★ E MCQ

A byte-addressed processor has 20 address bits. What is the maximum directly addressable space?

  1. 20 MiB
  2. 1 MiB
  3. 1 Mbit
  4. 64 KiB

Answer: B — 1 MiB

Twenty bits select 2^20 distinct byte locations, which is 1 MiB. Address width and data-bus width are separate quantities.

08-M38 · Memory-chip organization ★★ M MCQ

How many 4K×8 memory chips are needed to build 16K×16 memory?

  1. 2
  2. 8
  3. 4
  4. 16

Answer: B — 8

Depth expansion is 16K/4K=4 banks, and width expansion is 16/8=2 chips per bank. Total chips=4*2=8.

08-M39 · 8086 segmented addresses ★★★ M MCQ

For an 8086-style physical address calculation, segment=1234h and offset=0010h. What is the physical address?

  1. 12440h
  2. 12340010h
  3. 12350h
  4. 12344h

Answer: C — 12350h

Physical address=segment*16+offset=12340h+0010h=12350h. This is the explicitly stated 8086 real-mode-style model, not a generic rule for every modern addressing mode.

08-M40 · 8086 addressing modes ★★ M MCQ

In an 8086-style operand [BX+SI+8], which quantity forms the effective offset?

  1. BX + SI + displacement 8
  2. The value stored at AX
  3. DS*16 only
  4. IP + SP

Answer: A — BX + SI + displacement 8

The base/index/displacement are combined to form an offset, then the appropriate segment base participates in physical-address formation. This question asks for the offset, not the fetched operand value.

08-M41 · Endianness ★★★ E MCQ

A 16-bit value 1234h is stored little-endian at addresses 1000 and 1001. Which bytes appear there?

  1. 34h at 1000, 12h at 1001
  2. Both bytes are 34h
  3. 12h at 1000, 34h at 1001
  4. 01h at 1000, 23h at 1001

Answer: A — 34h at 1000, 12h at 1001

Little-endian stores the least significant byte at the lower address. The numeric value does not change; only byte order in memory is specified.

08-M42 · Flags ★★ E MCQ

An arithmetic operation produces zero in its destination. Which conventional flag is set to indicate that result?

  1. Carry flag necessarily
  2. Interrupt enable flag necessarily
  3. Overflow flag necessarily
  4. Zero flag

Answer: D — Zero flag

A zero-result indicator does not imply carry or signed overflow. Flag meanings must be interpreted according to the processor and operation.

08-M43 · Interrupts ★★★ M MCQ

An interrupt service routine should normally preserve which execution context as required by the calling/interrupt convention?

  1. Every file on disk regardless of use
  2. No state under any circumstances
  3. Registers and state it would otherwise corrupt before resuming interrupted code
  4. Only the screen brightness

Answer: C — Registers and state it would otherwise corrupt before resuming interrupted code

Some context is saved automatically by hardware and some by software, depending on the architecture. Correct restoration lets interrupted execution continue consistently.

08-M44 · Polling versus interrupts ★★ M MCQ

Compared with continuously polling a peripheral, interrupts can:

  1. Remove the need for a peripheral interface
  2. Allow the CPU to do other work until a relevant event occurs
  3. Eliminate all context-switch overhead
  4. Guarantee every response has zero latency

Answer: B — Allow the CPU to do other work until a relevant event occurs

Interrupts notify rather than requiring continuous checking. They have handling overhead and prioritization/timing considerations; they are not universally better for every workload.

08-M45 · Microcontrollers ★★★ E MCQ

A microcontroller typically integrates which components on one chip?

  1. Only an arithmetic gate
  2. Only a display cable
  3. Only an external hard drive
  4. CPU, memory and peripheral interfaces

Answer: D — CPU, memory and peripheral interfaces

Integration suits embedded control tasks. The exact memory sizes, peripherals and architecture vary; “microcontroller” does not specify one fixed instruction set.

08-M46 · Timers ★★ M MCQ

A timer receives a 1 MHz input clock with no prescaling. How many ticks correspond to 2 ms?

  1. 2,000,000
  2. 2
  3. 2000
  4. 200

Answer: C — 2000

The tick period is 1 microsecond. Two milliseconds equals 2000 microseconds, hence 2000 ticks. Do not insert an unstated divide-by-12 convention.

08-M47 · Timer preload ★★ M MCQ

An unsigned 16-bit up-counter overflows after 65536 counts. To overflow after 1000 ticks, what preload is needed?

  1. 1000
  2. 65535
  3. 64536
  4. 65536

Answer: C — 64536

Starting at 65536-1000=64536 leaves exactly 1000 increments until wraparound. This assumes the explicit counter model and no extra software timing delays.

08-M48 · ADC resolution ★★ M MCQ

An ideal 10-bit ADC spans 0 to 5 V using 1024 equal quantization intervals. What is interval width?

  1. Approximately 4.883 mV
  2. Approximately 9.766 mV
  3. 0.4883 V
  4. 5 mV exactly

Answer: A — Approximately 4.883 mV

The explicitly stated interval convention gives 5/1024 V≈0.0048828125 V. Some endpoint specifications use a different code-to-voltage convention, so the model is stated.

08-M49 · PWM ★★ E MCQ

A PWM signal is high for 2 ms in a 10 ms period. What is its duty cycle?

  1. 80%
  2. 20%
  3. 50%
  4. 5%

Answer: B — 20%

Duty cycle is high time divided by period: 2/10=0.2. Frequency is a separate quantity, here 1/0.01 s=100 Hz.

08-M50 · Watchdog timers ★★ M MCQ

A watchdog timer is primarily used to:

  1. Trigger recovery/reset when expected periodic servicing stops
  2. Make every interrupt nonmaskable
  3. Provide cryptographic random keys by definition
  4. Store an entire filesystem

Answer: A — Trigger recovery/reset when expected periodic servicing stops

Software services the watchdog during healthy execution. Failure to do so can indicate a hang and trigger recovery. A poor servicing design can mask faults, so it is not a complete reliability solution.

08-W01 · Number representations and overflow ★★★ M WRITTEN

Represent -5 in 8-bit two’s complement. Then add 7 and 3 using 4-bit two’s complement, show the bit addition, and distinguish signed overflow from unsigned carry.

Worked answer

+5 is 00000101. Invert to 11111010 and add one: -5 is 11111011.

Bit addition
  0111   (+7)
+ 0011   (+3)
------
  1010   (-6 in 4-bit two’s complement)

The true sum +10 is outside [-8,7], so signed overflow occurred. Both operands had sign 0 and the result has sign 1. There is no fifth-bit unsigned carry in 7+3=10, because 10 fits in four unsigned bits. Signed overflow and carry-out diagnose different range interpretations.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

08-W02 · Boolean simplification and gates ★★★ M WRITTEN

Simplify F=AB+AB′+A′C. Draw a gate circuit for the simplified expression and verify using a truth table.

Worked answer

AB+AB′=A(B+B′)=A. Therefore F=A+A′C=(A+A′)(A+C)=A+C. B does not affect the output.

ABCF
0000
0011
0100
0111
1001
1011
1101
1111

The table shows F is false only when A and C are both false, regardless of B. This matches the single OR gate.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

08-W03 · Karnaugh maps ★★★ M WRITTEN

Minimize F(A,B,C)=Σm(0,1,4,5,7) using a 3-variable K-map. Draw the map with Gray-code columns, identify groups and give a minimal SOP expression.

Worked answer

Place ones at 0,1,4,5,7. Group minterms {0,1,4,5}: B is always 0 while A,C vary, yielding B′. The remaining minterm 7 can pair with 5: A=1 and C=1 are fixed, yielding AC.

Thus F=B′+AC. The terms cover all ones and no zeros. The four-cell group spans columns 00 and 01 across both rows; the pair covers row A=1 at columns 01 and 11. Overlapping groups are allowed.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

08-W04 · NAND-only implementation ★★ M WRITTEN

Implement F=A+B using only 2-input NAND gates. Draw the circuit and derive the expression.

Worked answer

Use De Morgan: A+B=(A′B′)′. Make A′ with NAND(A,A) and B′ with NAND(B,B), then NAND their outputs.

If A=B=0, the inverter outputs are both 1 and the final NAND gives 0. If either input is 1, at least one inverter output is 0 and the final output is 1. This verifies OR behaviour. The gate count here is for two-input NAND primitives without a separate NOT primitive.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

08-W05 · Full-adder design ★★★ M WRITTEN

Derive sum and carry-out of a full adder from its truth table. Draw an implementation using two half-adder stages and an OR gate.

Worked answer

ABCinSumCout
00000
00110
01010
01101
10010
10101
11001
11111

Sum=A XOR B XOR Cin. Carry-out is the majority function AB+ACin+BCin, equivalently AB+Cin(A XOR B).

The two stages generate intermediate s1=A XOR B and c1=AB, then Sum=s1 XOR Cin and c2=s1*Cin. Cout=c1+c2.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

08-W06 · Multiplexer synthesis ★★ M WRITTEN

Implement F(A,B)=A XOR B using a 2-to-1 multiplexer and one inverter. Draw and explain both selector cases.

Worked answer

A 2-to-1 MUX outputs A′I0+AI1. Substituting I0=B and I1=B′ gives A′B+AB′, the XOR sum-of-products form.

ASelected inputOutput
0I0B
1I1B′

The inverter generates B′. The select bit should not be confused with one of the data input values. For A=B=1 the output is I1=0, distinguishing XOR from OR.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

08-W07 · Flip-flop tracing ★★★ M WRITTEN

A positive-edge D flip-flop starts with Q=0. The D values stable at five successive active edges are 1,0,0,1,1. Draw a labelled sampled-value timing diagram and give Q after each edge. Assume setup/hold are met.

Worked answer

Edge12345
D sampled10011
Q after edge10011

Q copies the sampled D at each positive edge and holds between edges in the ideal model. The initial state is zero until the first active edge. Real devices have clock-to-Q delay and setup/hold requirements; the ideal diagram does not claim zero physical propagation time.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

08-W08 · Synchronous counters ★★ H WRITTEN

Design a 2-bit synchronous binary up-counter using T flip-flops. Give state transitions, T-input equations and a block circuit.

Worked answer

Q1Q0 nowQ1Q0 nextT1T0
000101
011011
101101
110011

Q0 toggles every clock, so T0=1. Q1 toggles exactly when the old Q0 is 1, so T1=Q0. Both flip-flops share the same clock.

The sequence is 00→01→10→11→00. With initial reset 00, the design is a modulo-4 up-counter. Ripple counters use a different clocking arrangement and have different transient behaviour.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

08-W09 · Finite-state machine design ★★ M WRITTEN

Design a Moore machine that outputs 1 exactly when the number of 1s seen so far is odd. Give states, output values, transitions and a diagram.

Worked answer

Use E for even count and O for odd count. Start E because the empty input has zero ones. Output(E)=0; Output(O)=1. On 0 stay in the same state; on 1 toggle.

StateOutputNext on 0Next on 1
E0EO
O1OE

For input 1011 the states after symbols are O,O,E,O, with outputs 1,1,0,1. The output is associated with state rather than directly with the transition label.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

08-W10 · Memory-chip organization ★★ H WRITTEN

Build 16K×16 memory using 4K×8 chips. Calculate the chip count, address lines per chip and bank-select bits. Draw the bank organization.

Worked answer

Depth factor=16K/4K=4 banks. Width factor=16/8=2 chips in parallel per bank. Total=4*2=8 chips. Each 4K chip uses 12 local address bits; the whole memory has 14 word-address bits.

A two-to-four decoder generates bank enables. Within a bank, one chip drives D7:D0 and the other D15:D8. Read/write control applies consistently. This is a word-organized design; adding byte-addressability and byte enables would require the interface convention to be specified.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

08-W11 · 8086 address calculations ★★★ M WRITTEN

For an 8086-style data reference, DS=1000h, BX=0020h, SI=0004h and displacement=0008h. Find the effective offset and physical address of [BX+SI+8]. If a 16-bit value 1234h is stored there, show the bytes.

Worked answer

Effective offset=0020h+0004h+0008h=002Ch. Segment base=1000h*10h=10000h. Physical address=10000h+002Ch=1002Ch.

AddressStored byte
1002Ch34h
1002Dh12h

The byte order is little-endian. Offset calculation and memory contents are separate: the effective address is not the value stored there. This uses the explicitly stated 8086-style segment calculation without modern protected/long-mode assumptions.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

08-W12 · Timers and preload ★★ M WRITTEN

A 16-bit timer increments at 1 MHz and overflows at 65536. Compute a preload for a nominal 2 ms delay. Explain how interrupt latency and software overhead affect observed timing.

Worked answer

One tick is 1 microsecond, so 2 ms requires 2000 increments. Preload=65536-2000=63536 decimal=F830h. Loading F830h leaves 07D0h=2000 counts until wraparound.

This is the hardware interval under the stated clock and counting convention. If software reloads after handling an interrupt, elapsed time may include entry/exit and reload delay. Auto-reload hardware or calibrated scheduling can reduce that discrepancy.

Do not assume a classic divide-by-12 timer input when the question explicitly states 1 MHz at the timer. Clock prescalers and peripheral clock domains must be read from the given design.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

08-W13 · Interrupt servicing ★★ M WRITTEN

Draw the conceptual sequence of handling a peripheral interrupt. Explain saving context, identifying/clearing the source, doing bounded work and returning safely.

Worked answer

A request is recognized subject to masking and priority. Hardware saves its specified return state; the handler preserves any additional registers it changes. Identify the source, perform the required bounded service, acknowledge/clear it according to peripheral rules, and restore context before the interrupt return.

Lengthy blocking work is often deferred to foreground/task context. Incorrect clearing can lose events or retrigger continuously, depending on peripheral semantics. Nested interrupts require explicit priority/reentrancy reasoning. The flow is not a universal instruction sequence for all microcontrollers.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

08-W14 · ADC and PWM calculations ★★ M WRITTEN

An ideal 10-bit ADC divides a 0–5 V span into 1024 equal intervals. Find resolution and the ideal code floor(Vin/Δ) for Vin=2.5 V, clamped to 0..1023. A 1 kHz PWM has 25% duty cycle: find period and high time.

Worked answer

ADC interval Δ=5/1024 V≈4.8828125 mV. For Vin=2.5 V, floor(2.5/(5/1024))=512. At the upper endpoint the formula is clamped to 1023 as stated; other datasheet endpoint conventions should not be substituted.

PWM period=1/1000 s=1 ms. High time=0.25*1 ms=0.25 ms, with low time=0.75 ms. PWM duty and ADC code resolution are different concepts even when both use counters.

A filtered PWM voltage depends on circuit dynamics and load; it is not automatically a precise analog voltage simply because the average duty has a nominal value.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

08-W15 · Timing and synchronizers ★★ M WRITTEN

Explain setup time, hold time and metastability. Draw a two-flip-flop synchronizer for a single asynchronous control bit and state one limitation for multi-bit transfers.

Worked answer

Setup time is the required data stability interval before a sampling edge; hold time is after it. Violating the timing aperture may cause metastability, where the storage element takes unusually long to settle to a valid logic value.

The extra stage reduces propagation probability but does not make it mathematically zero. Synchronizing each bit of a changing multi-bit bus independently can produce an incoherent combination. Use a suitable handshake, asynchronous FIFO or encoding/protocol for coherent multi-bit transfers. A two-stage circuit also introduces latency.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

09 · Computer Architecture & Operating Systems

Calculation questions define cache capacity, block size, timing components and scheduling ties. KiB/MiB use powers of two. Page replacement begins with empty frames unless stated. Scheduling assumes one CPU and zero context-switch cost unless given. These conventions prevent multiple plausible answers.

Coverage: CPU performance and instruction execution; pipelining and hazards; caches/locality and memory hierarchy; processes/threads, scheduling and synchronization; deadlock; virtual memory, TLB and replacement; files, storage, I/O and protection.

Reading references: R19, R21, R22
