09-M01 · CPU performance ★★★ E MCQ

A program executes 10^6 instructions at average CPI=2 on a 1 GHz CPU. What is its CPU execution time?

  1. 1 ms
  2. 2 ms
  3. 0.5 ms
  4. 2 seconds

Answer: B — 2 ms

CPU time=instruction count*CPI/clock rate=10^6*2/10^9=0.002 s=2 ms. Clock rate alone is not a complete performance measure.

09-M02 · Weighted CPI ★★★ M MCQ

Compute average CPI for this instruction mix.

ClassFractionCPI
A0.51
B0.32
C0.24
  1. 7
  2. 1.9
  3. 1.5
  4. 2.333...

Answer: B — 1.9

Weight by executed instruction fractions: 0.5*1+0.3*2+0.2*4=0.5+0.6+0.8=1.9. An unweighted mean would be wrong for this mix.

09-M03 · Amdahl’s law ★★ M MCQ

A component takes 40% of execution time and is made twice as fast; the rest is unchanged. What is overall speedup?

  1. 2
  2. 1.6
  3. 1.25
  4. 1.4

Answer: C — 1.25

New normalized time is 0.6+0.4/2=0.8. Speedup=1/0.8=1.25. Improvements affect only the accelerated fraction.

09-M04 · Instruction throughput ★★ M MCQ

A CPU runs at 2 GHz with average CPI=4 for a particular workload. What is the instruction rate?

  1. 500 million instructions/s
  2. 2 million instructions/s
  3. 8 billion instructions/s
  4. 4 million instructions/s

Answer: A — 500 million instructions/s

Instructions/s=cycles/s divided by cycles/instruction=2×10^9/4=5×10^8. Comparing different instruction sets by MIPS alone can mislead.

09-M05 · Pipelining ★★★ M MCQ

An ideal k=5 stage pipeline executes n=10 instructions with one-cycle stages and no stalls. How many cycles are needed from first fetch to last completion?

  1. 10
  2. 14
  3. 15
  4. 50

Answer: B — 14

Filling the pipeline takes five cycles for the first instruction, then nine further instructions complete one per cycle: k+n-1=14.

09-M06 · Pipeline hazards ★★★ M MCQ

Which is a read-after-write data dependence?

  1. An instruction reads a register that an earlier instruction will produce.
  2. A branch changes no control flow
  3. Two instructions use different registers only
  4. Two instructions read the same unchanged register

Answer: A — An instruction reads a register that an earlier instruction will produce.

RAW concerns consuming a value before its producer has made it available. Forwarding and/or stalls can address pipeline timing without changing the underlying dependence.

09-M07 · Branch penalties ★★ M MCQ

Base CPI is 1. Branches are 20% of instructions; 10% of branches mispredict, costing 5 cycles each. What is average CPI?

  1. 1.1
  2. 1.5
  3. 1.02
  4. 2

Answer: A — 1.1

Extra cycles per instruction are 0.20*0.10*5=0.10. Adding the base gives 1.10. The misprediction rate is conditional on branch instructions.

09-M08 · Locality ★★★ E MCQ

Repeatedly accessing the same variable in a short time illustrates:

  1. Spatial locality only
  2. No locality
  3. A guaranteed cache miss
  4. Temporal locality

Answer: D — Temporal locality

Temporal locality concerns reuse over time. Accessing nearby addresses instead illustrates spatial locality. Real code can exhibit both.

09-M09 · Locality ★★★ E MCQ

Scanning contiguous array elements primarily exploits:

  1. A page fault on every element necessarily
  2. Spatial locality
  3. Only temporal locality
  4. A deadlock condition

Answer: B — Spatial locality

Loading a block brings nearby addresses into the cache. Sequential access can reuse those neighbouring bytes before the block is evicted.

09-M10 · Direct-mapped cache ★★★ M MCQ

A direct-mapped cache has 64 lines. Memory block number 130 maps to which line?

  1. 64
  2. 2
  3. 1
  4. 130

Answer: B — 2

Mapping uses block number modulo line count: 130 mod64=2. The tag distinguishes block 130 from other blocks sharing line 2.

09-M11 · Cache address fields ★★★ M MCQ

A 32-bit byte address is used with a 4 KiB direct-mapped cache and 16-byte blocks. How many index bits are needed?

  1. 12
  2. 4
  3. 20
  4. 8

Answer: D — 8

There are 4096/16=256 cache lines. Selecting a line requires log2 256=8 bits. Offset needs four bits and the remaining 20 form the tag.

09-M12 · Set-associative caches ★★ M MCQ

An 8 KiB 4-way cache has 32-byte blocks. How many sets does it have?

  1. 32
  2. 64
  3. 256
  4. 128

Answer: B — 64

Number of blocks=8192/32=256. Four blocks per set gives 256/4=64 sets. Associativity changes set count, not the total stated capacity.

09-M13 · Average memory access time ★★★ M MCQ

Cache hit time is 1 ns, miss rate 0.05 and additional miss penalty 40 ns. What is AMAT?

  1. 41 ns
  2. 2 ns
  3. 3 ns
  4. 2.05 ns

Answer: C — 3 ns

AMAT=hit time+miss rate*additional penalty=1+0.05*40=3 ns. The word additional avoids counting hit time twice.

09-M14 · Cache write policies ★★ M MCQ

A write-through cache ordinarily:

  1. Propagates each cache write to the next memory level
  2. Makes the cache read-only
  3. Never updates cached data
  4. Writes modified data only on eviction

Answer: A — Propagates each cache write to the next memory level

Write-back defers propagation until a dirty block is evicted or otherwise written back. Write-through often uses a write buffer; neither policy alone specifies write allocation.

09-M15 · Cache write policies ★★ E MCQ

A dirty bit is needed in a write-back cache mainly to indicate:

  1. The block was modified and needs propagation before eviction
  2. The block is necessarily invalid
  3. The block contains a virus
  4. The address tag is zero

Answer: A — The block was modified and needs propagation before eviction

A clean block need not be written back when evicted. “Dirty” is a memory-consistency state, not a statement about malicious data.

09-M16 · Endianness and architecture ★★ M MCQ

Endianness specifies:

  1. The order of process scheduling
  2. The size of every cache line
  3. The number of CPU cores
  4. The ordering of bytes of a multibyte object in memory

Answer: D — The ordering of bytes of a multibyte object in memory

It does not determine the numeric value or instruction rate. Bit numbering and network byte order are related conventions but should be specified separately.

09-M17 · Instruction-set addressing ★★ E MCQ

Immediate addressing supplies an operand:

  1. Only through a disk block number
  2. Always from an absolute memory address
  3. As a value encoded in the instruction
  4. Only through a stack pop

Answer: C — As a value encoded in the instruction

An immediate is a literal operand carried by the instruction encoding. Register and memory addressing obtain operand values through different sources.

09-M18 · I/O and DMA ★★ M MCQ

Direct memory access primarily allows:

  1. Memory to become nonvolatile
  2. A device/controller to transfer data to/from memory with reduced per-item CPU copying
  3. A process to bypass every protection rule
  4. All transfers without any CPU setup or synchronization

Answer: B — A device/controller to transfer data to/from memory with reduced per-item CPU copying

The CPU can configure a transfer and handle completion, while the controller moves the data. Cache coherency, permissions and synchronization still matter.

09-M19 · Processes and threads ★★★ E MCQ

Threads in the same process usually share:

  1. Each thread’s execution stack as the same stack object
  2. The process address space and many resources
  3. The program counter value at every instant
  4. No memory under any circumstances

Answer: B — The process address space and many resources

Threads share code/data address space, but each has its own execution context such as registers and stack. Shared data creates synchronization needs.

09-M20 · Process states ★★★ E MCQ

A process waiting for an I/O completion is typically:

  1. Running on the CPU necessarily
  2. Blocked/waiting
  3. Terminated automatically
  4. Ready for CPU execution with no dependency

Answer: B — Blocked/waiting

A blocked process cannot proceed until an external event occurs. A ready process can run when scheduled; it is not waiting on that I/O condition.

09-M21 · Context switches ★★ M MCQ

A context switch generally involves:

  1. Recompiling both programs
  2. Copying every file on disk
  3. Changing source language
  4. Saving/restoring execution state so another task can run

Answer: D — Saving/restoring execution state so another task can run

The saved state includes necessary register/program-counter information and task context. Cache/TLB effects may add cost beyond the direct save/restore work.

09-M22 · Process creation ★★ M MCQ

Starting with one process, each process executes fork() once in each of three loop iterations. All calls succeed. How many processes exist after the loop?

  1. 3
  2. 8
  3. 4
  4. 6

Answer: B — 8

Each iteration doubles the current process count: 1→2→4→8. This assumes every child continues through the remaining iterations and no process exits early.

09-M23 · FCFS scheduling ★★★ M MCQ

All processes arrive at time 0: P1 burst 5, P2 burst 3, P3 burst 1. FCFS order is P1,P2,P3. What is mean waiting time?

  1. 9
  2. 13/3 time units
  3. 5/3
  4. 3

Answer: B — 13/3 time units

Waits are 0,5,8, so mean=(0+5+8)/3=13/3. Waiting time excludes the process’s own execution time.

09-M24 · SJF scheduling ★★★ M MCQ

All arrive at time 0 with bursts P1=5,P2=3,P3=1. Under nonpreemptive SJF, what is mean waiting time?

  1. 3
  2. 5/3 time units
  3. 9
  4. 13/3

Answer: B — 5/3 time units

Order P3,P2,P1 gives waits 0,1,4. Their mean is 5/3. This result assumes known bursts and the given simultaneous arrival.

09-M25 · Round robin ★★★ M MCQ

Reducing the quantum in round-robin scheduling generally tends to:

  1. Guarantee no starvation in every system policy
  2. Improve responsiveness but increase context-switch overhead
  3. Eliminate all overhead
  4. Make the scheduler identical to nonpreemptive SJF always

Answer: B — Improve responsiveness but increase context-switch overhead

Smaller slices give runnable tasks more frequent chances, but scheduling/context switching happens more often. The best tradeoff depends on workload and switching cost.

09-M26 · Starvation and aging ★★ M MCQ

Aging is used in priority scheduling to:

  1. Make waiting tasks progressively less likely to run
  2. Increase the effective priority of tasks that have waited a long time
  3. Delete all low-priority processes
  4. Remove all interrupts

Answer: B — Increase the effective priority of tasks that have waited a long time

Gradual priority improvement can prevent indefinite postponement by a continuous stream of higher-priority work. It does not mean the physical age of the computer.

09-M27 · Race conditions ★★★ M MCQ

A race condition can occur when:

  1. Two tasks read separate immutable constants
  2. Only one task exists and no asynchronous events occur
  3. The result depends on an uncontrolled interleaving of accesses to shared state
  4. All shared accesses are correctly serialized

Answer: C — The result depends on an uncontrolled interleaving of accesses to shared state

Read-modify-write operations can interleave and lose updates. Mutual exclusion or appropriate atomic operations are needed for the actual invariant, not simply for individual source lines.

09-M28 · Semaphores ★★★ M MCQ

The fundamental wait operation on a counting semaphore must be:

  1. A compiler comment
  2. An unprotected read followed much later by a write
  3. A fixed one-second delay
  4. Atomic with respect to competing semaphore operations

Answer: D — Atomic with respect to competing semaphore operations

Testing/decrementing or blocking must be coordinated so competing tasks cannot both claim one available unit. Implementations use suitable atomic primitives and scheduling support.

09-M29 · Deadlock conditions ★★★ E MCQ

Which is one of the four Coffman deadlock conditions?

  1. No task ever holds a resource
  2. Unlimited resources
  3. Circular wait
  4. Mandatory preemption of every resource

Answer: C — Circular wait

The usual four are mutual exclusion, hold-and-wait, no preemption and circular wait. Breaking a necessary condition prevents deadlock under that resource model.

09-M30 · Resource-allocation graphs ★★ M MCQ

A resource-allocation graph has a directed cycle. When does that cycle suffice to establish deadlock?

  1. For arbitrary resource multiplicities without further checks
  2. Only when all edges are undirected
  3. When each resource type in the relevant model has a single instance
  4. Whenever two tasks share a source-code file

Answer: C — When each resource type in the relevant model has a single instance

With multiple instances, another free instance can sometimes allow progress despite a cycle. The single-instance assumption makes the cycle conclusive.

09-M31 · Banker’s algorithm ★★ M MCQ

A safe state in Banker’s algorithm means:

  1. The system is already deadlocked.
  2. No request ever waits.
  3. Every resource is currently unused.
  4. There exists an order in which all processes can meet remaining claims and finish.

Answer: D — There exists an order in which all processes can meet remaining claims and finish.

Safety is an existence guarantee under declared maximum claims. An unsafe state is not necessarily currently deadlocked, but lacks this completion guarantee.

09-M32 · Paging address fields ★★★ E MCQ

A 32-bit byte virtual address uses 4 KiB pages. How many offset bits are needed?

  1. 16
  2. 12
  3. 10
  4. 20

Answer: B — 12

4 KiB=4096=2^12 bytes, requiring 12 bits for the byte offset. The remaining 20 bits identify a virtual page.

09-M33 · Page-table size ★★ M MCQ

A single-level page table maps a full 32-bit virtual byte space with 4 KiB pages and 4-byte PTEs. What is its size?

  1. 1 MiB
  2. 4 MiB
  3. 16 MiB
  4. 4 KiB

Answer: B — 4 MiB

There are 2^(32-12)=2^20 entries. Multiplying by four bytes gives 2^22 bytes=4 MiB. This excludes any additional structures and assumes the complete flat table.

09-M34 · TLBs ★★★ E MCQ

A TLB primarily caches:

  1. Recent virtual-page to physical-frame translations
  2. Only arithmetic results
  3. Only application source code
  4. The entire disk directory necessarily

Answer: A — Recent virtual-page to physical-frame translations

It accelerates address translation. A TLB miss is not necessarily a page fault: the page-table entry may still indicate a resident page.

09-M35 · Effective access time ★★ M MCQ

TLB lookup costs 10 ns; memory costs 100 ns. A hit needs one memory access; a miss needs a page-table access plus data access. Hit rate is 0.9, no page faults or overlap. What is EAT?

  1. 210 ns
  2. 100 ns
  3. 110 ns
  4. 120 ns

Answer: D — 120 ns

EAT=0.9*(10+100)+0.1*(10+200)=99+21=120 ns. The model explicitly counts a serial lookup and two memory accesses on a TLB miss.

09-M36 · Page faults ★★★ M MCQ

A page fault occurs when a referenced virtual page:

  1. Is merely absent from the TLB in every case
  2. Requires OS handling because the translation is absent/not resident or violates access conditions
  3. Has an odd page number
  4. Must always be permanently invalid

Answer: B — Requires OS handling because the translation is absent/not resident or violates access conditions

A not-present page may be brought from backing storage if the access is valid. Protection faults and invalid addresses need other handling. A TLB miss with a valid resident PTE is not the same event.

09-M37 · Page replacement ★★★ E MCQ

Which replacement policy evicts the resident page whose next use is farthest in the future?

  1. Random replacement
  2. LRU
  3. FIFO
  4. Optimal replacement

Answer: D — Optimal replacement

The policy minimizes faults for a known reference trace but requires future knowledge. It is useful as a comparison bound, not a generally implementable online predictor.

09-M38 · Belady’s anomaly ★★ M MCQ

Which classical page policy can exhibit more faults after the frame count is increased?

  1. FIFO
  2. Optimal replacement
  3. LRU under its exact stack-policy model
  4. A policy that always knows future references and is optimal

Answer: A — FIFO

FIFO lacks the stack inclusion property and can show Belady’s anomaly on suitable traces. Exact LRU and optimal replacement have the relevant inclusion property.

09-M39 · Thrashing ★★★ M MCQ

Thrashing describes a condition where:

  1. Every page is permanently pinned
  2. All tasks have finished
  3. The CPU runs only integer additions efficiently
  4. Excessive paging dominates useful execution

Answer: D — Excessive paging dominates useful execution

If active working sets do not fit available memory, faults can repeatedly evict needed pages. Reducing multiprogramming or increasing effective memory can help, depending on the system.

09-M40 · Fragmentation ★★ M MCQ

Unused space inside an allocated fixed-size page/frame is an example of:

  1. A semaphore count
  2. External fragmentation
  3. Internal fragmentation
  4. A routing loop

Answer: C — Internal fragmentation

Internal waste is within an allocated unit. External fragmentation is free space split across separate holes that may not satisfy a large contiguous allocation.

09-M41 · Contiguous allocation ★★ M MCQ

Free holes in order are 100,500,200,300,600 units. First-fit places a 212-unit request in which original hole?

  1. 500
  2. 200
  3. 600
  4. 300

Answer: A — 500

Scan from the beginning and take the first hole large enough. The 100-unit hole fails and 500 succeeds. Best-fit would choose 300, a different policy.

09-M42 · Working sets ★★ M MCQ

A process working set over a reference window is:

  1. The set of pages referenced within that recent window
  2. Only the page-table root
  3. All virtual addresses ever possible
  4. Only pages containing operating-system code

Answer: A — The set of pages referenced within that recent window

The working set estimates the current locality needed to run without repeated faults. Its size depends on the chosen window and workload phase.

09-M43 · File-system links ★★ M MCQ

In a conventional Unix-style filesystem, a hard link refers to:

  1. Only a text path resolved like a symbolic link
  2. A separately copied file automatically
  3. A compressed backup image
  4. The same underlying inode/object as another directory entry

Answer: D — The same underlying inode/object as another directory entry

Multiple directory names can reference one inode. Removing one name need not remove the object while other links/open references remain. Filesystem-specific restrictions still apply.

09-M44 · RAID ★★ M MCQ

Four equal-size disks each have capacity S. A RAID 5 arrangement with one distributed parity equivalent has usable capacity:

  1. 4S
  2. S
  3. 2S
  4. 3S

Answer: D — 3S

One disk-equivalent of capacity is used for parity across stripes. RAID 5 tolerates one disk failure under its assumptions but is not a substitute for backup.

09-M45 · Disk scheduling ★ M MCQ

A disk head is at cylinder 50 and requests are 10,55,58,90. Which request does SSTF serve first?

  1. 90
  2. 58
  3. 10
  4. 55

Answer: D — 55

Distances are 40,5,8,40. The shortest seek is five cylinders to 55. Ties would require a policy, but none affects this first choice.

09-M46 · Protection ★★★ E MCQ

Why distinguish user mode from kernel mode?

  1. To force all user programs to share one stack
  2. To make arithmetic impossible in user mode
  3. To remove the need for memory protection
  4. To restrict privileged operations and mediate access through controlled interfaces

Answer: D — To restrict privileged operations and mediate access through controlled interfaces

Hardware privilege mechanisms support isolation and controlled system calls. They must be combined with correct kernel policy and implementation.

09-M47 · System calls ★★★ E MCQ

A system call normally provides:

  1. A controlled entry into an operating-system service
  2. An arbitrary jump to any kernel address
  3. A guarantee that the service never blocks
  4. A direct source-code macro only

Answer: A — A controlled entry into an operating-system service

The interface validates requests and performs privileged work where appropriate. Calling a library function is not always the same as making a system call.

09-M48 · Condition variables ★★ M MCQ

Why is a condition wait usually placed in a while loop checking the predicate?

  1. A signal guarantees every waiting task can proceed.
  2. The predicate may be false when the task resumes, including after spurious wakes or competition.
  3. The condition variable permanently stores all past signals.
  4. A while loop makes the mutex unnecessary.

Answer: B — The predicate may be false when the task resumes, including after spurious wakes or competition.

A wakeup is not ownership of the desired state. Reacquire the mutex and recheck the actual predicate before proceeding. Correct lock discipline prevents missed state transitions.

09-M49 · Interrupt versus trap ★★ M MCQ

Which is a typical synchronous exception/trap associated with the currently executing instruction?

  1. A divide-by-zero fault
  2. An external keyboard event
  3. A hardware timer tick
  4. An unrelated network packet arrival

Answer: A — A divide-by-zero fault

Synchronous exceptions arise from instruction execution; asynchronous interrupts arise independently. Exact terminology varies by architecture, so the examples clarify the intended distinction.

09-M50 · Memory-mapped files ★ M MCQ

A memory-mapped file principally allows:

  1. Every file to remain permanently in RAM
  2. Access to file-backed contents through a virtual-memory mapping
  3. All writes to become immediately durable without synchronization
  4. Bypassing every permission check

Answer: B — Access to file-backed contents through a virtual-memory mapping

The OS manages residency and mapping semantics. Visibility, flushing and durability require the relevant API/storage guarantees; mapping alone is not a persistence promise.

09-W01 · CPU performance comparison ★★★ M WRITTEN

Machine A runs a workload of 10^8 instructions at CPI=2 and 2 GHz. Machine B needs 1.2×10^8 instructions at CPI=1.5 and 1.5 GHz for the same task. Compute CPU times and the faster machine’s speedup.

Worked answer

A: time=10^8*2/(2×10^9)=0.10 s. B: time=1.2×10^8*1.5/(1.5×10^9)=0.12 s. A is faster for this task, with speedup T_B/T_A=0.12/0.10=1.2.

The comparison holds workload/functionality fixed, not instruction count. Different instruction sets/compilers can require different counts. Comparing only clock frequency or only CPI would ignore another part of CPU time.

These are CPU execution times under the model, excluding I/O and other waiting. Overall application elapsed-time ranking could differ if those factors were substantial.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

09-W02 · Cache address decomposition ★★★ M WRITTEN

A 32-bit byte-addressed machine has a 4 KiB direct-mapped cache with 16-byte blocks. Draw the address fields. For address 0x12345678, find tag, index and offset.

Worked answer

Block offset needs log2 16=4 bits. There are 4096/16=256 lines, requiring 8 index bits. Tag bits=32-8-4=20.

0x12345678 has offset 0x8. Index=(address>>4)&0xFF=0x67=103. Tag=address>>12=0x12345. The block base address is 0x12345670.

Use the address after removing block-offset bits to identify the block. Taking the last eight bits as the index would incorrectly mix offset and index.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

09-W03 · Memory hierarchy timing ★★★ H WRITTEN

A serial cache hierarchy has L1 hit time 1 ns, L1 local miss rate 0.1, L2 additional lookup time 5 ns, L2 local miss rate 0.2, and additional memory penalty 50 ns after an L2 miss. Calculate AMAT.

Worked answer

An L1 hit pays 1 ns. Every L1 miss additionally accesses L2; an L2 miss then adds 50 ns. Thus AMAT=1+0.1*(5+0.2*50)=1+0.1*15=2.5 ns.

EventProbabilityTotal time contribution
L1 hit0.90.9*1=0.9
L1 miss, L2 hit0.1*0.80.08*6=0.48
Both miss0.1*0.20.02*56=1.12

The weighted sum is 0.9+0.48+1.12=2.5 ns. L2 local miss rate is conditional on reaching L2; treating it as the fraction of all CPU accesses would overcount memory misses.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

09-W04 · Pipeline chart and speedup ★★ M WRITTEN

Draw an ideal five-stage pipeline chart for four instructions (IF,ID,EX,MEM,WB), with one cycle per stage and no hazards. Give completion cycles and compare total cycles with nonpipelined sequential stage execution.

Worked answer

Instruction completions occur in cycles 5,6,7,8. Total pipelined time is 5+4-1=8 cycles. Nonpipelined sequential execution requires 4*5=20 cycles under the same stage-time assumption; speedup=20/8=2.5.

This short batch does not achieve the asymptotic fivefold limit because filling and draining matter. Stalls, unequal stage times and pipeline register overhead reduce practical gains. Pipelining improves throughput; it does not make a single instruction pass through fewer logical stages.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

09-W05 · FCFS and SJF Gantt charts ★★★ M WRITTEN

Three processes arrive at time 0 with bursts P1=5,P2=3,P3=1. Draw FCFS in order P1,P2,P3 and nonpreemptive SJF. Compute waiting and turnaround times for both; ignore context-switch costs.

Worked answer

ProcessFCFS waitFCFS turnaroundSJF waitSJF turnaround
P10549
P25814
P38901

FCFS mean wait=13/3 and mean turnaround=22/3. SJF mean wait=5/3 and mean turnaround=14/3. With simultaneous arrivals, turnaround equals completion time. Waiting=turnaround-burst. This comparison assumes known bursts; real schedulers may estimate them.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

09-W06 · SRTF scheduling ★★★ M WRITTEN

Processes are P1(arrival0,burst8), P2(1,4), P3(2,2). Draw the preemptive shortest-remaining-time schedule and calculate waiting times. Ignore switching cost.

Worked answer

ProcessCompletionTurnaround=C-arrivalWaiting=turnaround-burst
P114146
P2762
P3420

At t=1, P2’s 4 is below P1’s remaining 7; at t=2, P3’s 2 is below P2’s remaining 3. After P3 finishes, P2 finishes before P1. Mean waiting=(6+2+0)/3=8/3. Preemptions change the waiting periods but not each process’s total CPU demand.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

09-W07 · Round-robin scheduling ★★★ M WRITTEN

All arrive at time 0, with queue order P1,P2,P3 and bursts 5,3,1. Quantum is 2 and switching cost is zero. Draw the schedule and compute completion/waiting times.

Worked answer

ProcessCompletionBurstWaiting
P1954
P2835
P3514

Timeline: P1 0–2, P2 2–4, P3 4–5, P1 5–7, P2 7–8, P1 8–9. Since all arrival times are zero, waiting=completion-burst. Mean wait=13/3.

A task that finishes before the quantum expires leaves immediately; P3 consumes only one unit. The values would change if context-switch overhead or later arrivals were introduced.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

09-W08 · Semaphores and bounded buffers ★★★ H WRITTEN

Give semaphore pseudocode for a bounded producer–consumer buffer of capacity N. Explain initialization and why a producer should not hold the mutex while waiting for an empty slot.

Worked answer

Pseudocode
empty = N; full = 0; mutex = 1

Producer:
    item = produce()
    wait(empty)
    wait(mutex)
    insert(item)
    signal(mutex)
    signal(full)

Consumer:
    wait(full)
    wait(mutex)
    item = remove()
    signal(mutex)
    signal(empty)
    consume(item)

empty and full count available slots/items; mutex protects buffer manipulation. If a producer locks mutex and then blocks on empty when the buffer is full, consumers may be unable to acquire mutex to remove an item, causing deadlock. The analogous ordering matters for consumers. Semaphore operations must be atomic; produce/consume work stays outside the buffer critical section.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

09-W09 · Deadlock and resource graphs ★★★ M WRITTEN

Two single-instance resources R1,R2 are allocated to P1,P2 respectively. P1 requests R2 and P2 requests R1. Draw the resource-allocation graph, identify all four deadlock conditions and propose one prevention rule.

Worked answer

Cycle: R1→P1→R2→P2→R1. Mutual exclusion holds because each resource has one exclusive owner; each process holds one while waiting for another; resources cannot be forcibly taken; circular wait exists.

A global acquisition order, such as always acquiring R1 before R2, prevents this circular wait when every process follows it. Another policy could require acquiring all needed resources atomically, reducing hold-and-wait at a potential utilization cost. With multiple instances, a cycle alone would not always prove deadlock.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

09-W10 · Banker’s safety algorithm ★★ H WRITTEN

Use the two-resource snapshot. Available=(1,1). Compute Need and find a safe sequence, if one exists.

ProcessAllocation(A,B)Max(A,B)
P0(1,0)(3,2)
P1(1,1)(2,1)
P2(1,0)(3,1)

Worked answer

ProcessNeed=Max-Allocation
P0(2,2)
P1(1,0)
P2(2,1)

Start Work=(1,1). P1 can finish, releasing (1,1), so Work=(2,2). P0 can then finish, releasing (1,0), giving (3,2). P2 can finish and return (1,0), leaving (4,2). A safe sequence is P1,P0,P2.

Other safe sequences may exist, such as P1,P2,P0. Need is not Max itself. Released resources equal current Allocation, not Max. Total resource count is initially Available+all allocations=(4,2), matching the final Work.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

09-W11 · Paging and TLB calculations ★★★ M WRITTEN

A 32-bit virtual byte address uses 4 KiB pages; physical memory is 1 MiB. Give virtual-page, offset and physical-frame bit counts. For virtual address 0x00005ABC mapped from VPN 5 to frame 0x2A, find the physical address.

Worked answer

Page size 2^12 gives 12 offset bits. Virtual page number has 32-12=20 bits. Physical space 1 MiB=2^20 bytes, so frame number has 20-12=8 bits.

Virtual address splits as VPN=0x5 and offset=0xABC. Frame base=0x2A*0x1000=0x2A000. Add the unchanged offset: physical address=0x2AABC. The mapping must be valid and have the required access permission; arithmetic alone does not establish authorization.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

09-W12 · Page-replacement traces ★★★ H WRITTEN

With three initially empty frames, compare FIFO and LRU on reference string 1,2,3,1,4,2,5. Show frames and fault counts. State your replacement order convention.

Worked answer

ReferenceFIFO framesFIFO H/FLRU framesLRU H/F
11,-,-F1,-,-F
21,2,-F1,2,-F
31,2,3F1,2,3F
11,2,3H1,2,3H
44,2,3F1,4,3F
24,2,3H1,4,2F
54,5,3F5,4,2F

FIFO faults=5; LRU faults=6. Columns show fixed physical frame slots. FIFO removes oldest-loaded resident page and does not refresh age on a hit. LRU refreshes recency on each access, causing it to evict page 2 for reference 4.

LRU is not guaranteed to outperform FIFO on every individual trace, even though it has useful locality and stack-policy properties. The trace must be simulated rather than ranked by reputation.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

09-W13 · File links and lifetime ★★ M WRITTEN

Explain hard links versus symbolic links using a file with two hard-link names a and b, plus symbolic link c pointing to path a. What happens after removing directory entry a?

Worked answer

a and b refer to the same underlying file object/inode; neither is a separate content copy. c stores a path reference that resolves through the name a. Removing a decreases the file’s hard-link count but b still names the file, so its data remains accessible through b.

The symbolic link c may become dangling because its target path a no longer resolves, even though the file is still available as b. Recreating a with a different file can cause c to refer to that new target.

Open-file references and filesystem-specific rules affect final reclamation. This is the conventional Unix-style distinction; it is not a claim that every filesystem supports every kind of link.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

09-W14 · RAID and backup ★★ M WRITTEN

Four equal disks each provide 2 TB. Compare nominal usable capacities and failure tolerance of RAID 0, a two-pair RAID 1 arrangement, and RAID 5. Explain why none replaces backup.

Worked answer

ArrangementUsable capacityFailure note
RAID 08 TBAny disk failure can lose the striped array
Two mirrored pairs4 TBOne failure in each pair is tolerable; both in one pair are not
RAID 56 TBOne disk failure tolerated under the model

Capacity calculations ignore metadata and assume equal disks. RAID 1 wording here explicitly means two independent mirrored pairs, not a four-way mirror.

RAID primarily addresses selected disk failures. Accidental deletion, malware, corruption or site loss can affect all live copies. Independent versioned backups and tested restoration protect different failure modes. Rebuild behaviour and correlated failures also affect practical risk.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

09-W15 · Memory allocation policies ★★ M WRITTEN

Free holes in address order are 100,500,200,300,600 units. Place one 212-unit request using first-fit, best-fit and worst-fit independently. State chosen hole/remainder and explain fragmentation.

Worked answer

PolicyChosen original holeRemainder
First-fit500288
Best-fit30088
Worst-fit600388

First-fit stops at the first adequate hole. Best-fit chooses the smallest adequate hole; worst-fit the largest. The policies are applied independently to the same original list, not sequentially to a mutated list.

Free memory split across noncontiguous holes is external fragmentation. Unused bytes inside an allocated fixed-size unit are internal fragmentation. No placement policy is universally best for every future request sequence; coalescing and the allocation model also matter.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

10 · Artificial Intelligence and Machine Learning

Work from assumptions and small numerical examples. Search behaviour depends on graph direction, edge costs, duplicate handling and tie-breaking. Machine-learning results depend on class definitions, data splits and evaluation thresholds. These are foundational exercises, not claims that a particular model will perform best on a real dataset.

Coverage: Uninformed and heuristic search; games and constraints; probabilistic reasoning; supervised and unsupervised learning; evaluation and leakage; gradients and regularization; trees, nearest neighbours, clustering and PCA; neural networks and elementary computer vision.

Reading references: R23, R24, R6

10-M01 · Breadth-first search ★★★ E MCQ

Which condition makes breadth-first search return a minimum-cost path in an unweighted graph?

  1. The graph must be a complete graph
  2. Every vertex has a different degree
  3. The heuristic overestimates all costs
  4. Every edge has the same positive cost

Answer: D — Every edge has the same positive cost

BFS expands by number of edges from the source. Equal positive edge costs make fewer edges equivalent to smaller path cost. With unequal costs, BFS can return a more expensive path.

10-M02 · Uniform-cost search ★★★ E MCQ

Uniform-cost graph search expands the frontier state with the smallest:

  1. Number of outgoing edges
  2. Alphabetically largest name
  3. Accumulated path cost g(n)
  4. Estimated remaining cost h(n) only

Answer: C — Accumulated path cost g(n)

A priority queue orders frontier paths by g. With nonnegative edge costs and appropriate duplicate handling, popping the goal establishes a lowest-cost path. Negative edges invalidate the usual finalization argument.

10-M03 · Heuristic search ★★★ E MCQ

A* orders its frontier using which evaluation?

  1. f(n)=degree(n)
  2. f(n)=g(n)+h(n)
  3. f(n)=h(n) only
  4. f(n)=g(n)-h(n)

Answer: B — f(n)=g(n)+h(n)

g is the cost already incurred and h estimates remaining cost. Greedy best-first search uses only h and can sacrifice optimality. Tie-breaking must be specified for an exact expansion sequence.

10-M04 · Admissible heuristics ★★★ E MCQ

An admissible heuristic for a nonnegative-cost shortest-path problem:

  1. Always equals the exact remaining cost
  2. Must strictly increase along every edge
  3. Never exceeds the true remaining optimal cost
  4. Always exceeds the exact remaining cost

Answer: C — Never exceeds the true remaining optimal cost

For each state, h(n) <= h*(n). An underestimate may be weak but remains admissible. Admissibility alone does not specify duplicate handling in graph search.

10-M05 · Consistent heuristics ★★ M MCQ

A heuristic is consistent when, for every edge n→m of cost c, it satisfies:

  1. h(n) >= h(m)+100
  2. h(n) <= c + h(m), with h(goal)=0
  3. h(n)=degree(n)
  4. h(n) > c + h(m)

Answer: B — h(n) <= c + h(m), with h(goal)=0

This is a triangle-inequality condition. In the usual nonnegative-cost A* setting it supports nondecreasing f along a path and avoids needing to reopen finalized nodes. An admissible but inconsistent heuristic may require reopening.

10-M06 · Search from a figure ★★★ M MCQ

Using the directed graph, what is the optimal cost from S to G?

  1. 10
  2. 8
  3. 5
  4. 7

Answer: D — 7

The three simple source-to-goal paths cost S-A-G=3+7=10, S-B-G=5+3=8, and S-A-B-G=3+1+3=7. The smallest is 7. Arrows forbid reverse traversal. Counting edges instead of summing weights would select the wrong criterion.

10-M07 · Minimax ★★★ E MCQ

A MAX root has two MIN children with leaf pairs (3,5) and (2,9). What value propagates to the root?

  1. 3
  2. 9
  3. 5
  4. 2

Answer: A — 3

The left MIN value is min(3,5)=3; the right MIN value is min(2,9)=2. MAX chooses max(3,2)=3. The largest leaf, 9, cannot be forced by MAX against the minimizing opponent.

10-M08 · Alpha-beta pruning ★★ M MCQ

Alpha-beta pruning, when applied correctly to the same minimax tree and depth:

  1. Works only when all leaves are positive
  2. Always returns a different approximate value
  3. Returns the same minimax value while potentially examining fewer leaves
  4. Requires every branch to be visited

Answer: C — Returns the same minimax value while potentially examining fewer leaves

Pruning discards branches that cannot change the decision given current bounds. Move ordering affects how much work is saved, not the correct minimax value.

10-M09 · Constraint satisfaction ★★ M MCQ

The minimum-remaining-values heuristic selects the next unassigned variable with:

  1. The largest variable name
  2. The most recently assigned value
  3. The smallest remaining legal domain
  4. The smallest numeric value in the whole problem

Answer: C — The smallest remaining legal domain

MRV tries the most constrained variable first, exposing failure early. It is distinct from choosing a least-constraining value after the variable has been selected.

10-M10 · Genetic algorithms ★ E MCQ

For a binary string, fitness equal to the count of 1 bits directly rewards:

  1. Only strings with alternating bits
  2. The shortest string when length is fixed
  3. Strings containing more 1s
  4. A guaranteed globally optimal next generation

Answer: C — Strings containing more 1s

Fitness defines the optimization objective. Selection, crossover and mutation are search mechanisms, not guarantees of finding the optimum in a fixed number of generations.

10-M11 · Bayesian networks ★★ M MCQ

In a directed acyclic Bayesian network, the joint distribution factorizes as:

  1. The maximum conditional probability in the graph
  2. The product of edge weights regardless of normalization
  3. The sum of all marginal distributions
  4. The product of each node’s conditional distribution given its parents

Answer: D — The product of each node’s conditional distribution given its parents

P(X1,...,Xn)=product_i P(Xi | Parents(Xi)). The DAG and local conditional tables define a normalized joint distribution when each table is valid.

10-M12 · Bayes rule ★★★ M MCQ

A disease prevalence is 0.1, sensitivity 0.8 and false-positive rate 0.2. What is P(disease | positive)?

  1. 0.1
  2. 0.8
  3. 4/13
  4. 8/9

Answer: C — 4/13

P(D and +)=0.1*0.8=0.08. P(+)=0.08+0.9*0.2=0.26. Divide: 0.08/0.26=4/13. Sensitivity is P(+|D), not the reverse conditional probability.

10-M13 · Learning paradigms ★★★ E MCQ

Predicting a continuous house price from labelled historical examples is typically:

  1. A deterministic sorting problem
  2. Supervised regression
  3. Only reinforcement learning
  4. Unsupervised clustering

Answer: B — Supervised regression

The label is a real-valued target. Classification predicts a category; clustering seeks structure without provided target labels. The statistical model still needs validation.

10-M14 · Learning paradigms ★★★ E MCQ

Grouping unlabelled observations by similarity is a typical example of:

  1. Clustering
  2. A compiler lexer
  3. Supervised classification
  4. Only dynamic programming

Answer: A — Clustering

Clustering is an unsupervised task. The choice of distance, scaling and cluster assumptions influences the resulting groups; groups need not equal meaningful real-world classes.

10-M15 · Train/validation/test splits ★★★ E MCQ

Which use of the held-out test set most directly undermines its role as an independent final evaluation?

  1. Repeatedly choosing hyperparameters to maximize test performance
  2. Reporting it after fixing the model and thresholds
  3. Preserving group boundaries when splitting
  4. Keeping it unused during training and tuning

Answer: A — Repeatedly choosing hyperparameters to maximize test performance

Tuning against test outcomes leaks test information into selection. Use training data to fit parameters and validation/cross-validation for choices; reserve a final test set.

10-M16 · Data leakage ★★★ M MCQ

For video frames from several recording sessions, which split is the strongest starting safeguard against near-duplicate leakage?

  1. Choose test frames based on model confidence after training
  2. Duplicate each frame into every split
  3. Keep related frames from each session/event in the same split
  4. Randomly scatter adjacent frames across train and test

Answer: C — Keep related frames from each session/event in the same split

Group/session splitting avoids evaluating on nearly identical versions of training events. Separate cameras or dates may require additional design. No split rule alone guarantees all deployment shifts are represented.

10-M17 · Accuracy ★★★ E MCQ

A binary classifier has TP=30, FP=10, FN=5, TN=55. What is accuracy?

  1. 0.85
  2. 0.75
  3. 6/7
  4. 0.60

Answer: A — 0.85

Accuracy=(TP+TN)/total=(30+55)/100=0.85. It counts correct predictions in both classes and can be misleading on severely imbalanced data.

10-M18 · Precision ★★★ E MCQ

For TP=30 and FP=10, precision for the positive class is:

  1. 0.60
  2. 0.25
  3. 0.85
  4. 0.75

Answer: D — 0.75

Precision=TP/(TP+FP)=30/40. It asks how often a positive prediction is correct, not how many actual positives are found.

10-M19 · Recall ★★★ E MCQ

For TP=30 and FN=5, recall for the positive class is:

  1. 1/7
  2. 6/7
  3. 3/4
  4. 0.95

Answer: B — 6/7

Recall=TP/(TP+FN)=30/35=6/7. It measures the share of actual positives detected. A threshold change can alter both TP and FN.

10-M20 · F1 score ★★★ M MCQ

If precision=3/4 and recall=6/7, what is F1?

  1. 0.6
  2. 0.75
  3. 0.8
  4. 0.85

Answer: C — 0.8

F1=2PR/(P+R). Here 2*(3/4)*(6/7)/((3/4)+(6/7))=4/5. Equivalently, with TP30 FP10 FN5, F1=60/(60+10+5)=0.8.

10-M21 · Class imbalance ★★★ E MCQ

A dataset has 990 negatives and 10 positives. A model predicts negative for every item. Which pair is correct?

  1. Accuracy 99%; recall 100%
  2. Accuracy 99%; positive-class recall 0%
  3. Accuracy 0%; recall 99%
  4. Accuracy 50%; recall 50%

Answer: B — Accuracy 99%; positive-class recall 0%

TN=990 and FN=10, so accuracy=990/1000=99% and recall=0/10=0. The high accuracy hides complete failure to identify positives.

10-M22 · Decision thresholds ★★ M MCQ

For fixed binary scores, lowering the positive threshold cannot decrease which count?

  1. True positives
  2. True negatives
  3. Precision in every dataset
  4. Correct predictions in every dataset

Answer: A — True positives

The positive prediction set can only expand. TP and FP cannot decrease, while TN and FN cannot increase. Precision may move either way; accuracy also need not improve.

10-M23 · ROC coordinates ★★ M MCQ

An ROC curve plots which quantities as the threshold varies?

  1. Accuracy against training epoch only
  2. True-positive rate against false-positive rate
  3. Specificity against sample index
  4. Precision against loss only

Answer: B — True-positive rate against false-positive rate

TPR=TP/(TP+FN); FPR=FP/(FP+TN). These denominators condition on actual positive and negative classes respectively. Precision–recall curves answer a different comparison.

10-M24 · Squared loss ★★★ E MCQ

For predictions (2,4) and true targets (1,6), what is mean squared error?

  1. 1.5
  2. 0.5
  3. 5
  4. 2.5

Answer: D — 2.5

Squared errors are (2-1)^2=1 and (4-6)^2=4. Their mean is 5/2=2.5. Root mean squared error would require taking the square root.

10-M25 · Cross-entropy ★★ M MCQ

For one binary example y=1 with predicted positive probability p=0.8, its natural-log cross-entropy loss is:

  1. 0.8
  2. -ln(0.8)
  3. ln(0.8)
  4. -ln(0.2)

Answer: B — -ln(0.8)

Binary cross-entropy is -[y ln p+(1-y) ln(1-p)]. Substituting y=1 leaves -ln0.8≈0.2231. Higher correct-class confidence lowers this individual loss.

10-M26 · Gradient descent ★★★ E MCQ

For L(w)=(w-3)^2, start w=1 and take one gradient-descent step with learning rate 0.1. What is the new w?

  1. 0.6
  2. 1.2
  3. 1.4
  4. 3

Answer: C — 1.4

dL/dw=2(w-3)=-4 at w=1. Update w_new=w-0.1*(-4)=1.4. The minus sign moves against the gradient.

10-M27 · Logistic regression ★★★ E MCQ

The sigmoid function maps a real score z to:

  1. max(0,z)
  2. 1/(1+exp(-z))
  3. z^2
  4. exp(z) without normalization

Answer: B — 1/(1+exp(-z))

The sigmoid output lies strictly between zero and one for finite z. Interpreting it as a calibrated probability requires more than its numeric range. max(0,z) is ReLU.

10-M28 · Nearest neighbours ★★ M MCQ

Why can feature scaling matter for Euclidean k-nearest neighbours?

  1. Scaling guarantees perfect class separation
  2. A feature with a large numeric scale can dominate distances
  3. Scaling always makes all points identical
  4. k-NN never uses distances

Answer: B — A feature with a large numeric scale can dominate distances

A distance combines differences across coordinates. Fit scaling parameters on training data only; apply the same transform to validation/test data to avoid leakage.

10-M29 · Naive Bayes ★★ M MCQ

The naive Bayes assumption is that features are:

  1. Always equal-valued
  2. Conditionally independent given the class
  3. Always marginally independent
  4. Independent of the class in every distribution

Answer: B — Conditionally independent given the class

The model approximates P(x1,...,xd|y) by product_j P(xj|y). This does not assert marginal feature independence after mixing classes.

10-M30 · Entropy ★★★ E MCQ

What is the base-2 entropy of a binary label with equal probabilities?

  1. 0 bits
  2. 1 bit
  3. 0.5 bits
  4. 2 bits

Answer: B — 1 bit

H=-0.5 log2 0.5-0.5 log2 0.5=1. A pure class distribution has entropy zero.

10-M31 · Information gain ★★ M MCQ

A balanced binary-label node splits into two equally sized, perfectly pure children. What is its information gain?

  1. 2 bits
  2. 0.5 bits
  3. 1 bit
  4. 0 bits

Answer: C — 1 bit

Parent entropy=1. Both children have entropy 0, so their weighted entropy is 0. Gain=1-0=1 bit. The comparison assumes base-2 logarithms.

10-M32 · Bagging ★★ M MCQ

Bagging typically trains models on:

  1. One sample with no replacement ever
  2. Bootstrap-resampled training sets and aggregates predictions
  3. Only adversarially chosen labels
  4. The held-out test set repeatedly

Answer: B — Bootstrap-resampled training sets and aggregates predictions

A bootstrap sample draws with replacement, conventionally the same number of rows as the original training set. Aggregation can reduce variance; it is not a universal guarantee of improved performance.

10-M33 · Boosting ★★ M MCQ

Which description best distinguishes a typical boosting process from independent bagged training?

  1. Models are added sequentially to address weaknesses of the current ensemble
  2. Only unsupervised distances are used
  3. All models must be trained independently on identical labels
  4. The test labels are required for every update

Answer: A — Models are added sequentially to address weaknesses of the current ensemble

Different boosting algorithms adjust example weights or fit gradients/residuals. The sequential corrective structure is the key distinction, not an assertion that all boosting methods share one loss.

10-M34 · Support vector machines ★★ M MCQ

For a linear separating hyperplane with parameters w,b and canonical hard-margin constraints, maximizing margin corresponds to:

  1. Making b exactly zero always
  2. Removing all support vectors
  3. Maximizing ||w|| without constraints
  4. Minimizing ||w||^2 subject to the constraints

Answer: D — Minimizing ||w||^2 subject to the constraints

The geometric margin is inversely related to ||w|| under the normalized constraints. Soft-margin SVMs allow violations through an additional penalty. Kernel choice changes the feature space.

10-M35 · K-means ★★★ E MCQ

A cluster contains points (0,0),(2,0),(1,3). What is its updated Euclidean k-means centroid?

  1. (0,0)
  2. (1,3)
  3. (1,1)
  4. (3,3)

Answer: C — (1,1)

The centroid is the coordinate-wise arithmetic mean: x=(0+2+1)/3=1, y=(0+0+3)/3=1. A medoid would have to be an observed point, unlike this centroid.

10-M36 · K-means limitations ★★ M MCQ

Standard Euclidean k-means minimizes:

  1. Always the global minimum regardless of initialization
  2. Within-cluster sum of squared distances to centroids
  3. The number of training labels
  4. The largest possible distance between every pair

Answer: B — Within-cluster sum of squared distances to centroids

Lloyd-style iterations alternate assignment and mean updates, converging to a local solution under standard conditions. Initialization and the chosen k affect the outcome.

10-M37 · Principal components ★★ M MCQ

The first principal component of centered data is a unit direction that:

  1. Uses the test labels to define a class boundary
  2. Always corresponds to the first original feature
  3. Minimizes all pairwise distances to zero
  4. Maximizes projected variance

Answer: D — Maximizes projected variance

PCA derives directions from the covariance structure. Centering and optional scaling affect the result. High variance does not automatically mean high predictive relevance.

10-M38 · Regularization ★★★ E MCQ

Compared with an unregularized fit, L2 regularization adds a penalty proportional to:

  1. The product of all errors
  2. The sum of squared parameter values
  3. The number of test labels
  4. Only the training sample order

Answer: B — The sum of squared parameter values

An L2 penalty discourages large weights and trades data fit against model complexity. Its strength is tuned without contaminating final test evaluation.

10-M39 · Regularization ★★ M MCQ

Which penalty is more directly associated with inducing exact zero coefficients in many standard convex fitting problems?

  1. Increasing every feature value equally
  2. A zero penalty always
  3. L1 penalty
  4. L2 penalty only

Answer: C — L1 penalty

The absolute-value penalty has a nonsmooth corner at zero and can produce sparse solutions. L2 usually shrinks coefficients continuously rather than setting many exactly to zero.

10-M40 · Bias and variance ★★★ M MCQ

Very low training error with substantially worse validation error most directly suggests:

  1. Overfitting or a train–validation mismatch that should be investigated
  2. Proof that more model complexity is needed
  3. Guaranteed underfitting only
  4. Proof that validation labels must be wrong

Answer: A — Overfitting or a train–validation mismatch that should be investigated

A large generalization gap can arise from excessive flexibility, small data or distribution/split problems. Diagnose rather than treating a single metric pattern as definitive causality.

10-M41 · Cross-validation ★★ M MCQ

In ordinary k-fold cross-validation on a fixed training pool, each fold is used for validation:

  1. In every run while also always training on it
  2. Never
  3. Once across the k runs
  4. Only if its score is highest

Answer: C — Once across the k runs

Each run trains on the other k-1 folds. Grouped or temporal data may require group/time-aware variants rather than random folds. A separate final test set can remain untouched.

10-M42 · Neural activations ★★★ E MCQ

ReLU applied to z=-3 gives:

  1. 1
  2. -3
  3. 3
  4. 0

Answer: D — 0

ReLU(z)=max(0,z). Its derivative away from the kink is 0 for negative inputs and 1 for positive inputs; the derivative at zero uses a convention in implementations.

10-M43 · Backpropagation ★★★ E MCQ

Backpropagation computes parameter derivatives chiefly by:

  1. Applying the chain rule through the computation graph
  2. Removing the loss function
  3. Searching all parameter values exhaustively
  4. Copying the labels to the outputs

Answer: A — Applying the chain rule through the computation graph

The chain rule combines local derivatives. An optimizer uses these gradients to update parameters; backpropagation itself is not identical to a particular optimizer.

10-M44 · Convolution dimensions ★★★ E MCQ

A 32×32 input uses a 3×3 convolution, stride1 and no padding. What is the output spatial size?

  1. 31×31
  2. 30×30
  3. 32×32
  4. 16×16

Answer: B — 30×30

Each dimension is floor((32-3+2*0)/1)+1=30. Output channels depend on the number of filters, a separate quantity.

10-M45 · Convolution parameters ★★ M MCQ

A convolution has eight 3×3 filters over three input channels, with one bias per output channel. How many trainable parameters?

  1. 216
  2. 224
  3. 27
  4. 72

Answer: B — 224

Each filter has 3*3*3=27 weights, plus one bias. Eight filters give 8*(27+1)=224. Spatial weight sharing means the input image width does not multiply this count.

10-M46 · Pooling ★★ M MCQ

A 2×2 max-pool with stride2 and no padding acts on a 30×30×8 tensor. What is its output shape?

  1. 30×30×8
  2. 15×15×4
  3. 28×28×8
  4. 15×15×8

Answer: D — 15×15×8

Pooling changes each spatial dimension to floor((30-2)/2)+1=15 and preserves channels. The operation has no learned weights in this standard form.

10-M47 · Intersection over union ★★★ M MCQ

Two continuous axis-aligned boxes each have area 4 and intersection area 1. What is IoU?

  1. 3/4
  2. 1/8
  3. 1/7
  4. 1/4

Answer: C — 1/7

Union=areaA+areaB-intersection=4+4-1=7. IoU=1/7. Do not divide only by one box’s area, which would measure a different overlap ratio.

10-M48 · Non-maximum suppression ★★ M MCQ

In conventional class-wise greedy non-maximum suppression, a high-score box is retained and then:

  1. All true-object identities become certain
  2. Every low-score box is increased to confidence1
  3. Lower-score boxes of the same class with IoU above a threshold are suppressed
  4. Every box of every class is removed regardless of overlap

Answer: C — Lower-score boxes of the same class with IoU above a threshold are suppressed

NMS is a postprocessing heuristic that reduces duplicate detections. Threshold and class-handling choices affect crowded-object behaviour; it is not a proof that suppressed boxes are false.

10-M49 · Transfer learning ★★ M MCQ

When adapting a pretrained vision model to a small labelled dataset, which is a legitimate strategy?

  1. Train a new task head and optionally fine-tune selected pretrained layers
  2. Select all hyperparameters on the final test set
  3. Duplicate test images into training to improve transfer
  4. Assume pretraining guarantees no bias

Answer: A — Train a new task head and optionally fine-tune selected pretrained layers

Transfer can reuse learned representations. Fine-tuning choices need validation and data provenance checks; benefit depends on task and domain compatibility.

10-M50 · Experimental reproducibility ★★★ E MCQ

Which record is most useful for reproducing a model evaluation?

  1. Only the researcher’s name
  2. Only the highest reported percentage
  3. Split identifiers, preprocessing, code/version, weights, settings, seeds and hardware/timing protocol
  4. Only a screenshot of the final graph

Answer: C — Split identifiers, preprocessing, code/version, weights, settings, seeds and hardware/timing protocol

Reproducibility requires the procedure and evaluation conditions, not merely the headline metric. Fixed seeds alone may not make every hardware/library operation deterministic.

10-W01 · Uniform-cost and A* traces ★★★ H WRITTEN

For the directed graph, run uniform-cost search from S to G with best-known-cost duplicate handling. Then run A* with h(S)=6,h(A)=4,h(B)=3,h(G)=0. List expansions and give the optimal path. Break ties alphabetically.

Worked answer

ExpandedUCS frontier after relaxation (cost)
SA:3, B:5
AB:4, G:10
BG:7
GStop: minimum cost7

The improved path to B replaces its cost 5 entry with cost 4. Parent pointers recover S→A→B→G, total 7. Stale queue entries are ignored.

ExpandedA* frontier after relaxation (g,h,f)
SA:(3,4,7), B:(5,3,8)
AB:(4,3,7), G:(10,0,10)
BG:(7,0,7)
GStop

This heuristic is admissible and consistent on these edges. Both methods expand S,A,B,G here; A* is not guaranteed to expand strictly fewer states in every example. Goal testing occurs on minimum-priority removal, not immediately when the first goal edge is generated.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

10-W02 · Minimax and alpha-beta drawing ★★★ M WRITTEN

Evaluate the tree using minimax. Search left-to-right with alpha-beta pruning and draw the evaluated tree, indicating any skipped leaf. MAX moves at the root; children are MIN.

Worked answer

Left MIN returns min(3,5)=3, so root alpha becomes 3. At the right MIN node, seeing its first leaf2 sets beta=2. Since beta<=alpha, the remaining leaf9 cannot raise that MIN value above3; it can be pruned. Root value=max(3,at most2)=3, selecting the left branch.

Without pruning all four leaves are inspected; with this ordering three are enough. The underlying true right-subtree value is 2, but the prune decision only needs the bound. A different visit order may reduce or eliminate the saving.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

10-W03 · Constraint-satisfaction trace ★★ M WRITTEN

Color a triangle A,B,C using colors {red,green,blue}, with adjacent vertices different. Draw one valid coloring and demonstrate forward checking after A=red and B=green. Explain why two colors cannot suffice.

Worked answer

Initially all three domains are {red,green,blue}. After A=red, both B and C lose red. Choose B=green; because B is adjacent to C, C loses green and is forced to blue. The assignment satisfies all three edges.

With only two colors, A uses one and B must use the other; C is adjacent to both, leaving no legal color. Forward checking removes values inconsistent with the most recent assignment; full arc consistency can propagate more generally. Domain restoration is required when backtracking.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

10-W04 · Bayesian-network diagram and inference ★★★ M WRITTEN

An alarm depends only on an equipment fault F. P(F)=0.1, P(alarm|F)=0.8, P(alarm|not F)=0.2. Draw the network and conditional tables, then compute P(alarm) and P(F|alarm).

Worked answer

F statePriorP(alarm|state)P(no alarm|state)
Fault0.10.80.2
No fault0.90.20.8

P(alarm)=P(F)P(alarm|F)+P(not F)P(alarm|not F)=0.08+0.18=0.26. P(F|alarm)=P(F and alarm)/P(alarm)=0.08/0.26=4/13≈0.3077.

Most alarms in this model occur without a fault: 0.18 exceeds0.08. This does not contradict sensitivity0.8, because base rates and false alarms enter the reverse conditional probability. The model assumes the supplied rates apply to the population being evaluated.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

10-W05 · Confusion matrix and metrics ★★★ M WRITTEN

Use the confusion matrix. State the positive class, calculate accuracy, precision, recall, specificity and F1, and explain why each denominator differs.

Actual / predictedPositiveNegative
Positive305
Negative1055

Worked answer

Positive means the class labelled Positive in the table. TP=30,FN=5,FP=10,TN=55, total 100.

MetricCalculationResult
Accuracy(30+55)/1000.85
Precision30/(30+10)0.75
Recall30/(30+5)6/7≈0.8571
Specificity55/(55+10)11/13≈0.8462
F12*30/(2*30+10+5)0.80

Precision conditions on predicted positives; recall on actual positives; specificity on actual negatives. Accuracy pools both correct classes; F1 combines positive-class precision and recall. Zero denominators require an explicitly stated convention in other datasets, but none occurs here.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

10-W06 · Linear-regression gradient step ★★★ M WRITTEN

For y_hat=wx with no bias, use data (x,y)={(1,2),(2,4)} and loss L=(1/(2m))Σ(wx-y)^2. Start w=0 and take one batch gradient-descent step with learning rate 0.1. Show loss and gradient before the step and loss afterward.

Worked answer

m=2. At w=0, residuals are -2,-4. Loss=(4+16)/4=5. The gradient is (1/m)Σ(wx-y)x=[(-2)*1+(-4)*2]/2=-5.

Update w_new=0-0.1*(-5)=0.5. New predictions are 0.5,1; residuals are-1.5,-3. New loss=(2.25+9)/4=2.8125.

The loss decreased, but the optimum w=2 has not been reached. The factor1/(2m) is part of the defined loss and cancels the derivative’s2. A different loss normalization requires a corresponding gradient; blindly copying a formula can introduce a factor-of-two error.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

10-W07 · Logistic-regression gradient ★★ H WRITTEN

For one example x=2,y=1, use p=sigmoid(wx+b) and binary cross-entropy. At w=0,b=0, compute p, gradients and one update with learning rate 0.1. Use natural logs.

Worked answer

Score z=0 gives p=0.5. For sigmoid plus cross-entropy, dL/dz=p-y=-0.5. Thus dL/dw=(p-y)x=-1 and dL/db=p-y=-0.5.

w_new=0-0.1*(-1)=0.1; b_new=0-0.1*(-0.5)=0.05. The updated score is 0.1*2+0.05=0.25 and p_new=1/(1+exp(-0.25))≈0.5622.

Original loss=-ln0.5≈0.6931; new loss≈0.5759. The prediction moves toward the positive label. This derivation assumes the stated one-example loss without regularization; adding a penalty changes the weight gradient.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

10-W08 · Decision-tree entropy and split ★★★ M WRITTEN

A node has eight examples: four positive and four negative. Candidate split A makes two children, each containing two positives and two negatives. Candidate split B makes two children: four positives only and four negatives only. Compute information gains and draw the chosen one-level tree.

Worked answer

Parent entropy H=-0.5log2(0.5)-0.5log2(0.5)=1 bit. Under A both children have entropy 1, so weighted entropy=(4/8)*1+(4/8)*1=1 and gain 0.

Under B both children are pure, with entropy 0. Weighted entropy 0 gives gain 1 bit. Information gain prefers split B for these training counts.

Perfect purity on this small training sample does not establish generalization. Tree depth, sample size, validation and pruning/regularization remain relevant. An empty-probability entropy term is treated as0 by continuity, not evaluated as log(0) alone.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

10-W09 · K-means assignment and update ★★★ M WRITTEN

For points A=(0,0),B=(0,2),C=(6,0),D=(6,2), initialize two centroids μ1=(0,0), μ2=(6,0). Use Euclidean distance. Show first assignments, updated centroids and squared-error objective after the update. Draw the resulting clusters.

Worked answer

PointAssigned clusterUpdated centroidSquared distance
A(0,0)1(0,1)1
B(0,2)1(0,1)1
C(6,0)2(6,1)1
D(6,2)2(6,1)1

No distance ties occur. Mean of A,B is(0,1); mean of C,D is(6,1). Updated within-cluster squared error=1+1+1+1=4. Reassignment with these means is unchanged, so the algorithm has stabilized for this initialization.

The standard k-means objective is a sum of squared distances, not the sum of distances. Different initial centroids and datasets can lead to different local solutions.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

10-W10 · Principal components ★★ M WRITTEN

Centered 2D data have covariance matrix [[4,0],[0,1]]. Find the first principal direction and its explained-variance ratio. Draw the two coordinate directions and explain what discarding the second component means.

Worked answer

The diagonal covariance has eigenvalues4 and 1 with eigenvectors(1,0) and(0,1). The first principal direction is the x-axis, up to an arbitrary sign. Explained variance=4/(4+1)=0.8, or 80%.

Projection onto the first component retains the x coordinate of centered observations and discards their y coordinate. Reconstruction from only PC1 sets centered y to zero; the lost variance is 1 of the total 5.

This maximizes retained variance among one-dimensional orthogonal projections, not necessarily predictive accuracy for any labels. Scaling before PCA can change the covariance matrix and chosen components.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

10-W11 · CNN shape and parameter diagram ★★★ H WRITTEN

Draw the shape pipeline for input 32×32×3 → convolution(8 filters,3×3,stride1,no padding,bias) → ReLU → max-pool(2×2,stride2) → flatten → dense(4 outputs,bias). Compute output shapes and total trainable parameters.

Worked answer

LayerOutputParameters
Conv30×30×88*(3*3*3+1)=224
ReLU30×30×80
Max-pool15×15×80
Flatten18000
Dense41800*4+4=7204

Total trainable parameters=224+7204=7428. Convolution reuses each filter over positions, so parameter count is not multiplied by30*30. Flatten changes shape but does not learn weights. The final four outputs can be logits; choosing a classification loss/softmax is a separate specification.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

10-W12 · Intersection over union drawing ★★★ M WRITTEN

Box A spans (0,0) to (2,2), and box B spans (1,1) to (3,3) in continuous coordinates. Draw both boxes, mark their overlap, and calculate IoU. Do not use inclusive integer-pixel +1 corrections.

Worked answer

Each box has width 2,height 2,area 4. Intersection width=min(2,3)-max(0,1)=1, similarly height 1, so intersection area 1. Union=4+4-1=7 and IoU=1/7≈0.1429.

For general rectangles clamp each overlap dimension at zero when no overlap exists. The coordinate convention matters: these are continuous boundaries, so no +1 is added. IoU measures geometric overlap; a detection metric also needs class matching and evaluation rules.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

10-W13 · Neural-network chain rule ★★ H WRITTEN

A scalar network has h=ReLU(w1*x), y_hat=w2*h, and L=0.5*(y_hat-y)^2. At x=2,y=1,w1=1,w2=2, draw its computation graph and calculate dL/dw1,dL/dw2.

Worked answer

Forward: z=w1*x=2,h=2,y_hat=4,error=3,L=4.5. Backward: dL/dy_hat=3. Therefore dL/dw2=3*h=6.

dL/dh=3*w2=6. Since z=2>0, dReLU/dz=1. Then dL/dw1=6*1*x=12. The gradient also depends on w2; forgetting this path factor would give the wrong result.

The derivative at the ReLU kink is irrelevant here. An optimizer would subtract a learning-rate-scaled gradient, but this question asks only for derivatives.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

10-W14 · Group-aware validation design ★★★ M WRITTEN

A vision dataset contains consecutive frames from 20 recording sessions, with two synchronized camera views per event. Propose a split and evaluation protocol that avoids obvious leakage. Explain why a high random-frame-split score might mislead.

Worked answer

Assign complete sessions, or a stricter grouping of related events/sites when needed, to training, validation and test before generating crops or augmentations. Keep both views and near-duplicate frames of an event together. For example use 12 sessions for training,4 validation,4 final test, while checking class coverage; these counts are illustrative, not universally optimal.

Fit preprocessing statistics on training only; select model settings and thresholds using validation only. Lock them before final testing. Report the metric definitions, class-wise results, uncertainty or per-session variation, difficult failures and the evaluated hardware/timing pipeline.

A random frame split may put nearly identical scenes and vehicles in training and test, measuring recognition of familiar conditions rather than new-session generalization. Session separation still may not represent a new city, camera or season; external-domain evaluation is a further question.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

10-W15 · Regularization and model selection ★★ M WRITTEN

A flexible model gets 98% training accuracy and 72% validation accuracy. A smaller model gets 90% and 85%. Explain a defensible next step, two possible causes of the gap, and why the final test set should not choose between repeated trials.

Worked answer

Prefer the smaller model provisionally under this validation comparison, but inspect sample sizes, class-wise errors, split quality and uncertainty before treating one measured score as decisive. The validation gap between models is 13 percentage points; the flexible model’s train–validation gap is 26 points.

Possible causes include fitting noise/idiosyncrasies due to excessive flexibility, a small training sample, or a distribution mismatch between splits. Test reasonable regularization strengths, better data coverage or simpler architectures using training/validation or suitable cross-validation.

Repeatedly selecting by final test performance adapts choices to that test set. Keep it untouched until the selection protocol is complete, then report its outcome without concealing unsuccessful trials. Training accuracy alone is not a sufficient selection rule.

Practice self-check (10 points): Method and setup 3; working 5; result and interpretation 2.

Source library and limits

The ten subject headings and format come from R1. R2 is a presentation example only. Questions, solutions, figures, stars and practice rubrics are original. References are study resources, not claims that these questions were set by their authors.

R1 · BUET CSE — April 2026 postgraduate admission notice

Open source

Source for the ten Group 1 headings and the announced examination format, not for the original questions or star ratings.

R2 · University of Dhaka — MSc CSE Admission Test 2021, user-supplied scan

Presentation reference only: weighted graphs, automata, scheduling/resource tables and code. No questions or answer keys are reproduced from this scan.

R3 · Harvard CS50 — Memory notes

Open source

Pointers, arrays and memory reasoning. Use the course’s linked C notes for prerequisites.

R4 · C11 committee draft N1570; C++ working draft

Open source

Primary language specifications. C++ draft: https://eel.is/c++draft/ . The bank states C11/C++17 assumptions; a current draft also contains later features not used here.

R5 · Oracle — Java Language Specification, Chapter 15

Open source

Expression and method-invocation semantics; consult linked chapters for classes and exceptions.

R6 · MIT — Mathematics for Computer Science

Open source

Logic, relations, induction, counting, graphs and probability.

R7 · MIT — Introduction to Algorithms

Open source

Algorithms, data structures, analysis and university problem sets.

Open source

Scanning, parsing, semantics, intermediate code and optimization.

Open source

Automata, grammars, decidability and language classes.

Open source

Relational design, SQL, transactions, indexing and database systems.

Open source
